# Election Commission — Live Session Edition

Most people meet the Election Commission of India only as a logo on a voter slip. That is the wrong starting point, and it is why answers on this topic go wrong. The Commission is not a government department that happens to run polls. It is a constitutional office created because the framers decided that one particular function — deciding *who may vote, who may stand, and on what terms the contest is held* — could not safely be left with the government that stands to win or lose by it.

Everything you will learn here follows from that single design decision. Why a separate article in a separate Part. Why the Chief Election Commissioner is removed like a Supreme Court judge while his two colleagues are not. Why Parliament, not the Constitution, fixes the six-year term. Why a non-statutory Model Code can stop a Chief Minister's announcement in forty-eight hours while a proven corrupt practice takes six years to bite. Why the Commission may cancel a poll but may never declare a returned candidate's election void.

This session teaches the topic in dependency order, not in textbook order. We begin with the constitutional problem, build the institution, then put the election machine around it, then test the doctrine against its hardest cases.

A word on how the practice works. Each lesson ends with one concept check that you answer in your head before reading the model answer, and one original Mains question with a complete model solution and a scoring note. The verified previous-year questions are collected at the end as an index, displayed exactly as printed and deliberately left unanswered, because an election-law question you have already seen solved teaches you nothing on exam day.

## Lesson 1: The Constitutional Problem, and the Exact Field of Article 324(1)

Progress: 1 / 20 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Why an independent election manager exists, and what it controls

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — Laxmikanth Chapter 42 read alongside the constitutional text
CA search: "Election Commission of India institutional role, 2026"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson; the file's single current anchor is placed in Lesson 7
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: the chain that produces an accepted result

```text
                    EQUAL CITIZENSHIP
                           |
            +--------------+--------------+
            |                             |
     accurate roll                 lawful candidature
            |                             |
            +--------------+--------------+
                           |
                 neutral campaign field
                           |
                      secure poll
                           |
                   verifiable count
                           |
                  lawful declaration
                           |
            ACCEPTED REPRESENTATIVE AUTHORITY

  Break any one link  ->  the result is contested, not accepted
```

Read the chain downward once. Each box is a separate opportunity for a government to win an election without winning the argument. Leave out a community from the roll and the count never mattered. Reject a nomination on a pretext and the campaign never mattered. Use state vehicles and state advertising and the poll never mattered. This is why the problem cannot be solved by honest counting alone.

### 1. Start with the objection, because it is a good one

A learner reasonably asks: India already has courts, a free press and a Parliament. Why add a separate constitutional authority for elections?

The answer is about *timing*. Courts work after the event; an election is a short, irreversible event. A roll error discovered in June cannot be cured by a judgment in December, because the House has already been constituted. A biased transfer of a district officer three days before the poll cannot be undone by a writ six months later. The framers needed an authority that could act **inside** the election window, with powers that bite in days rather than years.

The second reason is about *neutrality of the operator*. Elections need lakhs of staff, vehicles, police and buildings — almost all belonging to the Union and the States. If the government also directed them during the poll, the machinery of the State would be campaigning for one of the contestants. So the Constitution does not create a parallel civil service; it transfers *command over the existing one, for election purposes only*, to an authority outside ordinary executive control.

Hold that formula, because it explains a great deal later:

> The Election Commission is thin in permanent staff and enormous in temporary command.

### 2. Article 324(1), read slowly

The operative clause is short and should be learnt in its own words:

> The superintendence, direction and control of the preparation of the electoral rolls for, and the conduct of, all elections to Parliament and to the Legislature of every State and of elections to the offices of President and Vice-President held under this Constitution shall be vested in a Commission (referred to in this Constitution as the Election Commission).

Four separate things are vested, and candidates routinely lose a Prelims mark by merging them.

| Limb of Article 324(1) | What it covers | Why it is listed separately |
|---|---|---|
| Preparation of electoral rolls | Rolls for Parliament and every State Legislature | The roll is made long before any poll is announced |
| Conduct of those elections | The whole operational process | Supervision of the event itself |
| Elections to the office of President | A separate electoral college poll | Not an "election to Parliament" at all |
| Elections to the office of Vice-President | A separate electoral college poll | Same reason |

Note what the clause does **not** say. It does not say "all elections in India". It names the institutions. That naming is the whole boundary.

### 3. Three words that do different work

"Superintendence, direction and control" is not a flourish. Each word has been used by the Supreme Court to support a different kind of power.

```text
SUPERINTENDENCE  ->  oversight of a process run by others
                     (watching the machinery, demanding reports)

DIRECTION        ->  positive instructions that bind field officers
                     (how scrutiny is done, what an observer must check)

CONTROL          ->  power to correct, substitute or stop
                     (transfer an officer, order a fresh poll)
```

A useful discipline for Mains: when you write "the ECI has plenary powers", immediately say *which* of the three words you are relying on. Examiners reward the specificity, and it prevents the common overclaim we will dismantle in Lesson 16.

### 4. The boundary that is tested every few years

Article 324 covers Parliament, State Legislatures, President and Vice-President. It does **not** cover local government. Panchayat elections belong to the State Election Commission under **Article 243K**; municipal elections belong to the State Election Commission under **Article 243ZA**.

| | Election Commission of India | State Election Commission |
|---|---|---|
| Constitutional source | Article 324 | Articles 243K and 243ZA |
| Field | Parliament, State Legislatures, President, Vice-President | Panchayats and municipalities |
| Head | Chief Election Commissioner, plus other Election Commissioners as fixed | State Election Commissioner |
| Appointed by | President | Governor |
| Removal | CEC like a Supreme Court judge; others on the CEC's recommendation | Like a Judge of a High Court |
| Operating law | Constitution, RPA 1950 and RPA 1951, election rules | State local-government election laws |

The deeper point is not the table but the *reason* for it. Local self-government was constitutionalised in 1992–93, four decades after Part XV. The framers of Parts IX and IX-A copied the independence design — a separate commission, judge-like removal — but deliberately placed it at the State level, because panchayat and municipal elections are State subjects administered through State machinery. Sharing staff or holding polls on the same day does not merge the two authorities.

Two further exclusions are worth carrying: cooperative-society elections and a political party's own internal organisational elections are not Article 324 elections either. The Commission may require a registered party to hold internal elections as a condition of its own regulatory scheme, but it does not *conduct* them.

### 5. What the Commission is, in one sentence you can defend

It is a **permanent, independent, all-India constitutional body** that does not choose the winner but guarantees the conditions under which electors choose.

Unpack each adjective, because each is separately examinable:

- **Permanent** — it does not dissolve between elections; rolls are a continuous function.
- **Independent** — insulated by removal protections and by the bar on varying the CEC's service conditions to his disadvantage, not by being outside law.
- **All-India** — one body common to the Union and the States, which is why a State government cannot create its own commission for Assembly polls.
- **Constitutional** — created by the Constitution itself, so Parliament may regulate its working but cannot abolish it by ordinary law.

### 6. Three kinds of power, and why the classification is worth having

Across this file you will meet the Commission doing a startling range of things: preparing a roll, fixing a poll date, transferring a district officer, deciding which faction owns a party symbol, telling the President whether a sitting member has incurred a disqualification. A learner reasonably asks whether there is any order in that list, or whether the Commission simply does whatever an election seems to require.

There is an order. The standard organising classification sorts everything the Commission does into three kinds of power, and the sorting principle is a single question: **who finally decides?**

```text
                 POWERS AND FUNCTIONS OF THE COMMISSION
                                  |
     +----------------------------+----------------------------+
     |                            |                            |
  ADMINISTRATIVE               ADVISORY               QUASI-JUDICIAL
  it acts itself          it gives an opinion         it decides a
                          to another authority        dispute between
                                                      contesting parties
     |                            |                            |
  rolls, schedule,         opinion to the            recognition and
  machinery, code of       President or the          symbol disputes,
  conduct, symbols,        Governor on a             decided after notice
  repoll, countermand      member's disqualification and hearing
     |                            |                            |
  the act takes effect     the other authority       a reasoned order that
  on its own force         passes the order; the     binds the parties and
                           Commission does not       is open to judicial
                                                     review
```

Read the bottom row first, because that is where the difference bites. In the administrative column the Commission's own signature ends the matter. In the advisory column it does not: somebody else signs, and the Commission's opinion is an input — a decisive one in the two cases we reach in Lesson 18, but still an input. In the quasi-judicial column the Commission behaves like a tribunal: there are opposed parties, there must be notice and a hearing, and there must be reasons.

A standard enumeration of **fifteen** functions, sorted into those three columns, looks like this.

| Kind of power | Functions in the standard enumeration |
|---|---|
| Administrative | determining the territorial areas of constituencies on the basis of Parliament's Delimitation Commission legislation; preparing and periodically revising electoral rolls and registering eligible voters; notifying dates and schedules and overseeing the scrutiny of nominations; granting recognition to political parties and allotting symbols; appointing officers to inquire into disputes about electoral arrangements; determining the code of conduct to be observed by parties and candidates; preparing a roster for publicity of party policies on radio and television; cancelling polls where there is rigging, booth capturing or violence; requesting the President or a Governor for the staff needed to conduct elections; supervising the election machinery throughout the country; and registering parties and conferring national or State status on the basis of poll performance |
| Advisory | advising the President on the disqualification of members of Parliament; advising a Governor on the disqualification of members of a State Legislature; and certifying whether elections to a State Assembly can be held, which conditions the continuance of President's rule beyond one year |
| Quasi-judicial | acting as a court to settle disputes about the grant of recognition to parties and the allotment of symbols |

Two honest cautions about that standard list, because reproducing it uncritically is how a confident answer becomes a wrong one.

1. **"Determining the territorial areas of constituencies" is loose.** The Commission does not draw constituency boundaries at will. A separate statutory Delimitation Commission does that, and the Election Commission's continuing job is to keep the resulting order up to date. Lesson 17 is built on exactly that distinction.
2. **"Scrutinising nomination papers" is also loose.** The officer who scrutinises and accepts or rejects a nomination is the **Returning Officer**, acting under the Representation of the People Act, 1951. The Commission superintends, directs and controls that process; it does not perform it. Lesson 13 shows why the difference matters.

The third advisory function is the one almost everybody forgets, so learn it in its own terms. **Article 356(5)** provides that a resolution continuing President's rule in a State beyond one year cannot be passed unless both a Proclamation of Emergency is in operation and **the Election Commission certifies** that continuance is necessary because of difficulties in holding general elections to that State's Legislative Assembly. That certificate is not a power to govern a State; it is the Commission's professional statement about whether an election is feasible, placed as a lock on an emergency power.

Now the limit of the classification itself, because a map is not a source of authority:

- The three labels describe **the form** in which power is exercised. They do not **create** power. Every one of these functions must still trace to Article 324 or to a statute, which is the whole argument of Lesson 16.
- The same subject matter can move between columns. Symbols are allotted administratively at every election and adjudicated quasi-judicially when a recognised party splits. Disqualification is advised upon under Articles 103 and 192, but declared by the Commission itself in one narrow statutory situation we meet in Lesson 13.
- The columns are unequal in size. The Commission is overwhelmingly an administrator; its advisory and quasi-judicial roles are narrow and specific. An answer that inflates the last two columns misdescribes the institution.

**Mnemonic: run, advise, decide.** The Commission *runs* the election, *advises* on a member's disqualification, and *decides* party-and-symbol disputes.

### 7. The strongest objection, and the honest reply

**Objection.** If the Commission depends on the Union executive for appointment, budget and almost all its field staff, calling it "independent" is decorative.

**Reply.** The claim is serious and partly correct, and a good answer concedes the correct part. Independence here is not self-sufficiency; it is *legal insulation at the points where pressure is most effective*. The Constitution does three things: it puts the CEC's removal beyond executive will, it forbids worsening his service conditions after appointment, and it obliges the President and Governors to supply staff **when so requested by the Commission** under Article 324(6) — making the supply a duty rather than a favour. Operational autonomy is then produced by convention, transfer powers during the election period, and the Commission's own instructions.

**Residual.** What the Constitution does *not* secure is the appointment stage, a guaranteed secretariat, or financial autonomy of the kind Article 322 gives the Union Public Service Commission. That residual is the live constitutional argument we reach in Lesson 7. Say the residual aloud in your answer; do not pretend it is settled.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: "Statutory, regulatory and various quasi-judicial bodies"; "Appointment to various Constitutional posts, powers, functions and responsibilities of various Constitutional Bodies".

**Where this lesson is tested.** The inclusion–exclusion boundary is the most frequently examined idea in the entire topic, usually as a close-option Prelims trap or as the opening frame of a GS-II answer.

**Probable framings.** "The Election Commission of India is a constitutional trustee rather than an administrator. Examine." Or a statement-based objective question pairing an Article 324 institution with a local-body institution.

**Traps.**

- Writing that the ECI conducts "all elections in India".
- Writing that the ECI conducts elections to the office of the President *of* Parliament — the Presidential election is a separate limb, not a parliamentary election.
- Treating Article 324(6) as a courtesy rather than an obligation triggered by a request.
- Treating the Commission's advisory and quasi-judicial roles as equal in weight to its administrative role, or assuming the three-fold classification is itself a source of power.

**Answer use.** Open with the four-limb field, then the two excluded fields with their articles, and only then move to powers. An answer that establishes the boundary in two lines buys credibility for everything after it.

### Revision notes

1. Article 324(1) vests **superintendence, direction and control** in the Election Commission.
2. Four limbs: rolls for Parliament and State Legislatures; conduct of those elections; President's election; Vice-President's election.
3. The Commission is permanent, independent, all-India and constitutional.
4. Local-body elections belong to State Election Commissions under **Articles 243K and 243ZA**.
5. Cooperative-society and internal party organisational elections are outside Article 324.
6. Article 324(6): the President or Governor **shall**, when so requested, make staff available.
7. Independence is legal insulation, not administrative self-sufficiency.
8. The Commission protects the conditions of choice; it does not choose.
9. The unresolved residual is appointment, secretariat and finance — not the conduct of the poll.
10. Always name which of the three words — superintendence, direction, control — your claim rests on.
11. Powers and functions sort into **administrative** (the Commission acts), **advisory** (it gives an opinion and another authority decides) and **quasi-judicial** (it decides a dispute after notice and hearing). Mnemonic: run, advise, decide.
12. **Article 356(5)**: continuance of President's rule beyond one year requires both a Proclamation of Emergency in operation and the **Election Commission's certificate** that difficulties prevent holding Assembly elections.

### Concept check

**Question:** A State government holds its Assembly election and its municipal elections on the same day, uses the same district staff, and publishes one combined schedule. A commentator concludes that the Election Commission of India therefore "conducted" the municipal poll. Why is that conclusion wrong, and what exactly did the ECI control that day?

**Model answer:** Shared logistics do not transfer constitutional authority. The Assembly poll falls within Article 324(1) and was superintended, directed and controlled by the ECI; the municipal poll falls within Article 243ZA and was conducted by the State Election Commission, which issued its own schedule and ran its own process. The same collector may wear two hats on the same day under two different authorities. The ECI controlled the Assembly roll, the Assembly timetable, the candidature process and the Assembly poll and count — nothing in the municipal stream.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating common personnel or a common polling date as proof of common jurisdiction. Constitutional ownership follows the institution being elected, not the officer who happens to execute the work.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** "The Election Commission of India is a thin institution with enormous temporary command." Examine this description of its constitutional design. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (150 words):**
The description is accurate and constitutionally deliberate. Article 324(1) vests superintendence, direction and control of rolls and of elections to Parliament, State Legislatures and the offices of President and Vice-President in the Commission, but creates no parallel civil service for it. Instead, Article 324(6) obliges the President and Governors, when so requested, to make staff available, converting the ordinary administration into election machinery for a bounded period. The design has two merits: it avoids a costly permanent election bureaucracy, and it denies the incumbent government command over the officers who run its own election. Its cost is dependence — on State cadres, on Union budgeting and on convention for neutrality, which is why transfer powers, observers and written instructions matter so much in practice. The Commission is therefore best described as a constitutional trustee of electoral conditions, strong where the law insulates it and vulnerable where the law is silent.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** Full credit requires the structural pairing — minimal permanent apparatus set against Article 324(6) command — plus one named vulnerability. Answers that only list powers and functions cap at half marks because they never address the word "thin".

### Why the next lesson follows

We now know what the Commission controls. We do not yet know the constitutional environment around it: who may be a voter at all, which legislature writes election law, and what happens when someone wants to drag an ongoing election into court. Those are Articles 325 to 329, and they are the subject of Lesson 2.
