## Lesson 2: Part XV End to End — Articles 325 to 329, and the Ownership Map

Progress: 2 / 20 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: The six-article spine, Article 329A's life and death, and the boundary articles

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — constitutional text of Part XV verified against published renderings
CA search: "Part XV Constitution elections Articles 325 to 329, 2026 developments"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: the six-article spine, and what each article is protecting

```text
PART XV — ELECTIONS

324  WHO SUPERVISES ........... the Commission
      |
325  WHO MAY BE ON THE ROLL ... one general roll; no exclusion only on
      |                          religion, race, caste or sex
326  WHO MAY VOTE ............. adult suffrage, 18 years, subject to
      |                          stated disqualifications
327  WHO WRITES THE LAW ....... Parliament, for either House and for
      |                          State Legislatures
328  WHO ELSE MAY WRITE IT .... State Legislature, for its own House,
      |                          but subject to Parliament's law
329  WHO MAY INTERRUPT ........ courts may not, except by election
                                 petition after the result

  Supervision -> entitlement -> franchise -> legislative power -> judicial boundary
```

Read the rail downward. It moves from *who runs it*, to *who is counted*, to *who may vote*, to *who makes rules*, to *who may litigate and when*. Nothing in Part XV is decorative; each article closes a gap that could otherwise be used to capture an election.

### 1. Article 325: a negative guarantee, often read too widely

Article 325 says that there shall be **one general electoral roll for every territorial constituency**, and that no person shall be ineligible for inclusion in it, or claim to be included in any **special** electoral roll, **on grounds only of religion, race, caste, sex or any of them**.

Two halves, and both matter.

- The first half abolishes communal electorates. Before independence, separate electorates divided voters by community; Article 325 forecloses their return by requiring a single roll per constituency.
- The second half is the limiting phrase: **"on grounds only of"**. It bars exclusion *solely* on those grounds. It does not abolish lawful conditions of entitlement.

That limiting phrase is the examiner's favourite. Age, citizenship and ordinary residence remain conditions of registration, and a statutory disqualification — such as unsoundness of mind declared by a competent court — still operates. None of these is a "ground only of religion, race, caste or sex".

```text
Article 325 FORBIDS                    Article 325 DOES NOT TOUCH
---------------------------------      ---------------------------------
a separate roll for a community        the citizenship requirement
exclusion because of caste             the minimum-age requirement
exclusion because of sex               the ordinary-residence requirement
a claim to a special communal roll     statutory disqualifications
```

### 2. Article 326: the franchise, with its conditions stated

Article 326 makes elections to the House of the People and to the Legislative Assembly of every State elections **on the basis of adult suffrage**: every person who is a citizen of India, who is not less than eighteen years of age on the date fixed by or under law, and who is not otherwise disqualified under the Constitution or any law on the ground of **non-residence, unsoundness of mind, crime, or corrupt or illegal practice**, is entitled to be registered as a voter.

Three disciplines follow.

1. The voting age was **lowered from twenty-one to eighteen by the Constitution (Sixty-first Amendment) Act, 1988**. Do not write that it was eighteen from 1950.
2. Article 326 covers the **Lok Sabha and the State Legislative Assemblies**. The Rajya Sabha and the State Legislative Councils are indirectly or partly indirectly constituted and are not adult-suffrage elections.
3. Adult suffrage is not unconditional. The article itself lists the four categories of permissible disqualification. An answer that calls the franchise "universal and unqualified" has misread the text.

### 3. Articles 327 and 328: who writes election law

| Article | Who legislates | On what | Limit |
|---|---|---|---|
| 327 | Parliament | Elections to either House of Parliament **and** to the House or either House of a State Legislature — including rolls, delimitation of constituencies and all matters necessary to secure the due constitution of those bodies | "Subject to the provisions of this Constitution" |
| 328 | State Legislature | Elections to that State's Legislature, including rolls and all necessary matters | Operates only **in so far as provision in that behalf is not made by Parliament** |

This is an occupied-field relationship, and it explains why the operative election statutes are central. Parliament used Article 327 to enact the **Representation of the People Act, 1950** and the **Representation of the People Act, 1951**. Because Parliament has occupied the field comprehensively, Article 328 has little practical room.

The analytical point for Mains: Article 324 gives constitutional supervision, Article 327 gives statutory specification. They are complementary, and the relationship between them is exactly what the plenary-power cases in Lesson 16 are about. Supervision without statute would be arbitrary; statute without supervision would be unenforceable inside a three-week poll window.

### 4. Article 329: the judicial boundary, in two distinct clauses

Article 329 is headed "Bar to interference by courts in electoral matters" and has two limbs that are regularly confused.

```text
ARTICLE 329(a)
  validity of a LAW relating to delimitation of constituencies,
  or allotment of seats, made under Article 327 or 328
        -> shall not be called in question in any court

ARTICLE 329(b)
  an ELECTION to either House of Parliament or to the House or
  either House of a State Legislature
        -> shall not be called in question except by an
           ELECTION PETITION presented to such authority and in
           such manner as provided by or under a law made by the
           appropriate Legislature
```

Clause (a) protects the *map*. Clause (b) protects the *process and its outcome*, and channels every challenge into a single statutory route. Mixing them up produces a confident, wrong sentence such as "Article 329(a) bars election petitions".

Two qualifications keep the answer honest:

- Article 329(b) is a bar on *calling an election in question*, not a general abolition of judicial review. The Supreme Court's later doctrine permits intervention that **facilitates** rather than **retards** the electoral process.
- The bar does not extend to the Presidential and Vice-Presidential elections. Those disputes go to the **Supreme Court under Article 71**, not to a High Court election petition.

### 5. Article 329A: the article that was inserted and then removed

This is the part most summaries omit, and it is the most quotable fact in the topic.

```text
1975  Constitution (Thirty-ninth Amendment) Act inserts Article 329A
        -> placed disputes about the election of the Prime Minister
           and the Speaker beyond the ordinary judicial route
      |
1975  Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain
        -> the relevant clause struck down; free and fair elections
           and judicial review held part of the basic structure
      |
1978  Constitution (Forty-fourth Amendment) Act omits Article 329A
        -> the text itself is removed from the Constitution
```

Why it matters for this topic: it shows that the election-dispute route in Article 329(b) is not merely a procedural convenience. An attempt to immunise one office's election from adjudication was treated as a basic-structure violation. When you write that free and fair elections are part of the basic structure, this is the authority — not a general sentiment.

### 6. The ownership map: four boundaries you must be able to state

Half the errors in this topic are ownership errors — attributing to the Election Commission something that belongs to another decision-maker.

| Question | Decision-maker | Not |
|---|---|---|
| Who conducts panchayat and municipal elections? | State Election Commission, Articles 243K and 243ZA | The ECI |
| Who decides a **sitting** member's disqualification under Article 102 or 191? | The President or Governor, who **shall obtain the opinion of the Election Commission and act according to that opinion** (Articles 103 and 192) | The ECI acting alone |
| Who decides disqualification on the ground of defection? | The Speaker or Chairman under the Tenth Schedule, subject to judicial review | The ECI |
| Who decides whether a returned candidate's election is void? | The High Court on an election petition, with appeal to the Supreme Court | The ECI |

Note the precise phrasing in row two. Under Articles 103 and 192 the Commission's opinion is **binding on the President or Governor**, yet the Commission is not the deciding authority on the face of the Constitution. That is an unusual arrangement and it is worth one careful sentence in a Mains answer: the ECI supplies a determinative opinion within a decision that is formally made elsewhere.

### 7. Comparison treated as an argument, not a table

Why did the framers give the ECI an *opinion* role in Article 103 rather than a decision role? Because a sitting member's disqualification is a question about the composition of a House, and the Constitution generally routes such questions through the constitutional head of the polity. Making the opinion binding then prevents the head from acting on political advice. The design buys neutrality without making the election manager a tribunal over legislators.

Contrast the Tenth Schedule. Defection is about party discipline inside the House, so it was given to the presiding officer — a choice that has been heavily criticised precisely because the presiding officer is usually a partisan figure. Set the two side by side in an answer and you have a genuine comparative argument rather than two memorised rules.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: "Indian Constitution — significant provisions"; "Appointment to various Constitutional posts, powers, functions and responsibilities of various Constitutional Bodies".

**Where this lesson is tested.** Article 329(b) and the election-petition route form the backbone of the 2022 GS-II dispute question in the index. Article 325's "grounds only" phrase and the Article 326 amendment history are classic objective material.

**Probable framings.** "Article 329(b) trades access during the poll for a guaranteed remedy after it. Discuss." Or a statement question pairing Article 325 with residence conditions.

**Traps.**

- Saying Article 325 abolishes all conditions for enrolment.
- Saying Article 326 fixed eighteen years in 1950.
- Attributing the delimitation-law bar to Article 329(b) instead of 329(a).
- Forgetting that Article 329A was **omitted** in 1978 and writing about it in the present tense.
- Sending a Presidential-election dispute to a High Court.

**Answer use.** In any question on election disputes, the opening move is Article 329(b) plus the forum, not a list of RPA sections. Sections come second, as the implementation of the constitutional channel.

### Revision notes

1. Part XV runs from Article 324 to Article 329.
2. Article 325: one general roll; no exclusion or special roll **on grounds only of** religion, race, caste or sex.
3. Article 325 leaves citizenship, age, residence and statutory disqualifications intact.
4. Article 326: adult suffrage for the Lok Sabha and State Legislative Assemblies, age eighteen.
5. Age lowered from twenty-one by the **Sixty-first Amendment, 1988**.
6. Article 327: Parliament legislates on elections to Parliament and to State Legislatures.
7. Article 328: State Legislature legislates only where Parliament has not.
8. Article 329(a): no court may question the validity of a delimitation or seat-allotment law.
9. Article 329(b): an election is challengeable only by election petition as provided by law.
10. Article 329A was inserted in 1975 and **omitted** by the Forty-fourth Amendment, 1978.
11. *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975) anchors free and fair elections in the basic structure.
12. Presidential and Vice-Presidential election disputes go to the Supreme Court under Article 71.
13. Articles 103 and 192: the President or Governor obtains the ECI's opinion and acts according to it.
14. Defection disqualification belongs to the Speaker or Chairman under the Tenth Schedule.

### Concept check

**Question:** A petitioner approaches a High Court three days before polling, arguing that a particular constituency's boundaries were drawn under an unconstitutional delimitation law, and separately that the returning officer wrongly rejected a nomination. Which constitutional provisions govern each limb, and what is the likely outcome at that stage?

**Model answer:** The delimitation-law limb runs into **Article 329(a)**, which bars any court from questioning the validity of a law relating to delimitation of constituencies or allotment of seats made under Article 327 or 328. The nomination-rejection limb runs into **Article 329(b)**: the election may be called in question only by an election petition presented under the law made by the appropriate Legislature, and improper rejection of a nomination is a statutory ground to be raised there after the result. At three days before the poll, neither limb supports interrupting the process; the remedy is post-result, before the High Court.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing Article 329 abolishes judicial review of elections altogether. It relocates and times the remedy; it does not extinguish it, and *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* shows that removing the remedy entirely offends the basic structure.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** Examine how Articles 324, 327 and 329 together produce a workable balance between constitutional supervision, legislative specification and judicial restraint during an election. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (142 words):**
The three articles divide one problem among three organs. Article 324 vests superintendence, direction and control in the Election Commission, giving a neutral authority power to act inside the short election window where delayed remedies are useless. Article 327 authorises Parliament to specify the detail, producing the Representation of the People Acts of 1950 and 1951, so that supervision operates within enacted standards rather than discretion. Article 329 then restrains courts: clause (a) protects delimitation legislation from challenge, and clause (b) channels every election dispute into a post-result election petition, preventing litigation from freezing a national timetable. The balance works because each limb compensates for the others' weakness: statute disciplines supervision, supervision makes statute operable in real time, and deferred adjudication preserves both. Its cost is delay, which *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* shows cannot be cured by abolishing the remedy.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The marks are in the word "together". A script that treats the three articles as three separate paragraphs without showing the compensating relationship will not cross the mid-band, however accurate each paragraph is.

### Why the next lesson follows

The constitutional environment is now mapped. The next question is structural: Article 324(2) says the Commission consists of the Chief Election Commissioner "and such number of other Election Commissioners, if any, as the President may from time to time fix". That phrase has produced forty years of institutional change and one of the most-tested asymmetries in Indian Polity.

---

## Lesson 3: Composition, Chairmanship and Collegiality

Progress: 3 / 20 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Article 324(2) to (5), the 1989–1993 story, *T.N. Seshan*, and Regional Commissioners

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — Laxmikanth Chapter 42 composition and independence sections read
CA search: "Election Commission composition multi-member 2026"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: one chair, three equal votes, two unequal protections

```text
                    PRESIDENT fixes the number
                              |
              +---------------+---------------+
              |               |               |
            CEC              EC              EC
         (Chairman)
              |               |               |
 VOTE:        1               1               1   -> unanimity if possible,
              |               |               |      else majority
 REMOVAL:  like an SC    only on CEC's   only on CEC's
            judge        recommendation  recommendation

 REGIONAL COMMISSIONER (Art 324(4)) --- appointed to ASSIST,
   removable only on the CEC's recommendation (Art 324(5))
```

Look at the two rows marked VOTE and REMOVAL. They are deliberately different, and almost every objective question on composition lives in the gap between them. Equal decisional power; unequal security of tenure.

### 1. The Constitution does not fix the number

Article 324(2) provides that the Election Commission shall consist of the Chief Election Commissioner **and such number of other Election Commissioners, if any, as the President may from time to time fix**, and that the appointment of the CEC and other ECs shall, **subject to the provisions of any law made in that behalf by Parliament**, be made by the President.

Three consequences, each separately examinable:

1. A one-member Commission is constitutionally permissible. The words "if any" say so expressly.
2. The present strength of three is an executive and statutory settlement, not a constitutional command. Section 3 of the 2023 Act repeats the same formula.
3. The appointment power was always **conditional on a parliamentary law**. That clause is the hinge of the entire appointment controversy in Lessons 4 to 7: Parliament was invited from the beginning to legislate, and for over seven decades it did not do so on the appointment question.

### 2. The institutional story, told as a sequence

```text
25 Jan 1950   Election Commission established; single-member body
      |
15 Oct 1989   still single-member (the CEC alone)
16 Oct 1989   two Election Commissioners appointed
      |         (workload after the voting age fell from 21 to 18)
Jan 1990      the two posts discontinued; back to a single member
      |
Oct 1993      two Election Commissioners appointed again
      |
since 1993    three-member body, continuously
```

Teach yourself the *reason* at each step, not the dates alone. The 1989 expansion followed the Sixty-first Amendment's enlargement of the electorate. The 1990 reversion and the 1993 re-expansion were contested political decisions about whether a single powerful CEC or a collegial body better served neutrality. The litigation that followed settled the principle.

### 3. *T.N. Seshan v. Union of India* (1995): what was actually decided

The challenge argued, in substance, that the CEC was the Commission and that other Election Commissioners were subordinates, so that converting the body into a three-member commission with equal powers undermined the CEC's constitutional position.

The Court upheld the multi-member design. The holding has three limbs worth remembering separately:

- A multi-member Election Commission is constitutionally valid; Article 324(2) contemplates it.
- The CEC is the **Chairman** under Article 324(3), but chairmanship confers no superior vote and no power to overrule colleagues.
- Differences of opinion are resolved by **majority**, and the CEC's distinct removal protection does not convert him into a hierarchical superior in decision-making.

The reasoning is worth one sentence in an answer: the Constitution protects the *office* of the CEC more strongly because that office cannot be left vulnerable to executive pleasure, but protection of tenure is not the same thing as supremacy in deliberation.

### 4. Chairmanship: what it does and does not give

| The CEC as Chairman does | The CEC as Chairman does not |
|---|---|
| Preside over the Commission under Article 324(3) | Hold a casting or weighted vote |
| Lead the institution and set the agenda | Override a majority of two Election Commissioners |
| Recommend removal of an EC or a Regional Commissioner | Remove them personally |
| Represent the Commission institutionally | Decide alone matters allocated to the Commission |

The third row repays attention. The CEC's power under Article 324(5) is a **recommendation**; removal is effected by the President. Writing "the CEC can remove an Election Commissioner" is wrong in a way that costs a mark.

### 5. Regional Commissioners: the most misdescribed office in the topic

Article 324(4) is narrower than most notes suggest. Read it:

> Before each general election to the House of the People and to the Legislative Assembly of each State, and before the first general election and thereafter before each biennial election to the Legislative Council of each State having such Council, the President may also appoint after consultation with the Election Commission such Regional Commissioners as he may consider necessary to assist the Election Commission in the performance of the functions conferred on the Commission by clause (1).

Four controls sit in that sentence:

1. The appointment is tied to the **run-up to specified general or biennial elections** — it is not a standing power exercisable at any time for any purpose.
2. The appointing authority is the **President**, not the Commission.
3. Consultation with the Election Commission is mandatory before appointment.
4. The function is **to assist** the Commission in its Article 324(1) work.

Article 324(5)'s second proviso then protects the office: a Regional Commissioner shall not be removed from office **except on the recommendation of the Chief Election Commissioner** — the same protection as an Election Commissioner, and weaker than the CEC's.

```text
NOT a Regional Commissioner:
  X  head of a State Election Commission
  X  the Chief Electoral Officer of a State
  X  a permanent zonal election authority
  X  an authority that decides election petitions
```

The Chief Electoral Officer, by contrast, is a statutory officer under the Representation of the People Act, 1950, and we meet that machinery in Lesson 8. Keep the constitutional assistant and the statutory officer in separate compartments.

### 6. The removal asymmetry: state it precisely or not at all

The first proviso to Article 324(5): the **Chief Election Commissioner shall not be removed from his office except in like manner and on the like grounds as a Judge of the Supreme Court**, and his conditions of service shall not be varied to his disadvantage after appointment. The second proviso: any **other Election Commissioner or a Regional Commissioner shall not be removed from office except on the recommendation of the Chief Election Commissioner**.

Translate the first proviso into process, because examiners test the process: removal of a Supreme Court judge requires an address by **each House of Parliament supported by a majority of the total membership of that House and by a majority of not less than two-thirds of the members present and voting**, presented to the President in the same session, on the ground of **proved misbehaviour or incapacity**. The CEC therefore does not hold office during the pleasure of the President.

| Office | Removal route | Relative security |
|---|---|---|
| Chief Election Commissioner | President, following the parliamentary special-majority address process applicable to a Supreme Court judge, on proved misbehaviour or incapacity | Highest |
| Election Commissioner | President, only on the recommendation of the CEC | Intermediate, and dependent on the CEC |
| Regional Commissioner | President, only on the recommendation of the CEC | Intermediate, and dependent on the CEC |

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** If the two Election Commissioners vote equally with the CEC but can be removed on his recommendation, their independence is conditional on his goodwill. An EC who dissents regularly is structurally exposed.

**Reply.** The protection is real, not nominal. Before 1993 the practical fear was executive removal of an inconvenient member; the CEC-recommendation rule takes that lever out of the government's hands entirely, since the executive cannot initiate removal at all. *T.N. Seshan* also prevents the asymmetry from leaking into decision-making, so the dissent itself cannot be overruled.

**Residual.** The reply does not fully answer the objection. The rule removes executive pressure but substitutes intra-institutional dependence, and the Constitution gives the Election Commissioner no procedural safeguard — no inquiry, no hearing, no reasons — against a recommendation. This is a genuine, undecided design gap, and the standard reform proposal to equalise removal protection is evaluated in Lesson 7.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: powers, functions and responsibilities of Constitutional Bodies; appointment to constitutional posts.

**Where this lesson is tested.** Composition and the removal asymmetry are the highest-frequency objective content in this topic. *T.N. Seshan* is the standard case anchor for the collegial principle.

**Probable framings.** "Should the two Election Commissioners enjoy the same removal protection as the Chief Election Commissioner? Discuss." Or a statement question on whether the Constitution fixes the number of members.

**Traps.**

- "The Constitution provides for a three-member Commission." It does not.
- "The CEC has a casting vote." He does not; disputes go by majority.
- "All members are removed like Supreme Court judges." Only the CEC.
- "The CEC removes an Election Commissioner." He recommends; the President removes.
- "A Regional Commissioner heads the State Election Commission." Unrelated offices.

**Answer use.** When a question asks about independence, open with the asymmetry rather than the uniformity. The asymmetry is the analytical content; the uniformity of voting power is the qualification.

### Revision notes

1. Article 324(2): CEC **and such number of other ECs, if any**, as the President may fix.
2. Appointment by the President, **subject to any law made by Parliament**.
3. Article 324(3): when other ECs are appointed, the CEC acts as **Chairman**.
4. Single-member from 1950; two ECs on 16 October 1989; posts discontinued January 1990; two ECs again in October 1993; three-member since.
5. The 1989 expansion followed the electorate's enlargement by the Sixty-first Amendment.
6. *T.N. Seshan v. Union of India* (1995) upheld the multi-member body and equal decisional status.
7. Chairmanship gives no superior or casting vote; differences go by majority.
8. Article 324(4): Regional Commissioners appointed by the President, after consultation with the ECI, before specified general or biennial elections, **to assist**.
9. Article 324(5) first proviso: CEC removable only like a Supreme Court judge; service conditions not to be varied to his disadvantage.
10. Article 324(5) second proviso: other ECs and Regional Commissioners removable only on the CEC's recommendation.
11. Supreme Court judge removal: address by each House, special majority, proved misbehaviour or incapacity.
12. The CEC does not hold office during the pleasure of the President.
13. Equal votes and unequal removal protection coexist by design.
14. The unresolved gap: no stated procedure governs a CEC's removal recommendation.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two Election Commissioners outvote the Chief Election Commissioner on a contested decision about deferring a poll. The CEC records that as Chairman his view must prevail, and separately writes to the President recommending removal of one of the dissenting Commissioners. Assess both steps.

**Model answer:** The first step is bad in law. Article 324(3) makes the CEC Chairman, but *T.N. Seshan* holds that chairmanship carries no superior or casting vote; where opinions differ, the matter is decided by the majority, so the two Commissioners' view is the Commission's decision. The second step is formally within Article 324(5), which permits removal of an Election Commissioner only on the CEC's recommendation — but the power exists to protect the office from executive pressure, not to punish lawful dissent, and a recommendation made to reverse a majority decision would be an exercise for a collateral purpose, open to challenge on ordinary administrative-law grounds.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting stronger tenure protection into greater decisional authority. The two are independent: the CEC is better protected *and* equally outvoted.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** "Equal power without equal protection is an unstable institutional design." Critically examine this claim with reference to the Chief Election Commissioner and the other Election Commissioners. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (250 words):**
The claim identifies a real asymmetry but overstates its instability.

**The design.** Article 324(2) allows the President to fix the number of Election Commissioners; since October 1993 the Commission has been a three-member body. Article 324(3) makes the CEC Chairman. *T.N. Seshan v. Union of India* (1995) upheld this structure and held that the CEC's chairmanship carries no superior or casting vote, differences being resolved by majority. Decisional power is equal.

**The asymmetry.** The first proviso to Article 324(5) protects the CEC with Supreme Court judge removal — an address by each House supported by a majority of total membership and two-thirds of those present and voting, on proved misbehaviour or incapacity — and bars adverse variation of his service conditions. The second proviso protects other Election Commissioners and Regional Commissioners only through the requirement of the CEC's recommendation.

**Why it is unstable.** A Commissioner who dissents persistently depends on the goodwill of the colleague he has outvoted, and the Constitution prescribes no inquiry, hearing or reasons before such a recommendation.

**Why it is defensible.** The rule removes the government entirely from the removal of Election Commissioners, which was the historically realistic threat; and *Seshan* prevents the asymmetry from entering deliberation.

**Verdict.** The instability is procedural, not structural. Equalising removal to the parliamentary route would secure dissent but could make serious misconduct practically unaddressable. A calibrated reform — retaining the CEC-recommendation requirement while adding a mandatory independent inquiry, notice and recorded reasons — closes the gap without creating irremovable members.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** Reward the candidate who separates *decisional* equality from *tenure* security and then proposes a middle option. An answer that simply demands identical removal protection, without confronting the accountability cost, is incomplete even if well written.

### Why the next lesson follows

Article 324(2) ends with the words "subject to the provisions of any law made in that behalf by Parliament". For seventy-three years no such appointment law existed, and the vacuum was filled by executive practice. In 2023 the Supreme Court decided it could no longer wait. That judgment, and what it actually directed, is Lesson 4.

---
## Lesson 4: The Appointment Problem and *Anoop Baranwal*

Progress: 4 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The 1991 Act, the seventy-three-year vacuum, and exactly what the 2023 judgment directed

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — the enacted text of Act 49 of 2023 verified against the judgment's operative directions
CA search: "Anoop Baranwal v Union of India appointment Election Commissioners"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson; the dated litigation status is placed in Lesson 7
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: how a constitutional invitation became a judicial stopgap

```text
1950  Article 324(2): appointment by the President,
      "subject to the provisions of any law made in that
       behalf by Parliament"
          |
          |  Parliament legislates on SERVICE CONDITIONS
          |  (Act 11 of 1991) but NOT on APPOINTMENT
          v
1950-2023  appointment governed by executive practice:
           Government selects -> President appoints
          |
2 Mar 2023  Anoop Baranwal v. Union of India
            Constitution Bench fills the vacuum with an
            interim committee: PM + LoP (or leader of the
            single largest opposition party) + CJI
          |         expressly "till a law is made by Parliament"
          v
28 Dec 2023  Act 49 of 2023 receives assent
 2 Jan 2024  Act commences; 1991 Act repealed;
             CJI replaced by a PM-nominated Union Cabinet Minister
```

The arrow labelled "expressly" is the whole lesson. Miss it and you will write the single most common factual error in this topic.

### 1. The gap, stated without rhetoric

Article 324(2) does two things at once. It gives the appointment power to the President — meaning, in our system, the Union Council of Ministers — and it makes that power **subject to any law Parliament may make**. The Constitution thus anticipated a statute and left the executive in charge until one arrived.

Parliament did legislate, but on a different question. The **Election Commission (Conditions of Service of Election Commissioners and Transaction of Business) Act, 1991 (Act 11 of 1991)** dealt with salary, service conditions and how the Commission transacts business. It did not prescribe how the CEC and ECs were to be **selected**. So the selection stage remained what it had been since 1950: a recommendation prepared within the government, forwarded to the President.

Laxmikanth's standard criticism of the constitutional scheme identifies exactly this cluster of silences:

- the Constitution prescribes **no qualifications** — legal, educational, administrative or judicial — for members of the Commission;
- it does not specify their **term**;
- it does not **debar** a retiring Election Commissioner from further appointment by the government.

Three silences, and the appointment silence is the deepest, because it determines who fills the office in which the other two silences operate.

### 2. Why the silence mattered more than it looks

The intuitive reply is that every constitutional appointment in India is executive-led — judges apart — so why single out the Election Commission?

Because of what the office does. A regulator of banks supervises actors who are not the appointing government. An election manager supervises, during the campaign period, the conduct of the very government that selected him, and decides questions — poll dates, transfers of officials, enforcement against the party in power — in which that government is an interested party. The structural conflict is not hypothetical; it is the ordinary condition of the job.

That is why the argument is usually framed not merely as "independence" but as **the appearance of independence**. An electorate that doubts the selection of the umpire will doubt the score.

### 3. What the Court actually held in 2023

In ***Anoop Baranwal v. Union of India***, decided on **2 March 2023**, a Constitution Bench addressed petitions seeking an independent appointment mechanism. The operative response had a precise shape, and each element is separately examinable.

| Element | What the Court did |
|---|---|
| Diagnosis | Article 324(2) contemplated a parliamentary law; its prolonged absence left appointment to unregulated executive discretion |
| Remedy | Directed that appointments be made by the President on the advice of a committee of the **Prime Minister**, the **Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha** — or, where none is recognised, the **leader of the single largest opposition party** — and the **Chief Justice of India** |
| Duration | Expressly operative **"till a law is made by the Parliament"** |
| Past appointments | Not invalidated wholesale; the remedy was prospective and institutional |
| Further observations | A strongly worded appeal, not an operative direction, that a permanent secretariat and charged expenditure be considered |

Two of those rows are where marks are won.

**The duration row.** The committee was a **stopgap**, created to end a vacuum, not a constitutional amendment adding the CJI to Article 324. The Court said so in terms. Any answer asserting that the CJI was permanently placed on the panel misstates the judgment.

**The appeal row.** The Court's request that Parliament consider a permanent secretariat for the Commission and that its expenditure be charged on the Consolidated Fund of India was framed as an appeal. It is persuasive, not self-executing. Writing "the Supreme Court directed that ECI expenditure be charged" converts an appeal into an order.

### 4. The constitutional logic of a temporary judicial committee

Students often ask how a court can create an appointment procedure at all. The reasoning runs like this:

```text
STEP 1  Free and fair elections are part of the basic structure
          (Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain)
STEP 2  A free and fair election presupposes an independent
          election manager
STEP 3  Article 324(2) itself contemplates a law to regulate
          appointment; the Constitution did not intend unregulated
          executive choice as the permanent arrangement
STEP 4  Prolonged legislative silence has produced exactly that
STEP 5  The Court may fill the vacuum provisionally to protect the
          constitutional value, while expressly preserving
          Parliament's primacy
```

Step 5 is the restraint that makes the judgment defensible — and, simultaneously, the reason the Court could not complain when Parliament legislated differently nine months later. A stopgap that reserves the legislature's space must yield to the legislature's choice; the remaining question is not whether Parliament may legislate but whether the particular law it made satisfies the independence requirement that Step 2 identifies.

That distinction — **competence** versus **content** — is the single most useful sentence you can carry into any question on this controversy.

### 5. Example, and the limit of the example

Compare the appointment of the Director of the Central Bureau of Investigation. There, a statutory committee of the Prime Minister, the Leader of Opposition and the Chief Justice of India (or a nominee) selects, and Parliament itself wrote that formula into law. It is often cited as proof that a judge-inclusive panel is the natural model.

The limit of the comparison is important. The CBI Director's committee exists **because a statute says so**, and Parliament could amend it. It is therefore evidence that such a committee is workable, not evidence that it is constitutionally compelled for the Election Commission. Use the comparison to show feasibility; do not use it to claim a constitutional rule.

### 6. The strongest objection to the judgment, and the reply

**Objection.** A court that writes an appointment procedure is legislating. Article 324(2) reserved the field to Parliament, and the correct judicial response to legislative inaction is a declaration, not a substitute scheme.

**Reply.** The Court did not claim a permanent power. It supplied a default rule operative only until Parliament acted — a well-recognised interim technique where a constitutional value is being eroded by inaction, and one that leaves the legislature free to replace it at any time. The remedy was also minimal: it changed *who advises*, not what the office does.

**Residual.** The reply does not dissolve the difficulty. An interim scheme that lasts long enough becomes an expectation, and when the legislature departs from it the departure is read as a rebuke rather than an ordinary exercise of competence. That dynamic explains the temperature of the debate that followed. Say so; do not pretend the criticism is frivolous.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: appointment to constitutional posts; separation of powers; judicial pronouncements affecting institutional design.

**Where this lesson is tested.** It supplies the institutional half of any "independence of the ECI" question, and the historical half of any question on the 2023 Act.

**Probable framings.** "The independence of the Election Commission depends more on the appointment process than on removal safeguards. Examine." Or an objective item on what *Anoop Baranwal* directed.

**Traps.**

- Saying the judgment permanently added the CJI to the selection panel.
- Saying it struck down past appointments.
- Saying it prohibited Parliament from legislating.
- Treating the secretariat and charged-expenditure observation as an operative direction.
- Forgetting that an Act of 1991 already existed on service conditions, and calling 2023 "the first law on the Election Commission".

**Answer use.** Structure the controversy as competence versus content. Concede competence in one line — Article 324(2) expressly permits a law — and spend your words on content, which is where the real argument lives.

### Revision notes

1. Article 324(2) made appointment **subject to any law made by Parliament**; no appointment law existed until 2023.
2. The **Election Commission (Conditions of Service of Election Commissioners and Transaction of Business) Act, 1991 (Act 11 of 1991)** covered service conditions and transaction of business, not selection.
3. Laxmikanth's three constitutional silences: no prescribed qualifications, no specified term, no bar on later government appointment.
4. ***Anoop Baranwal v. Union of India***, **2 March 2023**, Constitution Bench.
5. Interim committee directed: **PM + Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha (or leader of the single largest opposition party) + CJI**.
6. The direction operated expressly **"till a law is made by the Parliament"**.
7. Past appointments were not invalidated wholesale.
8. The Court made a **fervent appeal** — not an operative declaration — on a permanent secretariat and charged expenditure.
9. The structural problem is the appearance of independence, not merely actual bias.
10. Separate **competence** (Parliament may legislate) from **content** (whether this law secures independence).
11. The CBI Director precedent shows feasibility of a judge-inclusive panel, not constitutional compulsion.
12. Act 49 of 2023 followed on **28 December 2023** and commenced **2 January 2024**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A commentator writes: "In 2023 the Supreme Court amended the Constitution to put the Chief Justice of India on the panel that appoints Election Commissioners, and Parliament then unconstitutionally reversed the amendment." Identify every error.

**Model answer:** Three errors. First, a court cannot amend the Constitution, and *Anoop Baranwal* did not purport to; it supplied an interim appointment norm under Article 324(2), which itself contemplates a parliamentary law. Second, the direction was expressly temporary — operative only "till a law is made by the Parliament" — so a later statute displacing it is the judgment's own stated consequence, not a reversal of it. Third, "unconstitutionally" assumes the answer: Parliament's competence to legislate is express, and the contested question is whether the content of the law — an executive-weighted selection committee — satisfies the independence requirement, a question that remains before the Court.

**Misconception to avoid:** Collapsing competence into content. Parliament clearly had the power to legislate; whether this particular formula is constitutionally adequate is a separate and still-open question.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** "A judicial stopgap that preserves legislative space cannot complain when the legislature departs from it." Comment with reference to the appointment of Election Commissioners. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (147 words):**
The proposition is largely sound. In *Anoop Baranwal* (2 March 2023) the Supreme Court responded to the prolonged absence of the law contemplated by Article 324(2) by directing appointment on the advice of the Prime Minister, the Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha and the Chief Justice of India — expressly "till a law is made by the Parliament". That reservation was the source of the remedy's legitimacy; a court filling a vacuum cannot simultaneously occupy the field. When Parliament enacted Act 49 of 2023, the interim norm lapsed by its own terms, and objections framed as defiance of the Court are misdirected. The proposition is not, however, complete: judicial review survives the stopgap's expiry. Parliament's competence is conceded, but the content of its law remains testable against the institutional independence that free and fair elections require. Deference on competence does not mean abdication on content.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The directive is "comment", which requires a position plus its qualification. Award the top band only where the script both accepts the proposition and identifies the surviving ground of review; scripts that merely narrate the judgment do not engage the directive.

### Why the next lesson follows

Parliament's answer was Act 49 of 2023. Coaching summaries of that Act are unusually unreliable because several Bill-stage provisions were changed before enactment. The next lesson reads the enacted text itself, section by section.

---

## Lesson 5: Act 49 of 2023 Read Closely — Sections 3 to 9

Progress: 5 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Composition, appointment, qualifications, Search Committee, Selection Committee, procedure and term

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — enacted text of Act No. 49 of 2023 read in full, sections 1 to 21
CA search: "CEC and other Election Commissioners Act 2023 enacted text sections"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: the appointment pipeline the Act creates

```text
   ELIGIBILITY FILTER  (s.5)
   holds or has held a post equivalent to the rank of
   SECRETARY TO THE GOVERNMENT OF INDIA
        + integrity
        + knowledge of and experience in the management
          and conduct of elections
                 |
                 v
   SEARCH COMMITTEE  (s.6)
   Minister of Law and Justice  ---- head
   two members not below the rank of Secretary to GoI
        -> prepares a PANEL OF FIVE PERSONS
                 |
                 v
   SELECTION COMMITTEE  (s.7)
   Prime Minister ................ Chairperson
   Leader of Opposition, Lok Sabha ... Member
   Union Cabinet Minister nominated by the PM ... Member
        -> may go OUTSIDE the panel of five  (s.8(2))
        -> regulates its own procedure "in a transparent
           manner"                              (s.8(1))
                 |
                 v
   PRESIDENT appoints by WARRANT UNDER HIS HAND AND SEAL  (s.4)
                 |
                 v
   TERM  (s.9)  six years from assuming office OR age 65,
                whichever is earlier; no re-appointment;
                EC-plus-CEC service capped at six years in aggregate
```

### 1. Why the enacted text, and not a summary, controls

Two provisions changed between the Bill and the Act, and both are standard objective-question material.

| Point | Bill stage, widely reproduced | Enacted Act 49 of 2023 |
|---|---|---|
| Salary comparator | Cabinet Secretary | **Salary equal to the salary of a Judge of the Supreme Court** (section 10(1)) |
| Search Committee head | Cabinet Secretary in several summaries | **Minister of Law and Justice** (section 6) |

If you have memorised "salary equal to Cabinet Secretary", unlearn it now. Section 10(1) of the enacted Act reads: "The Chief Election Commissioner and other Election Commissioners shall be paid a salary which is equal to the salary of a Judge of the Supreme Court". We return to section 10 in Lesson 6; it is flagged here because the Bill-stage error travels with the appointment story.

Three identifying facts worth holding: the statute is **Act No. 49 of 2023**; it received assent on **28 December 2023**; and it came into force on **2 January 2024** by notification S.O. 35(E).

### 2. Section 3: the Act does not constitutionalise three members either

Section 3 provides that the Election Commission shall consist of the Chief Election Commissioner **and such number of other Election Commissioners as the President may fix from time to time**. It reproduces Article 324(2)'s flexibility rather than fixing a number.

So the answer to "who fixes the number of Election Commissioners?" is the same before and after 2023: the **President**. Neither the Constitution nor the 2023 Act writes "two" into law.

### 3. Section 5: a qualification where the Constitution had none

Section 5 states that the CEC and other ECs **shall be appointed from amongst persons who are holding or have held a post equivalent to the rank of Secretary to the Government of India** and shall be **persons of integrity, who have knowledge of and experience in management and conduct of elections**.

Read the provision for what it adds and what it does not.

| Section 5 requires | Section 5 does not require |
|---|---|
| Current or past Secretary-equivalent rank | Judicial office or a legal qualification |
| Integrity | A prescribed degree or academic qualification |
| Knowledge of and experience in the management and conduct of elections | Prior service inside the Election Commission |

This is a genuine improvement on the constitutional blank that Laxmikanth criticised — there is now a stated eligibility standard. It is also a narrowing: the pool is drawn almost entirely from the senior civil service, which is simultaneously the group most familiar with election administration and the group whose careers have been spent under governments. Both halves of that sentence belong in a balanced answer.

A quiet interpretive question worth noticing: what does "knowledge of and experience in management and conduct of elections" mean for a career officer who has served as a District Election Officer or Chief Electoral Officer at some point? The Act supplies no test and no requirement of reasons on this point, which is why the transparency obligation in section 8(1) carries weight it might not otherwise have.

### 4. Section 6: the Search Committee

A Search Committee **headed by the Minister of Law and Justice**, comprising **two other members not below the rank of Secretary to the Government of India**, prepares a **panel of five persons** for the consideration of the Selection Committee.

Three precise points:

1. The head is a **political executive** — a Union Minister — not a civil servant.
2. The other two members are **officials of Secretary rank or above**; the Act sets a floor, not a fixed designation.
3. The output is a **panel of five**, not a single name and not a shortlist of unspecified length.

### 5. Sections 7 and 8: the Selection Committee and its latitude

Section 7(1) provides that the CEC and other ECs shall be appointed by the President **on the recommendation of a Selection Committee** consisting of:

- the **Prime Minister** — Chairperson;
- the **Leader of Opposition in the House of the People** — Member;
- a **Union Cabinet Minister to be nominated by the Prime Minister** — Member.

An **Explanation** to section 7 removes a practical doubt: where no Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha has been recognised, the **leader of the single largest party in opposition** is deemed to be the Leader of Opposition. This matters because recognition of an LoP depends on a party reaching a threshold of seats, which has not always happened.

Section 7(2) adds a saving clause: the appointment **shall not be invalid merely by reason of any vacancy in, or any defect in the constitution of, the Selection Committee**.

Section 8 then supplies the committee's working rules:

- **8(1)**: the Selection Committee **shall regulate its own procedure in a transparent manner**;
- **8(2)**: the Selection Committee **may also consider any other person than those included in the panel** by the Search Committee.

```text
   Search panel of five  ----------+
                                   |
   any other person (s.8(2))  -----+---> Selection Committee
                                   |     (self-regulated procedure,
                                   |      required to be transparent)
                                   v
                           recommendation to the President
```

Section 8(2) is doctrinally interesting. Without it, a Search Committee headed by a Minister could effectively control the outcome by controlling the list. With it, the five-name panel is not a cage — but the committee's freedom then depends entirely on how it exercises the transparency obligation in section 8(1), because the Act prescribes no criteria, no minimum deliberation time, and no requirement of recorded comparative reasons.

The honest formulation for an answer: section 8(2) removes one risk and creates another, and the Act leaves the second risk to be managed by practice rather than by text.

### 6. Section 9: term, age and the aggregate cap

Section 9 provides:

1. the CEC and other ECs hold office for a term of **six years from the date on which he assumes his office, or till he attains the age of sixty-five years, whichever is earlier**;
2. they are **not eligible for re-appointment**;
3. where an **Election Commissioner is appointed as Chief Election Commissioner**, his term shall **not be more than six years in aggregate** as Election Commissioner and Chief Election Commissioner.

The third limb is the one candidates miss. It prevents tenure-stacking: a person cannot serve six years as EC and then a fresh six as CEC.

```text
   END OF TENURE = the EARLIER of
        A.  date of assuming office + 6 years
        B.  65th birthday

   If an EC is later appointed CEC:
        (EC service) + (CEC service)  <=  6 years in aggregate

   Re-appointment:  barred
```

Say the constitutional caution aloud: **the six-year or sixty-five-year rule is statutory**. Article 324 does not state it. Before 2023 it came from the rules and the 1991 framework; it now sits in section 9. A statement that "the Constitution fixes a six-year term for the CEC" is simply false, and it is tested.

### 7. Comparison: what changed, and what only looks changed

| Question | Before 2 January 2024 | After |
|---|---|---|
| Who appoints? | President | President (unchanged) |
| Who advises on selection? | Government, by practice; then the *Anoop Baranwal* interim committee from March 2023 | Statutory Selection Committee under section 7 |
| Is there an eligibility standard? | No constitutional or statutory standard | Section 5 standard |
| Is there a formal search stage? | No statutory stage | Section 6 Search Committee, panel of five |
| Who fixes the number of ECs? | President | President (unchanged) |
| What is the term? | Six years or 65, from the earlier statutory framework | Six years or 65, section 9, with aggregate cap |
| Is re-appointment barred? | Not by the Constitution | Expressly barred by section 9(2) |

Reading the table as an argument rather than a list: the Act **professionalised and formalised** the process — eligibility, search, statutory term, express bar on re-appointment — while **retaining executive predominance at the decisive stage**. Both halves are true at once, and an answer that reports only one of them is incomplete.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: appointment to constitutional posts; statutory bodies and their governing legislation.

**Where this lesson is tested.** Section-level detail is objective-question territory, and the search-versus-selection distinction is a reliable close-option trap. The professionalisation-versus-predominance tension is the Mains content.

**Probable framings.** "Critically examine the appointment architecture created by the Chief Election Commissioner and other Election Commissioners Act, 2023." Or an objective item on who heads the Search Committee.

**Traps.**

- Attributing the Search Committee to the Cabinet Secretary.
- Saying the Selection Committee is confined to the five-name panel.
- Saying the Act fixes the number of Election Commissioners at two.
- Saying the six-year term is constitutional.
- Forgetting the aggregate six-year cap where an EC becomes CEC.
- Forgetting the Explanation that supplies a deemed Leader of Opposition.

**Answer use.** When you cite the Act, cite a section number. "Section 6", "section 7(1)", "section 8(2)", "section 9(3)" read as command of the statute; "the 2023 law says" reads as command of a newspaper.

### Revision notes

1. **Act No. 49 of 2023**; assent **28 December 2023**; in force **2 January 2024** (S.O. 35(E)).
2. Section 3: Commission consists of the CEC and such number of other ECs as the **President** may fix.
3. Section 4: appointment by the **President by warrant under his hand and seal**.
4. Section 5: appointee must hold or have held a post **equivalent to the rank of Secretary to the Government of India**, with integrity and knowledge and experience of election management and conduct.
5. Section 6: **Search Committee headed by the Minister of Law and Justice**, two other members not below Secretary rank, prepares a **panel of five**.
6. Section 7(1): **Selection Committee** = PM (Chairperson) + Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha + a **PM-nominated Union Cabinet Minister**.
7. Explanation to section 7: where no LoP is recognised, the **leader of the single largest opposition party** is deemed LoP.
8. Section 7(2): appointment not invalid merely for a vacancy or defect in the committee's constitution.
9. Section 8(1): committee regulates its own procedure **in a transparent manner**.
10. Section 8(2): committee **may consider a person outside** the Search Committee's panel.
11. Section 9(1): **six years from assuming office, or age 65, whichever is earlier**.
12. Section 9(2): **no re-appointment**.
13. Section 9(3): EC plus CEC service capped at **six years in aggregate**.
14. The tenure rule is **statutory**, not constitutional.
15. Enacted salary comparator is a **Judge of the Supreme Court**, not the Cabinet Secretary.

### Concept check

**Question:** A Search Committee forwards a panel of five names. The Selection Committee recommends a sixth person not on that panel, and one member of the committee is absent from the meeting. A petitioner says both facts vitiate the appointment. Evaluate, naming the provisions.

**Model answer:** Neither fact vitiates the appointment on the face of the statute. **Section 8(2)** expressly empowers the Selection Committee to consider a person other than those included in the Search Committee's panel, so recommending a sixth name is a statutory power, not a deviation. **Section 7(2)** provides that an appointment shall not be invalid merely by reason of any vacancy in, or defect in the constitution of, the Selection Committee, so absence alone does not invalidate it. What remains arguable is the quality of the process under **section 8(1)**, which obliges the committee to regulate its procedure in a transparent manner — a challenge would have to be built on the absence of criteria, dossiers or deliberation, not on the bare facts stated.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the five-name panel as a legal ceiling. It is a starting list that the Selection Committee may lawfully exceed; the real safeguard is transparency of procedure, not confinement to the panel.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** Critically examine the appointment architecture created by the Chief Election Commissioner and other Election Commissioners (Appointment, Conditions of Service and Term of Office) Act, 2023. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (250 words):**
The Act professionalises the appointment process while leaving its decisive stage executive-weighted.

**What it adds.** Article 324(2) always contemplated a parliamentary law but prescribed no qualification, term or procedure. Act 49 of 2023, in force from 2 January 2024, supplies all three. Section 5 confines eligibility to persons holding or having held a post equivalent to Secretary to the Government of India, who are of integrity and have knowledge and experience of election management. Section 6 creates a Search Committee headed by the Minister of Law and Justice with two members not below Secretary rank, producing a panel of five. Section 9 fixes six years or age sixty-five, bars re-appointment, and caps aggregate EC-plus-CEC service at six years, closing the tenure-stacking risk.

**What it leaves open.** Section 7's Selection Committee comprises the Prime Minister, the Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha and a Union Cabinet Minister nominated by the Prime Minister — two of three members drawn from the governing executive. Section 8(2) permits selection outside the Search Committee's panel, and section 7(2) saves an appointment despite a vacancy or defect in the committee. Section 8(1) requires transparent self-regulated procedure but prescribes no criteria, dossier timelines or recorded comparative reasons.

**Assessment.** The reform is real on eligibility and tenure, thin on the independence question that prompted it, since the composition determines outcomes that transparency language alone cannot discipline.

**Way forward.** Publish criteria and the shortlist principle, require adequate dossier time, mandate recorded comparative reasons, and report selections to Parliament.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The question says "architecture", so the script must cover eligibility, search, selection and tenure, not only the committee's composition. Reserve the highest band for answers that name at least four sections and still deliver a graded verdict rather than a verdict of approval or rejection.

### Why the next lesson follows

Sections 3 to 9 settle how a person reaches the office. Sections 10 to 18 settle what happens once there: pay, protection, removal, and how three people with one vote each actually transact business. Several of those provisions interact with Article 324(5) in ways that are easy to get wrong.

## Lesson 6: Service, Protection, Removal and Business — Sections 10 to 18

Progress: 6 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Pay and service conditions, the section 16 protection, the removal asymmetry in statute, and the collegial rule

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — enacted sections 10 to 21 of Act 49 of 2023 read in full
CA search: "Election Commission transaction of business unanimity majority"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: four protections, each with a different source and a different reach

```text
  PROTECTION                       SOURCE              REACH
  --------------------------------------------------------------------
  CEC removal like an SC judge     Art 324(5)          constitutional,
                                   + s.11(2)           highest

  CEC service conditions not to    Art 324(5)          constitutional,
  be varied to his disadvantage    first proviso       CEC only

  EC / Regional Commissioner       Art 324(5)          constitutional,
  removable only on CEC's          second proviso      dependent on CEC
  recommendation                   + s.11(3)

  No court to entertain or         s.16, Act 49        statutory, about
  continue civil/criminal          of 2023             the PERSON acting
  proceedings for official acts                        in official duty
```

Notice the right-hand column. Three protections guard the **office**; one guards the **person**. Treating them as a single bundle of "immunity" is the error this lesson removes.

### 1. Section 10: salary, and the clause everyone misquotes

Section 10(1): the CEC and other ECs **shall be paid a salary which is equal to the salary of a Judge of the Supreme Court**. A proviso adds that the salary, allowances and other conditions of service of a CEC or EC **holding office immediately before the commencement of the Act shall not be varied to their disadvantage**.

The remaining sub-sections are operational and worth one line each:

- **10(2)**: a pension-drawing appointee's salary is reduced by the amount of that pension, and by the commuted portion where applicable — a standard anti-double-payment rule for retired officers.
- **10(3)**: dearness allowance as admissible to a Judge of the Supreme Court.
- **10(4)**: encashment of **fifty per cent of earned leave** standing to credit at completion of tenure.
- **10(5)**: for an appointee who retired from government service earlier, leave-encashment is subject to the maximum admissible under the rules of his former service.

Then the qualification that stops a popular overclaim: **salary parity with a Supreme Court judge does not make the Commission's expenditure "charged" on the Consolidated Fund of India**. Article 324 contains no analogue of Article 322, which charges the expenses of the Union and State Public Service Commissions. The Commission's establishment expenditure is presented as a demand for grants within the Ministry of Law and Justice demands and is therefore votable. We return to that in Lesson 7; note here only that pay parity and financial autonomy are different questions.

### 2. Sections 11 to 15: resignation, removal and the rest of the service package

Section 11 restates the constitutional position in statutory form:

- **11(1)**: the CEC or an EC may resign at any time by writing under his hand addressed to the **President**.
- **11(2)**: the CEC **shall not be removed from his office except in like manner and on the like grounds as a Judge of the Supreme Court**.
- **11(3)**: other ECs **shall not be removed from office except on the recommendation of the Chief Election Commissioner**.

Sections 12 to 15 complete the package: leave granted under the rules of the service the appointee belonged to, with the power to grant, refuse, revoke or curtail leave vesting in the **President** (section 12); pension provisions, including the rule that a serving government officer is deemed to have retired on entering office (section 13); entitlement to subscribe to the General Provident Fund (section 14); and a rule-making power for the President on travelling allowance, medical facilities, leave travel concession, conveyance and other service conditions (section 15).

One analytically useful detail hides in section 13: a resignation counts as demission of office for the Act's purposes **only if** the person has completed the section 9 term, attained sixty-five years, or the demission is medically certified as necessitated by ill-health. The provision is about pension consequences, but it quietly discourages early exit.

### 3. Section 16: read the words, then draw the boundary

> Notwithstanding anything contained in any other law for the time being in force, no Court shall entertain or continue any civil or criminal proceedings against any person who is or was a Chief Election Commissioner or an Election Commissioner for any act, thing or word, committed, done or spoken by him when, or in the course of acting or purporting to act in the discharge of his official duty or function.

Four features of that text, each of which a loose paraphrase destroys:

1. It bars a court from **entertaining or continuing** proceedings — it covers pending cases, not only new ones.
2. It covers **civil or criminal** proceedings.
3. It protects a person who **is or was** a CEC or EC — the protection survives demitting office.
4. The connecting test is an act, thing or word done **when, or in the course of, acting or purporting to act** in discharge of official duty. "Purporting to act" widens it; "official duty or function" still anchors it.

Now the boundary, which is where the marks are:

| Section 16 plausibly covers | Section 16 does not give |
|---|---|
| A suit or prosecution against the individual for an official order or an official statement | Immunity for private conduct unconnected with office |
| Proceedings begun before and continuing after the Act's commencement | Validation of an unlawful election order |
| Words spoken in the course of, or purporting to be in, official duty | Abolition of judicial review of the Commission's decisions |

The last row is the one to say explicitly in an answer. A challenge to an ECI order is a proceeding **against the Commission's decision**, not a civil or criminal proceeding **against the person** of a Commissioner. Section 16 therefore does not touch writ jurisdiction over the Commission's orders, which remains the ordinary route for testing its exercises of power.

### 4. Sections 17 and 18: how three equals actually decide

- **Section 17**: the business of the Election Commission shall be transacted in accordance with the provisions of this Act.
- **Section 18(1)**: the Commission **may, by unanimous decision, regulate the procedure for transaction of its business and also allocation of its business** among the CEC and other ECs.
- **Section 18(2)**: all business shall, **as far as possible, be transacted unanimously**, and if the CEC and other ECs differ in opinion on any matter, the matter **shall be decided according to the opinion of the majority**.

```text
            MATTER BEFORE THE COMMISSION
                        |
              attempt unanimity  (s.18(2))
                   |          |
                 yes          no
                   |          |
         Commission        decide by MAJORITY
          decision           (s.18(2))
                                |
                        Commission decision

  Note: allocation of business among members requires a
  UNANIMOUS decision under s.18(1) --- a higher bar than
  the majority rule that decides ordinary business.
```

That last note is subtle and genuinely examinable. The Act sets **two different thresholds**: ordinary business is decided by majority, but the meta-rules — the Commission's own procedure and the distribution of work among members — require **unanimity**. The design makes sense: a majority should be able to decide an issue, but a majority should not be able to reorganise the institution so as to exclude the minority member from categories of work.

Section 18(2) is also the statutory confirmation of *T.N. Seshan*: the CEC's opinion counts as one opinion.

### 5. Sections 19 to 21: the tail that carries one high-value fact

- **Section 19**: power to remove difficulties, exercisable by the Central Government **within five years** of commencement, by order not inconsistent with the Act, every such order to be laid before each House.
- **Section 20**: every rule and order under the Act to be laid before each House for a total period of thirty days, subject to modification or annulment, without prejudice to anything previously done.
- **Section 21**: **the Election Commission (Conditions of Service of Election Commissioners and Transaction of Business) Act, 1991 (11 of 1991) is hereby repealed**, with the usual saving for things done under the repealed Act and an express preservation of section 6 of the General Clauses Act, 1897.

Section 21 is the fact most notes omit. The 2023 Act is not merely an appointment law bolted onto an existing framework; it **replaced** the 1991 Act, absorbing service conditions and transaction of business into the same statute. That is why sections 10 to 18 look familiar — they are the 1991 subject matter, re-enacted with changes, inside the new Act.

### 6. The strongest objection, and the reply

**Objection.** Taken together, sections 10 to 18 make the Commission *more* dependent on the executive than before: the President grants or refuses leave, the President makes service rules, the Central Government holds a difficulty-removal power for five years, and the officers appointed are career civil servants chosen by a committee the executive dominates.

**Reply.** Each limb is narrower than it sounds. The leave and rule-making powers are exercised by the President in the constitutional sense — that is, on advice — but they concern conditions of service, not decisions about elections; nothing in sections 12 to 15 touches the Commission's Article 324 functions. The difficulty-removal power in section 19 is time-limited, must not be inconsistent with the Act, and must be laid before Parliament. Most importantly, the one lever that would actually matter — removing an inconvenient Commissioner — is foreclosed by Article 324(5) and section 11.

**Residual.** The reply holds for the formal position and is weaker on the informal one. Service conditions can be used to signal displeasure in ways that fall short of removal, and the Constitution's non-variation guarantee protects **the CEC alone**. That asymmetry, again, is unresolved.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: conditions of service and protections of constitutional functionaries; accountability of constitutional bodies.

**Where this lesson is tested.** The salary comparator, the removal asymmetry and the majority rule are high-frequency objective items; the section 16 boundary is excellent Mains material because most candidates overstate it.

**Probable framings.** "Do the protections enjoyed by the Election Commission amount to immunity from accountability? Examine." Or a statement item on who may remove an Election Commissioner.

**Traps.**

- "Salary equal to the Cabinet Secretary." The enacted comparator is a Supreme Court judge.
- "All ECI expenditure is charged on the Consolidated Fund." It is not.
- "Section 16 means ECI decisions cannot be challenged." It protects persons, not orders.
- "The CEC removes an EC." He recommends; the President removes.
- "The Commission decides everything by majority." Procedure and allocation of business need unanimity.
- "The 1991 Act is still in force." It was repealed by section 21.

**Answer use.** For any accountability question, pair each protection with its limit in the same sentence. "Section 16 bars civil and criminal proceedings against the person for official acts, but leaves the Commission's orders fully reviewable" is worth more than two separate sentences saying each half.

### Revision notes

1. Section 10(1): salary **equal to that of a Judge of the Supreme Court**.
2. Section 10(3): dearness allowance as admissible to a Supreme Court judge; 10(4): encashment of fifty per cent of earned leave at completion of tenure.
3. Pay parity does **not** make ECI expenditure charged; Article 324 has no analogue of Article 322.
4. Section 11(1): resignation in writing addressed to the **President**.
5. Section 11(2): CEC removable only in like manner and on like grounds as a Supreme Court judge.
6. Section 11(3): other ECs removable only on the **CEC's recommendation**.
7. Article 324(5) second proviso extends the same protection to **Regional Commissioners**.
8. Section 12: power to grant, refuse, revoke or curtail leave vests in the President.
9. Section 16: no court shall **entertain or continue** civil or criminal proceedings against a present or former CEC or EC for an act, thing or word in, or purporting to be in, discharge of official duty.
10. Section 16 does not cover private conduct, validate unlawful orders, or bar review of ECI decisions.
11. Section 17: business transacted in accordance with the Act.
12. Section 18(1): procedure and **allocation of business** regulated by **unanimous** decision.
13. Section 18(2): business unanimous as far as possible; differences decided by **majority**.
14. Section 19: difficulty-removal power for **five years**, orders laid before Parliament.
15. Section 21: the **Act 11 of 1991 is repealed**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A defeated candidate files a writ petition challenging an order of the Election Commission countermanding a poll, and separately files a criminal complaint against an Election Commissioner for remarks he made at an official press conference explaining that order. How does section 16 of Act 49 of 2023 apply to each?

**Model answer:** The two proceedings are different in kind. The criminal complaint is a proceeding against a **person** who is an Election Commissioner for **words spoken in the course of acting in discharge of his official duty**, and section 16 provides that no court shall entertain or continue such a proceeding; the protection applies even after he demits office. The writ petition is not a civil or criminal proceeding against the person at all — it challenges the **Commission's order**, and section 16 says nothing about it. The countermanding order therefore remains testable on ordinary grounds of legality, fairness and proportionality.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading section 16 as general institutional immunity. It is a personal protection tied to official acts, words and things; the Commission's decisions stay within judicial review.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** "The statutory protections of the Election Commissioners secure the office without insulating its decisions." Comment. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (150 words):**
The statement is accurate. Article 324(5) and section 11 of Act 49 of 2023 secure the office: the Chief Election Commissioner is removable only in like manner and on like grounds as a Supreme Court judge, his service conditions cannot be varied to his disadvantage, and other Election Commissioners and Regional Commissioners are removable only on the CEC's recommendation, taking the removal lever out of executive hands. Section 16 adds a personal shield, barring courts from entertaining or continuing civil or criminal proceedings against a present or former Commissioner for acts, things or words in, or purporting to be in, discharge of official duty. None of this touches the Commission's orders. A decision to countermand a poll, delete a name, freeze a symbol or enforce the Model Code remains reviewable for legality and fairness. The design protects individuals from intimidation so that decisions can be fearlessly made and openly tested.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** Credit turns on the person-versus-order distinction being stated explicitly. Scripts that list protections without identifying what remains reviewable cannot exceed the middle band, because the second half of the statement is left unaddressed.

### Why the next lesson follows

We now have the office, its occupants and their protections. What we have not yet assessed is whether the whole arrangement delivers independence in practice — and that assessment must take account of where the constitutional challenge to the 2023 Act currently stands, of how the Commission is funded, and of which reform proposals are law and which are only proposals.
