## Lesson 7: Independence as a System, and the Live Constitutional Challenge

Progress: 7 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Appointment, finance, secretariat and enforcement, with the dated status of the challenge to Act 49 of 2023

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — enacted Act 49 of 2023 and the Union Budget placement of the Commission's establishment expenditure
CA search: "challenge to CEC appointment Act 2023 Supreme Court hearing 2026"
CA found: Supreme Court split verdict on referring the challenge to a larger Bench, 23 September 2026; papers directed to be placed before the Chief Justice of India for a Constitution Bench of appropriate strength (status checked 2 October 2026)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

This is the one lesson in the file that carries a live, date-sensitive anchor. Everything dated here must be re-verified before you use it in an examination hall; nothing dated here should be written as settled law.

### Visual: independence is a product, not a single guarantee

```text
      APPOINTMENT   x   TENURE AND REMOVAL   x   FINANCE
            x   SECRETARIAT AND STAFF   x   COLLEGIAL PROCESS
                 x   TRANSPARENT ENFORCEMENT
                              =
                   EFFECTIVE INDEPENDENCE

  It is a PRODUCT, not a sum:
  if any factor approaches zero, the product collapses,
  however strong the remaining factors are.
```

Why multiplication and not addition? Because the factors are not substitutes. A Commission with perfect tenure protection and a captured appointment process is not "mostly independent"; it is independent in a way that never gets tested, because the person who would have resisted was never appointed. Use this framing in Mains: it converts a list into an argument.

### 1. Factor one: appointment — the live question

The constitutional challenge to Act 49 of 2023 concentrates on **section 7**. The argument is not that Parliament lacked competence; Article 324(2) expressly contemplates a law. The argument is about composition: the Prime Minister chairs, and the third member is a Union Cabinet Minister **nominated by the Prime Minister**, so two of three seats sit on the same side of the table, and the Leader of Opposition can be outvoted in every case.

The defence is equally stateable. Parliament exercised an express constitutional power; the Leader of Opposition's presence is itself a departure from the pre-2023 position of pure executive choice; section 8(1) imposes a transparency obligation; and the appointee must satisfy section 5's eligibility standard, which did not exist before.

**Dated status, checked 2 October 2026.** The challenge remains undecided. The Supreme Court's order of **22 March 2024** declined interim interference with section 7 and with appointments made under it, treating its observations as tentative because the constitutional challenge was sub judice; the same order expressed concern that full candidate details should be circulated to every member of the Selection Committee and that procedural sanctity requires fair deliberation. On **23 September 2026** a two-judge Bench delivered a **split verdict on the preliminary question whether the challenge should be referred to a larger Bench**, and both judges directed that the papers be placed before the **Chief Justice of India for the constitution of a Constitution Bench of appropriate strength**.

The answer discipline that follows is strict:

```text
PERMITTED to write
  "The Act operates. The challenge to section 7 is pending.
   Interim interference was declined on 22 March 2024, and on
   23 September 2026 the matter was directed to be placed before
   the Chief Justice of India for a larger Bench."

NOT PERMITTED to write
  X  "the Act has been upheld"
  X  "the Act has been struck down"
  X  "the Act has been stayed"
  X  "the Chief Justice of India has been restored to the panel"
  X  any prediction of the outcome stated as fact
```

A split verdict on a **reference** is not a split verdict on the **merits**. Say "referral", not "verdict", and you will be right whatever happens next.

### 2. Factor two: tenure and removal

Covered in Lessons 3 and 6, so here only the evaluative point. The CEC's protection is genuinely strong — it requires the same parliamentary special-majority address as a Supreme Court judge's removal. The other Commissioners' protection is strong against the executive and weak against the institution. The standard reform is to equalise; the standard objection is that identical parliamentary removal for three members could make serious misconduct practically unaddressable. The middle option — retaining the CEC-recommendation requirement but attaching a mandatory independent inquiry, notice and recorded reasons — is the proposal most defensible in an answer, because it names a cost and then prices it.

### 3. Factor three: finance, where the nuance is genuinely technical

Three propositions, in order.

1. **Article 324 has no charging clause.** Article 322 charges the expenses of the Union and State Public Service Commissions on the respective Consolidated Funds. Article 324 contains no equivalent. So the Commission's administrative expenditure is **not** automatically charged.
2. **The Commission's establishment expenditure appears as a demand for grants** presented within the Ministry of Law and Justice demands in the Union Budget. Being a demand means it is **votable**, which is the formal difference from charged expenditure.
3. ***Anoop Baranwal* appealed for change.** The judgment made a **fervent appeal** that a permanent secretariat be created for the Commission and that its expenditure be charged on the Consolidated Fund of India. An appeal is not a declaration; nothing in the judgment made the expenditure charged.

| Dimension | Current position | Reform claim |
|---|---|---|
| Institutional expenditure | Presented as a demand for grants within the Law and Justice demands; votable | Charge ECI expenditure on the Consolidated Fund of India |
| Secretariat | ECI secretariat within the existing staffing and budget framework | A statutorily or constitutionally independent permanent secretariat |
| Judicial language | A judicial appeal to consider change | Not a self-executing amendment |

Two cautions. Salary parity with a Supreme Court judge under section 10(1) is **not** evidence that the whole budget is charged; those are different questions. And the practical significance of "charged" should not be oversold: charged expenditure is non-votable but still discussable, and the government retains control over the amount proposed. The reform is about removing a symbolic and procedural lever, not about giving the Commission a blank cheque.

### 4. Factor four: secretariat and staff

Article 324(6) obliges the President or the Governor of a State, **when so requested by the Election Commission**, to make available such staff as may be necessary for discharging the Commission's clause (1) functions. That is a duty, and it is the constitutional root of the Commission's command over district machinery during an election.

But the Commission's own permanent secretariat is comparatively small: Deputy Election Commissioners drawn from the civil services, with Secretaries, Joint Secretaries, Deputy Secretaries and Under Secretaries in the secretariat. Dependence on deputation cuts both ways. It gives the Commission experienced administrators; it also means careers continue in the parent cadre after the deputation ends. That is the structural argument for a permanent secretariat with its own cadre.

### 5. Factor five: collegiality — and factor six: transparent enforcement

Collegiality is handled by section 18 and *T.N. Seshan*. The remaining factor is the one most within the Commission's own control and least discussed: **the visible consistency of enforcement**.

The point is practical. A Commission that acts fast but publishes neither the complaint, nor the response, nor the reasons, invites the inference that similar conduct by different parties is treated differently — an inference it then cannot rebut with evidence. The reform does not require new powers.

| Stage | Minimum public record |
|---|---|
| Complaint | Date, category of complainant, type of allegation |
| Preliminary view | Whether the matter is Model-Code-only or overlaps a statutory offence |
| Response | Notice issued and time allowed |
| Decision | The rule applied, the evidence, the reasons, and the members deciding |
| Follow-up | Compliance, referral for prosecution, or closure |
| Review | Comparative consistency across parties, States and elections |

This is the strongest near-term reform in the whole topic, because it requires **no constitutional amendment and no new statute** — only a published practice. Say that explicitly in an answer; examiners reward a reform that is implementable.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** All this talk of appointment is beside the point. The Commission has, over seven decades, conducted elections whose results have repeatedly removed incumbent governments. Performance is the real test, and it is passed.

**Reply.** The record is genuine and must be conceded; it is also the reason the institution's capital is worth protecting. But a performance argument is weak at exactly the moment it is needed. Independence that depends on the personal qualities of successive incumbents is not a design; it is luck. Design questions are about the worst case, not the average.

**Residual.** Neither side can be verified in advance. The honest conclusion is conditional: the Commission's operational capacity is not in doubt; the unresolved question is who selects those who exercise it, and that question is now before the Court.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: constitutional bodies and their independence; appointment to constitutional posts; accountability and institutional reform.

**Where this lesson is tested.** This is the single most likely Mains theme in the topic. It also supplies the status discipline that protects you from writing a sentence that has become false.

**Probable framings.** "The independence of the Election Commission depends more on the appointment process than on removal safeguards. Examine in the light of the 2023 Act."

**Traps.**

- Describing the 2023 Act as upheld, struck down or stayed.
- Describing a split verdict on referral as a verdict on validity.
- Inferring charged expenditure from salary parity.
- Converting the *Anoop Baranwal* appeal into a direction.
- Writing that the Act was passed "against the Supreme Court's order", when the order was expressly temporary.

**Answer use.** Use the product framing. List the six factors, identify which the Constitution secures, which the statute secures and which nothing secures, and reserve your verdict for the gap.

### Revision notes

1. Independence is a **product** of appointment, tenure, finance, secretariat, collegiality and transparent enforcement.
2. The challenge concentrates on **section 7** of Act 49 of 2023 and on executive predominance, not on parliamentary competence.
3. **22 March 2024**: interim interference declined; observations treated as tentative; concern recorded over circulation of full candidate details and fair deliberation.
4. **23 September 2026**: split verdict on **whether to refer** the challenge to a larger Bench; papers directed to be placed before the Chief Justice of India for a Constitution Bench. Status checked 2 October 2026.
5. Permitted formulation: the Act **operates while the challenge is pending**.
6. Article 324 contains **no charging clause**; contrast **Article 322** for Public Service Commissions.
7. ECI establishment expenditure is presented as a **demand for grants** within the Ministry of Law and Justice demands, and is votable.
8. *Anoop Baranwal* made a **fervent appeal**, not a direction, for a permanent secretariat and charged expenditure.
9. Article 324(6): staff **shall** be made available when requested — a duty, not a courtesy.
10. The Commission's own secretariat is small and deputation-based; Deputy Election Commissioners come from the civil services.
11. Removal reform: equalise protection, or retain the CEC-recommendation rule with inquiry, notice and reasons.
12. The most implementable reform is a published complaint-to-outcome record requiring no amendment.
13. Performance arguments cannot answer design questions, because design is about the worst case.

### Concept check

**Question:** In an answer written in October 2026, a candidate writes: "Since the Supreme Court's split verdict, the 2023 Act stands partly invalidated, and the Chief Justice of India has been restored to the selection panel." Correct the sentence and state the safe formulation.

**Model answer:** Both assertions are wrong. A split verdict delivered on 23 September 2026 concerned only the **preliminary question of reference to a larger Bench**; it decided nothing about validity, and a split cannot partly invalidate a statute. Nor has the Chief Justice been restored: the *Anoop Baranwal* arrangement was expressly temporary, operative only until Parliament made a law, and section 7 of Act 49 of 2023 replaced it. The safe formulation is: the Act continues to operate; the challenge to section 7 is pending; interim interference was declined on 22 March 2024; and the papers have been directed to be placed before the Chief Justice of India for the constitution of a larger Bench.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading procedural orders as merits outcomes. Refusal of interim relief, a reference order and a split on reference are all stages, not decisions on constitutionality.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** "The independence of the Election Commission of India depends more on its appointment process than on its removal safeguards." Examine. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (249 words):**
The proposition is substantially correct, though removal safeguards remain necessary.

**Why removal protection is not enough.** Article 324(5) protects the Chief Election Commissioner with Supreme Court judge removal and bars adverse variation of his service conditions; other Election Commissioners and Regional Commissioners are removable only on the CEC's recommendation, and section 11 of Act 49 of 2023 restates this. These provisions defeat one threat — dismissal of an inconvenient incumbent — but they operate only after a person is in office. They cannot affect who enters it.

**Why appointment is decisive.** For over seven decades Article 324(2)'s contemplated law did not exist, leaving selection to executive practice. *Anoop Baranwal* (2 March 2023) supplied an interim committee of the Prime Minister, the Leader of Opposition and the Chief Justice of India, expressly until Parliament legislated. Section 7 of Act 49 of 2023 substituted a Prime Minister-nominated Union Cabinet Minister for the Chief Justice, so two of three members are drawn from the governing executive. Section 8 requires transparent self-regulated procedure but prescribes no criteria, dossier time or recorded reasons.

**Qualification.** Appointment is necessary but not sufficient. Independence also depends on finance — Article 324 has no analogue of Article 322 — on a deputation-based secretariat, and on visibly consistent enforcement.

**Status discipline.** The challenge to section 7 is pending; the Act operates and must not be described as upheld or struck down.

**Verdict.** Secure appointment through published criteria and recorded reasons; equalise removal with an inquiry safeguard; publish complaint outcomes.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** This question punishes stale writing. Deduct for any script that states an outcome in the pending challenge; reserve the top band for answers that treat appointment as necessary-but-not-sufficient and name at least one non-appointment factor with its constitutional reference.

### Why the next lesson follows

Independence is about who commands. The next question is what is being commanded: the actual machinery that prepares rolls, receives nominations, runs polling stations and counts votes — almost none of which belongs permanently to the Commission.

---
## Lesson 8: The Election Machine — Officers, Staff and Observers

Progress: 8 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The statutory field machinery, deemed deputation, observers and the limits of each office

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — RPA 1950 Part IIA officer provisions and Laxmikanth Chapter 42 machinery section
CA search: "Election Commission observers deployment framework"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: one constitutional body, a borrowed army

```text
  ELECTION COMMISSION OF INDIA  (Art 324; Nirvachan Sadan)
        |  Deputy Election Commissioners, Secretaries and
        |  secretariat staff
        v
  CHIEF ELECTORAL OFFICER of each State / UT     [RPA 1950, s.13A]
        |
        v
  DISTRICT ELECTION OFFICER                      [RPA 1950, s.13AA]
        |
        +-------------------------------+
        |                               |
  RETURNING OFFICER            ELECTORAL REGISTRATION OFFICER
  (candidature, poll,          (the ROLL: inclusion, correction,
   count, declaration)          deletion)         [RPA 1950, s.13B]
        |                               |
  PRESIDING OFFICER                ASSISTANT ERO   [RPA 1950, s.13C]
  and polling parties

  Running alongside, reporting directly to the Commission:
      GENERAL, POLICE, EXPENDITURE and MICRO OBSERVERS
```

Two chains, not one. The **roll chain** ends at the Electoral Registration Officer; the **poll chain** ends at the Presiding Officer. Mixing them produces the common error of saying a Returning Officer deleted a name from the roll.

### 1. The problem this machinery solves

The Commission has, at its own headquarters, a few hundred officers. A general election needs roughly a million polling stations staffed by several million personnel, secured by central and State forces, using buildings and vehicles the Commission does not own.

There were three possible designs. Build a permanent election service — prohibitively expensive and idle between elections. Let State governments run their own elections — the problem Article 324 exists to prevent. Or borrow the existing administration and change who commands it during the election period. India chose the third, and nearly every operational controversy in Indian elections is a consequence of that choice.

### 2. The statutory offices, with their exact sources

Part IIA of the Representation of the People Act, 1950 is titled "Officers", and it creates the field hierarchy:

| Section | Office | Core function |
|---|---|---|
| 13A | **Chief Electoral Officer** | Supervises the preparation, revision and correction of electoral rolls in the State or Union territory, subject to the Commission's superintendence, direction and control |
| 13AA | **District Election Officer** | Coordinates and supervises all election work in the district, subject to the Commission's superintendence |
| 13B | **Electoral Registration Officer** | The statutory authority for the electoral roll of a constituency — inclusion, correction, transposition and deletion |
| 13C | **Assistant Electoral Registration Officer** | Assists the ERO; acts subject to the ERO's control |
| 13CC | **Deemed deputation** | Officers under sections 13A, 13AA, 13B and 13C are **deemed to be on deputation to the Election Commission** for the period of their election duties |

Section **13CC** is the one to underline. During their election functions these officers are deemed on deputation to the Commission, and during that period they are subject to the Commission's **control, superintendence and discipline**. That is how a State government's own officer becomes, for the duration, answerable elsewhere — and it is the statutory backing for the Commission's power to seek transfers and to direct disciplinary action during the election period.

The **Returning Officer** and **Presiding Officer** belong to the poll chain and are provided for under the Representation of the People Act, 1951 and the Conduct of Elections Rules. In practice the District Election Officer is usually the District Magistrate or Collector, and the Returning Officer for a constituency is an officer of government or a local authority designated for that constituency.

### 3. Why the roll chain and the poll chain are kept separate

```text
  ROLL CHAIN                          POLL CHAIN
  ----------------------------        ----------------------------
  Electoral Registration Officer      Returning Officer
  decides WHO IS AN ELECTOR           decides WHOSE NOMINATION IS VALID,
                                      conducts the poll and count,
                                      and DECLARES the result
       |                                   |
  appeal to the Chief Electoral       no appeal; challenge lies by
  Officer (RPA 1950, s.24)            ELECTION PETITION after the result
                                      (Art 329(b); RPA 1951)
```

The separation is deliberate. Roll decisions affect individuals and are reversible before the poll, so they get an administrative appeal. Candidature and result decisions affect the composition of a legislature, so they are frozen during the process and tested afterwards in court. Once you see that logic, you will never route a roll grievance to an election petition or a nomination rejection to the Chief Electoral Officer.

### 4. Observers: an information channel, not a parallel authority

The Commission deploys observers who report directly to it, outside the ordinary district hierarchy. The statutory anchor is in the Representation of the People Act, 1951, supplemented by detailed Commission instructions.

| Observer | Primary lens | Typical output |
|---|---|---|
| General observer | Overall conduct and level playing field | Reports, corrective recommendations, verification of critical processes |
| Police observer | Security, vulnerability mapping, force deployment | Security assessment and deployment advice |
| Expenditure observer | Candidate spending, surveillance teams, accounting | Shadow observation register, discrepancy notices |
| Micro observer | Process at a specific critical polling station | Direct poll-day report on that station |
| Awareness observer | Voter awareness and participation activity | Reports on outreach and accessibility |

The analytical value of observers is informational. Without them, the Commission would learn about a district only through the district's own officers — the very people whose neutrality may be in question. The observer closes that information asymmetry.

The limit must be stated with equal firmness: an **observer does not replace the Returning Officer**, does not decide a nomination, does not declare a result, and does not adjudicate an election petition. His power is to report, to recommend, and in specified situations to require that a process be stopped and referred to the Commission. Authority remains with the statutory officer; the observer supplies the Commission's eyes.

### 5. Example with its limit: the transfer power

A widely cited instance of the Commission's operational autonomy is its practice, during the election period, of directing transfers of officers connected with election work — typically those posted in their home districts, those who have completed a long stay in a district, or those against whom complaints of partisanship are substantiated.

What makes it lawful? The combination of Article 324's superintendence, direction and control; section 13CC's deemed deputation and attendant disciplinary control; and Article 324(6)'s obligation on the President and Governors to supply staff on request.

Where does the example stop? It stops at the ordinary civil-service relationship. The Commission is not the appointing authority of these officers, cannot dismiss them, and cannot direct transfers unrelated to election work or outside the election period. The power is functional and time-bound, not a general authority over State administration. An answer that cites the transfer power as proof of plenary authority over the civil service has overdrawn it.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** A borrowed administration cannot be neutral. The officer who is on deemed deputation for six weeks knows that for the remaining forty-six weeks his posting, promotion and transfer depend on the State government.

**Reply.** That is precisely why the design adds three correctives: the observer system, which reports outside the district chain; the transfer power, which removes officers whose neutrality is compromised; and written, published instructions, which reduce discretion at the field level and make deviation visible.

**Residual.** The correctives reduce the problem without dissolving it, because they operate on individuals rather than on incentives. The structural proposals — a dedicated election cadre, or statutory protection for officers who resist improper instructions during the election period — would address incentives, but each carries a cost in money, capacity or rigidity. Present both sides; this is a genuine open question, not a settled failure.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: appointment and functioning of constitutional bodies; GS-II governance: role of civil services in a democracy.

**Where this lesson is tested.** The officer hierarchy with section numbers is objective material; the borrowed-administration argument is high-value Mains content and connects this topic to civil-service neutrality.

**Probable framings.** "The credibility of Indian elections rests on the temporary reallocation of command over the permanent administration. Discuss."

**Traps.**

- Saying the Returning Officer prepares or corrects the electoral roll.
- Saying an observer can cancel a poll or decide a nomination.
- Saying the Chief Electoral Officer is appointed by the State government alone.
- Saying the Commission can dismiss a State officer.
- Confusing a Regional Commissioner under Article 324(4) with the Chief Electoral Officer under section 13A.

**Answer use.** When a question asks "how does the ECI actually enforce anything?", the answer is this lesson, not Article 324 alone. Name section 13CC; it converts an abstract claim about superintendence into a concrete mechanism.

### Revision notes

1. The Commission has a small permanent secretariat and borrows the field machinery.
2. Deputy Election Commissioners are drawn from the civil services and work in the Commission's secretariat.
3. **RPA 1950, section 13A** — Chief Electoral Officer of a State or Union territory.
4. **Section 13AA** — District Election Officer; usually the District Magistrate or Collector.
5. **Section 13B** — Electoral Registration Officer, the statutory authority for the roll.
6. **Section 13C** — Assistant Electoral Registration Officer.
7. **Section 13CC** — these officers are **deemed to be on deputation to the Election Commission** during election work, and subject to its control, superintendence and discipline.
8. Returning Officers and Presiding Officers belong to the poll chain under the RPA 1951 and the rules.
9. Roll grievances: administrative appeal to the Chief Electoral Officer under **RPA 1950, section 24**.
10. Candidature and result grievances: **election petition** after the result, under Article 329(b).
11. Observers — general, police, expenditure, micro and awareness — report directly to the Commission.
12. An observer reports and recommends; he does not replace the Returning Officer.
13. The transfer power rests on Article 324 plus section 13CC plus Article 324(6); it is functional and time-bound.
14. The structural residual is incentives: officers' careers remain with the parent government.

### Concept check

**Question:** Ten days before polling, the Commission directs the transfer of a Superintendent of Police posted in his home district, and in the same week an observer sends a report recommending that a Returning Officer's acceptance of a particular nomination be reversed. Which action is within the system's design, and which is not?

**Model answer:** The transfer is within the design. Officers deployed on election work fall under the Commission's superintendence, direction and control under Article 324, are deemed on deputation to the Commission under **section 13CC of the RPA 1950** with attendant disciplinary control, and Article 324(6) obliges the State to make staff available — so a time-bound, election-related transfer is a lawful exercise of command. The observer's recommendation is not an operative act: an observer reports and recommends, he does not decide, and scrutiny of nominations is the Returning Officer's statutory function. If the acceptance was improper, the remedy is an election petition after the result, since Article 329(b) bars calling the election in question midway.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating an observer's report as a decision. Observers exist to close the Commission's information gap, not to displace statutory officers.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** Explain how the Election Commission of India exercises operational command over officials who are not its own employees. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (148 words):**
Command is assembled from three sources rather than from ownership of staff. First, **Article 324(1)** vests superintendence, direction and control of rolls and elections in the Commission, and **Article 324(6)** obliges the President or a Governor, when so requested, to make staff available — a duty, not a favour. Second, **Part IIA of the Representation of the People Act, 1950** creates the statutory field offices: the Chief Electoral Officer (section 13A), the District Election Officer (section 13AA), the Electoral Registration Officer (section 13B) and his assistants (section 13C); **section 13CC** deems these officers to be on deputation to the Commission during election work, with its control, superintendence and discipline. Third, observers reporting directly to the Commission supply information outside the district chain. Together these permit binding instructions, replacement or transfer of compromised officers, and verification. The command is functional and time-bound, not a general authority over State administration.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The deciding element is section 13CC. A script that cites only Article 324 explains the authority but not the mechanism, and should sit a band lower than one that names the deemed-deputation provision and the time-bound limit.

### Why the next lesson follows

We have repeatedly referred to "the RPA 1950" and "the RPA 1951" as if the distinction were obvious. It is not, and it is the most profitable distinction in this topic — one statute builds the electorate and the map, the other runs the contest and settles its disputes.

---

## Lesson 9: Two Statutes, Two Jobs — RPA 1950 against RPA 1951

Progress: 9 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: What each Act owns, why Parliament enacted two, and the citation errors that cost marks

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — arrangement of sections of the RPA 1950 read in full; RPA 1951 scheme cross-checked
CA search: "Representation of the People Act 1950 1951 distinction"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: two Acts, two stages of the same election

```text
  BEFORE THERE IS A CONTEST              ONCE THERE IS A CONTEST
  --------------------------------       --------------------------------
  RPA 1950  (Act 43 of 1950)             RPA 1951  (Act 43 of 1951)
  12 May 1950                            assented 17 July 1951

  . allocation of seats                  . notification of elections
  . delimitation framework and           . qualifications and
    consolidation of orders                disqualifications of
  . election officers (Part IIA)           members
  . ELECTORAL ROLLS: who is an           . registration of political
    elector, and how the roll is           parties (s.29A)
    made, revised and corrected          . nominations, scrutiny,
  . appeals on roll entries                withdrawal, poll and count
  . bar on civil-court jurisdiction      . election expenses and accounts
    over roll questions                  . CORRUPT PRACTICES and
                                           electoral offences
                                         . ELECTION PETITIONS and appeals

  Mnemonic:  1950 BUILDS THE ELECTORATE.  1951 RUNS THE ELECTION.
```

### 1. Why two Acts at all?

Because the two jobs are separated in time and in purpose.

In 1950 the Constitution had just come into force, the first general election had not been held, and the country needed to know how many seats each State had, how constituencies would be drawn, who would hold the pen on the rolls, and who was entitled to be on them. None of that is about a contest; all of it must exist before a contest is possible. Hence the **Representation of the People Act, 1950** — an Act to provide for allocation of seats, delimitation of constituencies, the qualifications of voters and the preparation of electoral rolls.

Only then could Parliament write the rules of the contest itself: how an election is notified, who may stand, what conduct is corrupt, how expenses are accounted for, and how a result is challenged. Hence the **Representation of the People Act, 1951**.

The chronology is itself the mnemonic. First you decide the constituency and the electorate; then you hold the election.

### 2. The ownership table you should be able to reproduce

| Subject | Owning Act | Representative provisions |
|---|---|---|
| Allocation of seats in the House of the People and Assemblies | 1950 | Sections 3, 4, 7 |
| Consolidation of delimitation orders; keeping them up to date | 1950 | Sections 8, 9 |
| Number of seats reserved for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes | 1950 | Section 7(1A)–(1C) and the Second Schedule (sections 9A and 9B, once used for identifying ST-reserved constituencies, stand omitted; that identification is now a Delimitation Commission function) |
| Election officers | 1950 | Sections 13A, 13AA, 13B, 13C, 13CC |
| Who may be registered as an elector | 1950 | Sections 15 to 20 |
| Preparation, revision and special revision of rolls | 1950 | Section 21 |
| Correction, inclusion and appeals | 1950 | Sections 22, 23, 24 |
| Bar on civil-court jurisdiction over roll entitlement | 1950 | Section 30 |
| Notification of general elections and by-elections | 1951 | Part III (sections 14 to 16), with casual vacancies dealt with separately in the Act |
| Qualifications and disqualifications of members | 1951 | Part II (sections 3 to 11B) |
| Registration of political parties | 1951 | Section 29A |
| Nominations, scrutiny, withdrawal, poll and counting | 1951 | Part V (sections 30 onwards) |
| Candidature in more than one constituency | 1951 | Sections 33(7) and 70 |
| Election expenses and lodging of accounts | 1951 | Sections 77, 78 and 10A |
| Corrupt practices | 1951 | Section 123 |
| Exit-poll restriction during the notified period | 1951 | Section 126A |
| Election petitions, void grounds, appeal | 1951 | Sections 80, 80A, 81, 83, 86, 98, 99, 100, 116A |

You do not need every number. You do need the **assignment**: if the issue concerns the roll or the map, it is 1950; if it concerns the contest, the candidate or the dispute, it is 1951.

### 3. The three citation errors that cost marks

```text
WRONG                                   RIGHT
-----------------------------------     -----------------------------------
"Disqualification of a candidate        RPA 1951, sections 8 to 11B
 under the RPA 1950"

"Election petitions under the           RPA 1951, sections 80 to 116A
 RPA 1950"

"Electoral rolls are prepared under     RPA 1950, sections 15 to 25;
 the RPA 1951"                          Article 325 supplies the equality
                                        guarantee
```

The first two errors are the most damaging, because questions on disqualification and on election disputes name the Act of 1951 in the question itself. Citing the wrong Act in an answer to a question that states the right one is an avoidable self-inflicted wound.

### 4. Where the two Acts meet

They are not sealed from each other. Three junctions are worth knowing.

**Junction one: the elector and the candidate.** Section 4 and section 5 of the **1951** Act require, for membership of the House of the People and of a State Legislative Assembly respectively, that the person be an **elector for a parliamentary or Assembly constituency** as the case may be. Entitlement built under the 1950 Act therefore becomes a qualification under the 1951 Act.

**Junction two: roll disqualification and electoral offence.** Section 16 of the **1950** Act disqualifies from registration a person who is not a citizen, who is of unsound mind and so declared by a competent court, or who is for the time being disqualified from voting under a law relating to corrupt practices and other offences in connection with elections. The last limb reaches into the 1951 Act's world.

**Junction three: delimitation.** The 1950 Act carries the allocation and consolidation framework, while actual delimitation exercises are conducted by Commissions constituted under separate Delimitation Acts. Lesson 17 untangles that.

### 5. A comparison treated as an argument

Why did Parliament not simply enact one consolidated election code?

Two reasons, and both are defensible in an answer. First, **amendment frequency differs**. Seat allocation and roll machinery change rarely and are tied to census and delimitation cycles; conduct rules, expenditure ceilings and offence provisions change far more often. Separate statutes let Parliament amend the volatile parts without reopening the structural ones.

Second, **the audiences differ**. The 1950 Act is addressed largely to administrators building a roll; the 1951 Act is addressed to candidates, parties, voters and courts. A single code would mix an administrative manual with a penal and procedural code.

The cost of the choice is the one we have been managing all lesson: two Acts of the same name and almost the same year, which examinees confuse.

### 6. Objection and reply

**Objection.** This is pure memorisation. Nothing turns on which Act a provision sits in.

**Reply.** Something does turn on it, and it is jurisdictional. Section 30 of the 1950 Act bars civil courts from entertaining the question whether a person is entitled to be registered in a roll, and the roll remedy is an administrative appeal to the Chief Electoral Officer under section 24. Article 329(b) and sections 80 and 80A of the 1951 Act route election disputes to the High Court. Two different statutes, two different forums, two different time windows. Knowing which Act you are in tells you where to go — and in an answer, it tells the examiner you understand the architecture rather than the list.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: statutory bodies and legislation implementing constitutional provisions; electoral reform.

**Where this lesson is tested.** Every one of the routed Mains questions on disqualification, corrupt practices and election disputes names the **1951** Act. The 1950 Act underpins the roll questions. The distinction is also prime objective material.

**Probable framings.** It rarely forms a question by itself; it forms the first line of a correct answer to several questions.

**Traps.**

- Citing the 1950 Act for disqualification, corrupt practices or election petitions.
- Citing the 1951 Act for the preparation of electoral rolls.
- Assuming the Delimitation Commission is constituted under the RPA 1950.
- Forgetting that being an elector is itself a statutory qualification for membership.

**Answer use.** Open any RPA answer with one sentence of allocation — "the rolls are governed by the Act of 1950, the conduct of and disputes about elections by the Act of 1951" — and then stay inside the correct statute.

### Revision notes

1. **RPA 1950** (Act 43 of 1950, 12 May 1950): allocation of seats, delimitation framework, election officers, electoral rolls, appeals, bar on civil courts.
2. **RPA 1951** (Act 43 of 1951): notification, qualifications and disqualifications, party registration, conduct of elections, expenses, corrupt practices, offences, election petitions.
3. Mnemonic: **1950 builds the electorate; 1951 runs the election**.
4. Officers sit in **Part IIA of the 1950 Act**, sections 13A to 13CC.
5. Rolls: sections 15 to 25 of the 1950 Act; special revision in section 21(3).
6. Roll appeal to the Chief Electoral Officer: section 24 of the 1950 Act.
7. Civil courts barred on roll entitlement: section 30 of the 1950 Act.
8. Party registration: section 29A of the **1951** Act.
9. Corrupt practices: section 123 of the **1951** Act.
10. Election petitions: sections 80 to 116A of the **1951** Act.
11. Being an elector is a statutory qualification for membership under the 1951 Act.
12. Section 16 of the 1950 Act links roll disqualification to corrupt-practice disqualification.
13. Two Acts because amendment cycles and audiences differ.
14. The practical payoff is jurisdictional: different forums and different time windows.

### Concept check

**Question:** A voter's name is deleted from the roll; a defeated candidate alleges that a rival distributed money on polling day. State which Act governs each grievance, the forum, and the timing.

**Model answer:** The deletion is a **roll** question under the **Representation of the People Act, 1950**. The Electoral Registration Officer acts under section 22, which requires an opportunity to be heard before deletion on the stated grounds, and an appeal lies to the Chief Electoral Officer under section 24; section 30 bars a civil court from entertaining the question of entitlement to registration. The remedy is available before the poll. The bribery allegation is a **corrupt practice** under **section 123 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951**, which can be pursued only by an election petition before the High Court after the result, because Article 329(b) permits an election to be called in question only in that way.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that anything "about an election" goes to a court immediately. Roll grievances have an administrative route available in real time; conduct grievances have a judicial route available only afterwards.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** "The division of Indian election law between the Acts of 1950 and 1951 is an administrative convenience with jurisdictional consequences." Comment. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (147 words):**
The division began as convenience and hardened into structure. The Representation of the People Act, 1950 was enacted before the first general election to allocate seats, provide the delimitation framework, create election officers and, above all, build and maintain electoral rolls; the Act of 1951 then supplied the rules of the contest — notification, qualifications and disqualifications, party registration, conduct, expenses, corrupt practices and election petitions. Separation let Parliament amend volatile conduct rules without reopening structural provisions. The consequences are jurisdictional. A roll grievance is decided by the Electoral Registration Officer with an appeal to the Chief Electoral Officer under section 24 of the 1950 Act, and section 30 ousts the civil court; a challenge to an election proceeds only by election petition before the High Court under sections 80 and 80A of the 1951 Act, read with Article 329(b). Forum and timing both follow the statute.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The directive is "comment", so a correct allocation table alone is insufficient. Award the top band only where the script converts the division into a forum-and-timing consequence, naming at least one provision from each Act.

### Why the next lesson follows

The 1950 Act's central subject is the roll. Who is entitled to be on it, what "ordinarily resident" means, when entitlement is tested, and how identity is now verified — those are the questions that decide whether the equality promised by Article 325 is real.

---
## Lesson 10: Who Gets on the Roll — Entitlement, Residence, Overseas Electors and the Qualifying Date

Progress: 10 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Article 325 in operation, RPA 1950 sections 15 to 20A, overseas electors, the four qualifying dates and the Aadhaar provisions

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — RPA 1950 sections 14 to 20A and the Election Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021 read in enacted text, together with the Commission's published overseas-elector enrolment procedure
CA search: "electoral roll entitlement qualifying date Aadhaar 2026"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson; the roll-revision litigation is placed in Lesson 11
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: the entitlement test, applied in order

```text
  STEP 1   Is there a roll for this constituency?
           s.15: every constituency shall have an electoral roll,
           prepared under the ECI's superintendence, direction
           and control
                      |
  STEP 2   Is the person DISQUALIFIED for registration?   [s.16]
             (a) not a citizen of India               -> disqualified
             (b) of unsound mind and so declared by
                 a competent court                    -> disqualified
             (c) for the time being disqualified from
                 voting under a law on corrupt
                 practices and election offences      -> disqualified
                      |
  STEP 3   Does the person satisfy the CONDITIONS?        [s.19]
             (a) not less than 18 years of age on the
                 QUALIFYING DATE
             (b) ORDINARILY RESIDENT in the constituency
                      |
  STEP 4   Duplication checks
             s.17: not in more than one constituency
             s.18: not more than once in any constituency
                      |
                 ENTITLED TO BE REGISTERED
```

Work downward, never sideways. Section 16 is a **disqualification** filter; section 19 is a **qualification** test; sections 17 and 18 are integrity rules. A question that asks "is X entitled?" is answered by running all four steps, in that order.

### 1. Article 325 in operation

Lesson 2 established the rule: one general roll per constituency, and no exclusion or claim to a special roll **on grounds only of** religion, race, caste or sex. Section 15 of the RPA 1950 is its operational counterpart — for every constituency there **shall be** an electoral roll prepared in accordance with the Act under the superintendence, direction and control of the Election Commission.

Put the two together and you get the governing proposition of this lesson: **universality is constitutional; eligibility is statutory**. Article 325 removes identity-based exclusion; the Act of 1950 then states citizenship, age, residence and sanity conditions that apply to everyone equally.

### 2. Section 19, and the two conditions that do the work

Section 19 provides that, subject to the preceding provisions, every person who **(a) is not less than eighteen years of age on the qualifying date** and **(b) is ordinarily resident in a constituency** shall be entitled to be registered in the electoral roll for that constituency.

Note that citizenship appears not here but in section 16(1)(a), as a disqualification. The drafting matters: a non-citizen is not someone who fails a condition of registration, but someone expressly disqualified from being registered at all.

The age requirement traces to Article 326 and was **lowered from twenty-one to eighteen by the Constitution (Sixty-first Amendment) Act, 1988**; section 19(a) was correspondingly amended in 1989.

### 3. "Ordinarily resident": a term with four statutory rules

Section 20 does not define ordinary residence positively. It does something more useful for examinations: it settles four recurring doubts.

| Situation | Statutory rule | Section |
|---|---|---|
| A person owns or possesses a dwelling house in a constituency | That **alone** does not make him ordinarily resident there | 20(1) |
| A person is temporarily absent from his place of ordinary residence | He does **not** thereby cease to be ordinarily resident there | 20(1A) |
| A sitting MP or MLA is absent from his constituency on duties as such member | He does **not** thereby cease to be ordinarily resident in the constituency where he was registered at the time of his election | 20(1B) |
| A person is a patient in an establishment for mental illness, or is detained in prison or other legal custody | He is **not** by reason of that deemed ordinarily resident there | 20(2) |
| A person has a **service qualification** | He is deemed ordinarily resident in the constituency where he would have been but for that qualification | 20(3) |
| A person holds an office declared by the President in consultation with the ECI | He is deemed ordinarily resident as provided | 20(4) |

Three of these are genuinely analytical.

**Owning a house is not residence (20(1)).** This forecloses the purchase of an address to gain a vote in a chosen constituency.

**Temporary absence does not break residence (20(1A)).** This protects students, migrant workers and travellers, and it is the provision to cite whenever a roll exercise is criticised for deleting names on the ground of absence at the time of a field visit.

**Service voters (20(3)).** Members of the armed forces, members of a force to which the Army Act provisions are applied, persons employed under the Government of India in a post outside India, and specified others are deemed ordinarily resident at their home constituency. This is why the service-voter and postal-ballot machinery exists: the entitlement is anchored at home, but the person is elsewhere. The **Election Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021** amended section 20(6) to replace "wife" with "spouse", making the service-voter provision gender-neutral, with a corresponding change in section 60(b)(ii) of the RPA 1951.

### 4. The one statutory exception to ordinary residence: overseas electors

Section 20 works entirely inside India. It tells you when a person who lives somewhere in India counts as ordinarily resident in a particular constituency. It has nothing to say about a citizen who lives in Dubai, Toronto or Nairobi and whose ordinary residence is, on any honest reading, not in India at all.

Parliament dealt with that gap separately, by inserting **section 20A** into the Representation of the People Act, 1950 in 2010. The provision does not relax the residence test; it **substitutes a documentary proxy** for it.

```text
  ORDINARY RESIDENCE TEST            SECTION 20A SUBSTITUTE TEST
  (s.19(b) + s.20)                   (overseas electors)
  ----------------------             ---------------------------
  where the person actually          the place of residence IN INDIA
  and habitually lives               entered in the person's PASSPORT
            |                                     |
  established by enquiry             established by the passport entry,
  and field verification             verified through the BLO at that
            |                        address or the Indian Mission
            |                                     |
            +-----------------+-------------------+
                              |
                  REGISTRATION IN ONE CONSTITUENCY
                              |
                  franchise exercised under the
                  ordinary law for that constituency
```

Who qualifies. Section 20A entitles a citizen of India who satisfies three conditions together:

1. his name is **not already included** in an electoral roll;
2. he has **not acquired the citizenship of any other country**; and
3. he is **absent from his place of ordinary residence in India** owing to employment, education or otherwise outside India, whether temporarily or not.

Where he is registered. In the electoral roll of the constituency in which **the place of residence in India entered in his passport** is located. The time within which, and the manner in which, such registration happens is left to rules.

Notice that none of the three conditions is arbitrary. Condition (2) exists because section 16(1)(a) disqualifies a non-citizen from registration altogether, so acquiring another citizenship ends the entitlement at its root. Condition (1) exists because sections 17 and 18 forbid registration in more than one constituency and more than once in any constituency; an overseas elector is being given *a* registration, not a second one.

How it works in practice. Application is made on the prescribed overseas-elector form with self-attested copies of the relevant passport pages, including the page carrying the address in India and the page carrying the valid visa endorsement. Verification runs through the Booth Level Officer at the passport address and, where no relative is available to verify, through the concerned Indian Mission. Overseas electors are carried in a **separate section at the end of the roll** for the polling-station area, and no elector photo identity card is issued to them; the passport itself is the identity document.

Now the limit, stated carefully, because this is where answers routinely overclaim:

- What section 20A creates is a **registration entitlement** plus the ordinary franchise that registration carries. An overseas elector exercises that franchise **in person**, at the polling station of the constituency in which he is registered.
- There is at present **no postal, proxy, online or remote voting facility** for overseas electors in law. A legislative proposal to permit proxy voting for them was brought in 2017 and did not become law, and a proposal to extend the electronically transmitted postal-ballot facility to overseas electors has been discussed but has not been given effect. Treat every one of these as a **proposal**, never as an entitlement.
- The same caution governs the adjacent problem of **internal** migration. The Commission developed and demonstrated a prototype multi-constituency remote voting machine to political parties in January 2023, precisely to address domestic migrants who cannot travel home to vote. It has remained a **prototype under consultation**: it would require legislative, administrative and technological change, and it has not been used in any election. Write it as a proposal with a named obstacle, and you are safe; write it as a facility, and you are wrong.

The analytical payoff is worth one line in any roll answer. Indian electoral law anchors the vote to a **place**, and the two great exceptions — the service voter under section 20(3) and the overseas elector under section 20A — are both devices for keeping that anchor when the person has physically left. Each solves the registration problem; neither solves the problem of actually reaching a polling booth, which is why migration remains the largest untreated participation gap in the system.

### 5. The qualifying date: one date became four

Before 2022, section 14(b) of the RPA 1950 defined "qualifying date", in relation to preparation or revision of a roll, as **the 1st day of January** of the year in which it is prepared or revised. A person turning eighteen on 2 January therefore waited for the following year's revision.

The **Election Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021 (Act No. 49 of 2021)**, which received assent on **29 December 2021**, substituted in section 14(b) the words "the 1st day of January" with "**the 1st day of January, the 1st day of April, the 1st day of July and the 1st day of October**".

```text
  BEFORE                     AFTER (Act 49 of 2021)
  ------------------         ----------------------------------
  one qualifying date        four qualifying dates in a year
  1 January                  1 Jan | 1 Apr | 1 Jul | 1 Oct
       |                              |
  worst case: a person       worst case: a wait of up to
  turning 18 on 2 Jan        roughly three months
  waits almost a year
```

The reform is small in text and large in effect: it reduces the maximum exclusion period for a newly eligible adult from about twelve months to about three.

Be careful with two adjacent numbers. **Act 49 of 2021** is the Election Laws (Amendment) Act; **Act 49 of 2023** is the appointment and service Act for the Commission. Same number, different years, different subjects.

### 6. Aadhaar: what the 2021 Act actually permits

This is a provision widely misreported, so read the inserted sub-sections of section 23 of the RPA 1950:

- **Section 23(4)**: the Electoral Registration Officer **may, for the purpose of establishing the identity of any person, require that such person may furnish the Aadhaar number**; a proviso allows him to require the Aadhaar number **from persons already included in the roll** for the purposes of **authentication of entries** and to identify registration of the same person in more than one constituency or more than once in the same constituency.
- **Section 23(5)**: every person whose name is included in the roll **may intimate** his Aadhaar number to the prescribed authority, in the prescribed form and manner, on or before a date notified by the Central Government.
- **Section 23(6)**: **no application for inclusion shall be denied and no entries shall be deleted for inability to furnish or intimate the Aadhaar number due to such sufficient cause as may be prescribed**, with a proviso permitting such a person to furnish other prescribed alternate documents.

Section 5 of the same amending Act then inserted rule-making heads in section 28(2) of the RPA 1950 — clause (hhha) for the authority, form and manner of intimation, and clause (hhhb) for the sufficient cause and alternate documents.

Now the three propositions that follow, stated carefully:

1. The statute **permits** the Electoral Registration Officer to require an Aadhaar number for identity and for authentication of existing entries.
2. Section 23(5) uses the word "**may intimate**" for existing electors.
3. Section 23(6) is a **protective floor**: inability to furnish, for a prescribed sufficient cause, cannot by itself cause denial of inclusion or deletion of an entry, and alternate documents are contemplated.

```text
PERMITTED to write
  "The 2021 amendment allows the ERO to require Aadhaar for
   identity and for authenticating existing entries, subject to
   the section 23(6) safeguard and alternate documents."

NOT PERMITTED to write
  X  "Aadhaar is mandatory for voting."
  X  "Aadhaar linkage is a condition of remaining on the roll."
  X  "Non-furnishing of Aadhaar results in deletion."
```

The operational detail — which authority, which forms, which alternate documents, what counts as sufficient cause — is left to rules. Keep the Act and the rules in separate sentences; the Act creates the authority and the safeguard, the rules supply implementation.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Conditioning an entitlement on a document administered by another authority converts a constitutional promise of universal adult franchise into a documentation test, and falls hardest on exactly the people least likely to hold documents.

**Reply.** The Act anticipated this, which is why section 23(6) was drafted as a prohibition rather than a hope: inclusion cannot be denied, and an entry cannot be deleted, for inability to furnish Aadhaar for a prescribed sufficient cause, and alternate documents are expressly contemplated. Section 20(1A) independently protects the temporarily absent. Duplication across constituencies is a genuine integrity problem that sections 17 and 18 already prohibit and that authentication can detect.

**Residual.** The protection is only as strong as the rules that define "sufficient cause" and the alternate-document list, and as the field practice that applies them. Statutory safeguards and administrative outcomes are different things, and an answer should say that the first does not automatically produce the second.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: representation of the people; welfare and rights dimension of electoral participation; statutory implementation of constitutional guarantees.

**Where this lesson is tested.** The four qualifying dates and the Aadhaar safeguard are precise, recent and objective-friendly. Ordinary residence is the analytical content that distinguishes a good roll answer.

**Probable framings.** A statement question on qualifying dates, or a Mains question on inclusive and accurate electoral rolls.

**Traps.**

- "The qualifying date is 1 January." It has been four dates since the 2021 amendment.
- "Aadhaar is mandatory." It is not; section 23(6) protects against denial or deletion for inability with sufficient cause.
- "Owning a house in a constituency makes you a voter there." Section 20(1) says it does not.
- "A prisoner is ordinarily resident in the prison." Section 20(2) says he is not.
- Confusing **Act 49 of 2021** with **Act 49 of 2023**.
- Treating section 20A as creating a right for overseas electors to vote by post, proxy or remote machine. It creates a **registration** entitlement at the passport address; the vote is cast in person.
- Treating citizenship as a section 19 condition rather than a section 16 disqualification.

**Answer use.** For any roll question, begin with the four-step entitlement test. It demonstrates that you know the statute's internal logic and not merely a list of requirements.

### Revision notes

1. **Section 15**: every constituency shall have an electoral roll, prepared under the ECI's superintendence, direction and control.
2. **Section 16**: disqualified for registration — non-citizen; unsound mind so declared by a competent court; for the time being disqualified from voting under a corrupt-practices or election-offences law.
3. **Section 19**: entitled if **not less than eighteen years on the qualifying date** and **ordinarily resident** in the constituency.
4. **Sections 17 and 18**: no registration in more than one constituency, and not more than once in any constituency.
5. **Section 20(1)**: ownership or possession of a dwelling house alone is not ordinary residence.
6. **Section 20(1A)**: temporary absence does not end ordinary residence.
7. **Section 20(1B)**: a sitting MP or MLA absent on duties as such member does not cease to be ordinarily resident in his registered constituency.
8. **Section 20(2)**: a patient in a mental-illness establishment, or a person in prison or legal custody, is not ordinarily resident there by reason of that.
9. **Section 20(3)**: a person with a service qualification is deemed ordinarily resident at his home constituency.
10. **Act 49 of 2021**, assented **29 December 2021**, substituted four qualifying dates — **1 January, 1 April, 1 July, 1 October** — and made the service-voter spouse language gender-neutral in section 20(6) of the 1950 Act and section 60(b)(ii) of the 1951 Act.
11. **Section 23(4)**: the ERO may require an Aadhaar number for identity, and for authenticating existing entries and detecting duplicates; **section 23(5)**: existing electors **may intimate** their Aadhaar number.
12. **Section 23(6)**: no denial of inclusion and no deletion for inability to furnish Aadhaar for a prescribed sufficient cause; alternate documents contemplated.
13. Section 28(2)(hhha) and (hhhb) carry the rule-making heads for intimation and for sufficient cause and alternate documents.
14. **Section 20A** (inserted in 2010): an Indian citizen not already on a roll, who has not acquired another citizenship and is absent from his place of ordinary residence in India for employment, education or otherwise, is entitled to registration in the constituency of the **place of residence in India entered in his passport**; overseas electors appear in a separate section at the end of the roll and are identified by the passport.
15. An overseas elector votes **in person**. There is no postal, proxy, online or remote facility for him in law; a 2017 proxy-voting proposal did not become law, and the remote voting machine for domestic migrants remains a demonstrated **prototype**, not a facility.

### Concept check

**Question:** A twenty-two-year-old construction worker owns an inherited house in District A but has lived and worked in District B for three years, returning home for two months each year. He holds no Aadhaar number and says he cannot obtain one. In which constituency is he entitled to be registered, and can his application be refused for want of Aadhaar?

**Model answer:** Entitlement follows **ordinary residence** under section 19(b), not ownership: section 20(1) provides that owning or possessing a dwelling house in a constituency does not by itself make a person ordinarily resident there, so the inherited house in District A does not create entitlement. Three years of living and working in District B establish ordinary residence there, and his two-month annual return is a temporary absence that, by section 20(1A), does not break it. On Aadhaar, section 23(4) permits the Electoral Registration Officer to require the number, but **section 23(6)** forbids denial of an application for inability to furnish it due to a prescribed sufficient cause, and allows prescribed alternate documents. His application cannot be refused on that ground alone.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating property, domicile or family origin as the test. The statute uses ordinary residence, and expressly excludes house ownership as a sufficient basis.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** "An electoral roll must be simultaneously inclusive and accurate; the two objectives pull in opposite directions." Discuss with reference to the statutory framework governing entitlement. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (249 words):**
The tension is real, and Indian law manages it by placing each objective in a different set of provisions.

**Inclusion.** Article 325 forbids exclusion on grounds only of religion, race, caste or sex, and Article 326 establishes adult suffrage at eighteen after the Sixty-first Amendment, 1988. Section 19 of the Representation of the People Act, 1950 states only two conditions — age on the qualifying date and ordinary residence. Section 20 protects mobile populations: house ownership is not residence, temporary absence does not break it, and service voters are deemed resident at home. The Election Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021 added three further qualifying dates — 1 April, 1 July and 1 October — cutting the maximum wait for a newly eligible adult from about a year to about three months.

**Accuracy.** Section 16 disqualifies non-citizens, persons of unsound mind so declared, and those disqualified from voting under corrupt-practice law. Sections 17 and 18 forbid multiple registration. Section 23(4), inserted in 2021, permits the Electoral Registration Officer to require an Aadhaar number for identity and for authenticating existing entries.

**Where they pull apart.** Verification demands documents; inclusion demands that documentary incapacity not become disenfranchisement.

**How the statute reconciles them.** Section 23(6) bars denial of inclusion or deletion for inability to furnish Aadhaar for a prescribed sufficient cause, and allows alternate documents; section 22's proviso requires an opportunity to be heard before deletion.

**Verdict.** The reconciliation is procedural, so its success depends on rules and field practice, not on text alone.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The question asks about a tension, so a script that describes only entitlement or only verification cannot exceed the middle band. The top band requires the reconciling provisions — section 23(6) and the hearing requirement — to be named as the hinge.

### Why the next lesson follows

Entitlement tells us who should be on the roll. It does not tell us how the roll is actually built, revised and corrected — or what happens when the Commission orders an intensive revision and asks existing electors to prove their eligibility again.

---

## Lesson 11: Making and Correcting the Roll, and the Special Revision

Progress: 11 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: RPA 1950 sections 21 to 25 and 30, the special revision power, and the 2026 roll-revision judgment

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — RPA 1950 sections 21, 22, 23, 24, 25 and 30 read in enacted text
CA search: "Supreme Court Special Intensive Revision electoral rolls judgment"
CA found: a decided judgment, not a pending development; it is taught below as settled doctrine with its date, and the file's live anchor remains the appointment challenge in Lesson 7
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: three different revisions, and one process

```text
  ORDINARY ANNUAL REVISION          [s.21(2)]
      before each general election to the House of the People
      or a State Assembly, and in the prescribed year/manner
      PROVISO: failure to revise does not affect the validity
               or continued operation of the existing roll
                        |
  SPECIAL REVISION                  [s.21(3)]
      "the Election Commission may AT ANY TIME, for REASONS TO
       BE RECORDED, direct a special revision of the electoral
       roll for any constituency or part of a constituency
       IN SUCH MANNER AS IT MAY THINK FIT"
      PROVISO: the existing roll continues in force until the
               special revision is completed
                        |
  CONTINUOUS CORRECTION             [ss.22, 23]
      correction, transposition and deletion (s.22)
      inclusion on application (s.23)
                        |
  APPEAL                            [s.24]
      to the Chief Electoral Officer from an ERO's order
      under s.22 or s.23
                        |
  CIVIL COURT                       [s.30]  --- BARRED
```

### 1. Section 21: three powers, not one

Section 21(1) provides for the **initial preparation** of the roll for every constituency before each general election, in the prescribed manner and by reference to the qualifying date.

Section 21(2) provides for **revision** in the prescribed manner before each general election to the House of the People or to a State Legislative Assembly, and in other prescribed years, with a **proviso** that if the roll is not so revised, the **validity or continued operation of the existing roll is not thereby affected**. That proviso is practically important: an unrevised roll does not become void.

Section 21(3) is the provision that has carried most of the recent constitutional weight. Read it in full:

> Notwithstanding anything contained in sub-section (2), the Election Commission may at any time, for reasons to be recorded, direct a special revision of the electoral roll for any constituency or part of a constituency in such manner as it may think fit:
>
> Provided that subject to the other provisions of this Act, the electoral roll for the constituency, as in force at the time of the issue of any such direction, shall continue to be in force until the completion of the special revision so directed.

Four controls are built into those words: **at any time**; **for reasons to be recorded**; **for a constituency or part of one**; **in such manner as it may think fit**. And one protection: the **existing roll continues in force** until the special revision is complete, so nobody is left roll-less in the interim.

### 2. Section 22: correction, transposition and deletion — with a mandatory hearing

Section 22 empowers the Electoral Registration Officer, on application or on his own motion and after such inquiry as he thinks fit, to amend, transpose or delete an entry which:

- **(a)** is erroneous or defective in any particular;
- **(b)** should be transposed because the person has changed his place of ordinary residence **within** the constituency; or
- **(c)** should be deleted because the person is dead, has ceased to be ordinarily resident in the constituency, or is otherwise not entitled to be registered in that roll.

Then the safeguard, in the proviso: before taking action under clause (a) or (b), or action under clause (c) on the ground that the person has **ceased to be ordinarily resident** or is **otherwise not entitled**, the Electoral Registration Officer **shall give the person concerned a reasonable opportunity of being heard**.

Read the proviso carefully and you will notice an intelligent exclusion: a deletion on the ground that the person is **dead** does not require a hearing, for obvious reasons, while every other deletion ground does. That is the kind of textual precision examiners reward.

### 3. Sections 23, 24, 25 and 30: inclusion, appeal, fee and the ousted court

**Section 23(1)–(2)**: any person whose name is not included may apply to the Electoral Registration Officer, who shall direct inclusion if satisfied of entitlement; a proviso requires that where the applicant is registered elsewhere, the other constituency's Electoral Registration Officer be informed and shall strike off the name.

**Section 23(3)** carries a timing rule that candidates miss: no amendment, transposition or deletion under section 22, and no direction for inclusion under section 23, shall be made **after the last date for making nominations** for an election in that constituency (or in the parliamentary constituency comprising it) and **before the completion of that election**. The roll is frozen once the contest formally begins.

**Section 24**: an appeal lies, within the prescribed time and manner, **to the Chief Electoral Officer** from an order of the Electoral Registration Officer under section 22 or section 23.

**Section 25**: applications and appeals must be accompanied by the prescribed fee.

**Section 30**: no civil court has jurisdiction to entertain or adjudicate whether a person is or is not entitled to be registered in an electoral roll, or to question the legality of any action taken by or under the authority of an Electoral Registration Officer.

```text
  ROLL GRIEVANCE ROUTE
      ERO decision (s.22 / s.23)
            -> appeal to Chief Electoral Officer (s.24)
            -> writ jurisdiction remains for illegality,
               jurisdictional error or breach of natural justice
            X  no civil suit (s.30)
```

Section 30 ousts the **civil court**, not the constitutional courts. A writ petition alleging that a deletion was made without the hearing section 22 requires is not barred; it is not an adjudication of entitlement but a challenge to the legality of the process. State that distinction precisely — it is the difference between a correct answer and a confident error.

### 4. The special revision tested: the 2026 judgment

On **27 May 2026**, in ***Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India***, the Supreme Court decided the challenge to the Commission's Special Intensive Revision of the electoral rolls in Bihar. The judgment was delivered by **Chief Justice Surya Kant** for a Bench comprising himself and **Justice Joymalya Bagchi**, after hearings spread over twenty-nine days, the matter having been reserved on **29 January 2026**.

The Court framed four questions, and the four answers are the doctrine you must carry.

| Question | Holding |
|---|---|
| Did the Commission have the **power** to conduct such an exercise? | Yes. **Section 21(3)** of the RPA 1950 expressly contemplates a special revision in exceptional situations, materially distinct from ordinary revision under section 21(2) read with the Registration of Electors Rules, 1960. The exercise did not **supplant** the statutory framework; it "breathes life into the constitutional mandate under Article 324 within the precise statutory contours provided by Section 21(3)" |
| Did it pursue a **legitimate purpose** and satisfy **proportionality**? | Yes. The purpose — restoring accuracy, completeness and integrity of the rolls — is legitimate; the means bore a rational nexus; and the measures were neither arbitrary nor manifestly excessive. Proportionality, the Court said, is assessed "not in the abstract, but in the manner in which it is implemented", and safeguards adopted during the exercise, together with directions issued during the hearings, rendered the process constitutionally compliant in execution |
| Did the **procedure** violate the Act and the 1960 Rules? | No. The safeguards of Rule 21A — notice, inquiry, hearing and reasoned determination — were preserved **in substance**, distributed across enumeration, publication of draft rolls and the claims-and-objections stage; the statutory framework requires "fairness in action" rather than a single rigid format. The documentation regime was upheld as based on intelligible criteria with a nexus to roll integrity, the list of acceptable documents having been expanded during the exercise |
| Could the Commission examine **citizenship**? | A **limited inquiry into citizenship for electoral purposes** is permissible, but it is **not a final determination of citizenship under the Citizenship Act, 1955**. Where the Commission is not satisfied, it must **refer** the matter to the competent authority under that Act for adjudication according to law |

Two further points from the judgment are worth carrying.

**On the presumption from an existing entry.** The petitioners relied on *Lal Babu Hussein v. Electoral Registration Officer* (1995) for a presumption of citizenship for persons already on the roll. The Court held the presumption **rebuttable**: inclusion creates a presumption of validity, but calling upon electors to furnish supporting material in the course of such an exercise does not negate it. *Lal Babu Hussein* emphasised **procedural fairness in deletion proceedings**; it did not make entries immune from scrutiny.

**On consequential directions.** The Court directed the Commission to refer, within four weeks, all cases where names had been deleted on the ground that the persons were found not to be citizens; the competent authority was to conclude adjudication before the next Vidhan Sabha or local-body elections, whichever was earlier, after notice and hearing, with restoration to the roll if the persons were found to be citizens. Persons domiciled in the State whose names may have been wrongly deleted on the ground of absence were held entitled to make representations to be decided according to law.

### 5. The answer discipline this judgment demands

```text
PERMITTED
  "Section 21(3) authorises a special revision at any time,
   for reasons to be recorded, and the Court has upheld a
   special intensive revision as within that power and
   proportionate as implemented, while confining the
   Commission's citizenship inquiry to electoral purposes
   with a mandatory reference to the competent authority."

NOT PERMITTED
  X  "The ECI can decide who is a citizen."
  X  "The Court held the ECI cannot examine citizenship at all."
  X  turning a State-specific revision into a permanent
```
