```text
     nationwide rule
  X  reciting contested elector totals, deletion figures or
     fraud allegations as established fact
```

The two outer errors are mirror images, and both are common. The Court neither gave the Commission citizenship-determining power nor denied it any inquiry; it drew a line between **satisfaction for electoral registration** and **adjudication of status**.

### 6. The seven-question audit for any roll-revision answer

When a question asks you to evaluate a revision — in any State, in any year — do not argue from the headline. Argue from this checklist.

| Audit question | Why it decides the answer |
|---|---|
| Under what exact order and statutory provision was it directed? | Separates lawful special revision under section 21(3) from administrative improvisation |
| Were reasons recorded, as section 21(3) requires? | The recording of reasons is a statutory condition, not a formality |
| Who bears the burden of proof, and is it realistic? | Determines whether verification becomes exclusion |
| Was individual notice practical, and was a hearing given before deletion? | Section 22's proviso and the fairness requirement |
| Were migration, homelessness, disability and documentary incapacity addressed? | Inclusion is half of integrity |
| Is an appeal available and decidable before the poll? | Section 24; a remedy after polling is no remedy |
| Is aggregate data independently auditable? | Public confidence needs evidence, not assurance |

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: statutory and constitutional powers of the Election Commission; citizenship; due process in administrative action.

**Where this lesson is tested.** It directly supports the 2026 GS-II question in the index on the Commission's position while revising electoral rolls and whether it may examine the citizenship of voters. The procedural provisions are also sound objective material.

**Probable framings.** "Discuss the position of the Election Commission of India while undertaking the revision of electoral rolls. Can it examine the question of citizenship of voters?"

**Traps.**

- Confusing ordinary revision under section 21(2) with special revision under section 21(3).
- Forgetting that the existing roll continues in force until a special revision is complete.
- Forgetting the hearing requirement before deletion under section 22.
- Saying civil courts can decide roll entitlement; section 30 bars them.
- Saying the Commission can conclusively determine citizenship.
- Freezing contested numbers from a particular State exercise into a general claim.

**Answer use.** Build the answer in this order: statutory power, recorded reasons, procedural safeguards, proportionality in implementation, citizenship boundary, reference to the competent authority. That order mirrors the Court's own reasoning and reads as structured rather than assembled.

### Revision notes

1. **Section 21(1)**: initial preparation of the roll before each general election.
2. **Section 21(2)**: revision before each general election to the House of the People or an Assembly; a proviso saves the validity of an unrevised roll.
3. **Section 21(3)**: the Commission may **at any time, for reasons to be recorded**, direct a **special revision** for a constituency or part of one, **in such manner as it may think fit**; the existing roll continues until completion.
4. **Section 22**: correction, transposition and deletion by the Electoral Registration Officer, with a **reasonable opportunity of being heard** before most actions; death is the exception.
5. **Section 23(1)–(2)**: inclusion on application, with intimation to the other constituency where the applicant is already registered.
6. **Section 23(3)**: no correction, deletion or inclusion between the last date for nominations and the completion of that election.
7. **Section 24**: appeal to the **Chief Electoral Officer**; **section 25**: prescribed fee.
8. **Section 30**: civil courts barred on entitlement and on the legality of an Electoral Registration Officer's actions; constitutional remedies survive.
9. ***Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India***, **27 May 2026**: special intensive revision upheld.
10. Bench: **Chief Justice Surya Kant and Justice Joymalya Bagchi**; reserved 29 January 2026.
11. The exercise was held to operate **within** section 21(3) and to further Article 324, not to supplant the statute.
12. Proportionality assessed in implementation, not in the abstract; safeguards and in-hearing directions mattered.
13. Rule 21A safeguards held preserved **in substance** across enumeration, draft publication and claims-and-objections.
14. *Lal Babu Hussein* (1995): the presumption from an existing entry is **rebuttable**; it secures fair deletion procedure, not immunity from scrutiny.
15. Citizenship: a **limited inquiry for electoral purposes** is permitted; final determination belongs to the competent authority under the **Citizenship Act, 1955**, to whom unsatisfied cases must be referred.

### Concept check

**Question:** A Commission order directs a special intensive revision in one State, records its reasons, and requires existing electors to furnish listed documents. A petitioner argues that (i) the Commission may revise rolls only before a general election, and (ii) persons already on the roll enjoy a conclusive presumption of eligibility. Assess both arguments.

**Model answer:** Both fail. On the first, section 21(2) governs ordinary revision before a general election, but **section 21(3)** opens with a non-obstante clause and permits the Commission to direct a special revision **at any time**, for reasons to be recorded, for a constituency or part of one, in such manner as it thinks fit — so timing is not a bar where reasons are recorded. On the second, *Lal Babu Hussein* establishes a presumption of validity attaching to an existing entry, but the Supreme Court held in 2026 that the presumption is **rebuttable** and that requiring supporting material during such an exercise does not negate it; the case secures procedural fairness in deletion, not immunity from verification. The real safeguards are the recorded reasons, the continuance of the existing roll until completion, the hearing before deletion, and the appeal under section 24.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading a presumption as a prohibition. A rebuttable presumption shifts who must explain; it does not forbid the inquiry.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** Discuss the position of the Election Commission of India while revising electoral rolls, and examine whether it may inquire into the citizenship of voters. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (250 words):**
**The power.** Article 324(1) vests superintendence, direction and control of the preparation of electoral rolls in the Commission, and Article 325 guarantees one general roll without exclusion on grounds only of religion, race, caste or sex. The Act of 1950 supplies the machinery: section 15 requires a roll for every constituency; section 19 states entitlement by age on the qualifying date and ordinary residence; and section 21 provides for preparation, ordinary revision and, under section 21(3), a **special revision at any time, for reasons to be recorded, in such manner as the Commission thinks fit**, the existing roll continuing until completion.

**The limits.** Section 22 requires a hearing before deletion on grounds other than death; section 23(3) freezes the roll from the last date for nominations until that election completes; section 24 gives an appeal to the Chief Electoral Officer; section 30 ousts civil courts but not constitutional review.

**Citizenship.** Section 16(1)(a) disqualifies a non-citizen from registration, so the Commission must form a view on eligibility. In *Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India* (27 May 2026) the Court upheld a special intensive revision as within section 21(3) and proportionate as implemented, and held that the Commission may make a **limited inquiry into citizenship for electoral purposes** but **cannot finally determine status**; unsatisfied cases must be referred to the competent authority under the Citizenship Act, 1955, with restoration if citizenship is established.

**Verdict.** The Commission is a registering authority with a bounded eligibility inquiry, not a citizenship tribunal.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The decisive discrimination is between **eligibility satisfaction** and **status adjudication**. Scripts that answer only "yes" or only "no" to the citizenship limb cannot reach the top band, however well the roll machinery is described.

### Why the next lesson follows

Running through this lesson is an unexamined assumption: that being on the roll gives you a right of some constitutional weight. What kind of right is the vote? The answer is more contested than most notes admit, and it decides the first limb of a recent GS-II question.

---
## Lesson 12: Is the Right to Vote a Fundamental Right?

Progress: 12 / 20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: The statutory-right line, its qualifications, and how to answer the question without overclaiming

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — RPA 1951 section 62 and the leading authorities on electoral rights checked
CA search: "right to vote fundamental right India Supreme Court position"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson; the authorities below are decided and dated
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: one act, three different legal characterisations

```text
  THE SINGLE ACT OF VOTING, DISSECTED

  (1) ENTITLEMENT to be registered and to vote
         source: Article 326 + RPA 1950 s.19 + RPA 1951 s.62
         character: STATUTORY RIGHT on the dominant line
                    (a minority judicial view calls it
                     a CONSTITUTIONAL right traceable to Art 326)

  (2) EXPRESSION involved in casting the vote
         source: Article 19(1)(a)
         character: aspects treated as FUNDAMENTAL
                    (the right to know; the secret negative vote)

  (3) FORUM to enforce or dispute it
         source: Article 329(b) + RPA 1951
         character: STATUTORY REMEDY, post-result only
```

Most wrong answers happen because a candidate answers for limb (1) using authority from limb (2), or the reverse. Keep the three apart and the question becomes easy.

### 1. Start with the puzzle

Voting is the most democratic act a citizen performs, and the Constitution guarantees adult suffrage in Article 326. So why is it not simply a fundamental right?

Because of **where it is placed and how it is created**. Part III confers rights that a citizen may enforce against the State directly under Article 32. Article 326 sits in Part XV and does something different: it *directs the basis on which elections shall be held* and says who "shall be entitled to be registered as a voter". The actual act of voting is then conferred and regulated by statute — **section 62 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951** states who has the right to vote at an election, and attaches conditions, including the bar in section 62(5) on persons confined in prison or in lawful police custody.

A right whose existence, content and exceptions are supplied by a statute is, on the traditional analysis, a statutory right.

### 2. The dominant line of authority

| Case | Proposition |
|---|---|
| *N.P. Ponnuswami v. Returning Officer* (1952) | The right to vote is a creature of statute and must be subject to the limitations imposed by it; the whole electoral right, including the right to dispute an election, is statutory |
| *Jyoti Basu v. Debi Ghosal* (1982) | "A right to elect, fundamental though it is to democracy, is, anomalously enough, neither a fundamental right nor a Common Law Right. It is pure and simple, a statutory right." The same was said of the right to be elected and the right to dispute an election |
| *Kuldip Nayar v. Union of India* (2006) | Reaffirmed that the right to vote is a statutory right, subject to the limitations imposed by the statute that creates it |
| *Rajbala v. State of Haryana* (2015) | Reiterated the statutory characterisation while testing statutory conditions on contesting local-body elections |

The quoted sentence from *Jyoti Basu* is the single most useful citation in the topic. Note its internal tension — "fundamental though it is to democracy" — which the Court itself calls anomalous. That concession is what later judgments build on.

### 3. The qualification: Article 19(1)(a) enters through expression

The Court has repeatedly held that although the entitlement is statutory, certain dimensions of voting engage **freedom of speech and expression**.

- In ***Union of India v. Association for Democratic Reforms*** (2002) and ***People's Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India*** (2003), the Court held that a voter's **right to know** the criminal, financial and educational antecedents of candidates is part of Article 19(1)(a); an uninformed vote is not a free expression of choice.
- In ***People's Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India*** (2013) — the NOTA case — the Court treated the casting of a **negative vote** as an exercise of expression, and directed a secret "None of the Above" option to protect the secrecy that makes that expression free.

So the correct formulation is not "voting is a fundamental right" but: **the entitlement is statutory, while specific expressive and secrecy dimensions of the electoral process attract Article 19(1)(a) protection**.

### 4. The minority view, labelled as such

In ***Anoop Baranwal v. Union of India*** (2023), a separate concurring opinion by **Justice Ajay Rastogi** took the view that, by virtue of **Article 326**, the right to vote is better described as a **constitutional right** rather than a merely statutory one, and connected the act of voting to expression under Article 19(1)(a).

Handle this carefully in an answer.

```text
PERMITTED
  "The dominant line treats the right to vote as statutory
   (Jyoti Basu; Kuldip Nayar; Rajbala), while a concurring
   opinion in Anoop Baranwal (2023) described it as a
   constitutional right flowing from Article 326, and the
   Court has long protected expressive dimensions under
   Article 19(1)(a)."

NOT PERMITTED
  X  "The Supreme Court has held that the right to vote is a
      fundamental right."
  X  presenting a concurring opinion as the holding
  X  omitting the dominant line because the minority view is
      more attractive
```

The examiner is not testing which view you prefer. The examiner is testing whether you can distinguish a **holding** from a **concurrence**, and a **statutory right** from a **constitutional right** from a **fundamental right**.

### 5. Why the characterisation has practical consequences

This is not a taxonomy exercise. Three consequences follow.

**First, the remedy.** If voting were a Part III right, an aggrieved voter would move the Supreme Court under Article 32 as of right. Because the entitlement is statutory and Article 329(b) channels election challenges, the ordinary routes are an administrative appeal on the roll under section 24 of the RPA 1950 and an election petition after the result.

**Second, the permissible restriction.** A statutory right may be conditioned by the statute that creates it — hence section 62(5)'s disqualification of persons in prison or lawful police custody, upheld in ***Anukul Chandra Pradhan v. Union of India*** (1997) on grounds including the practical difficulties of polling in custody and the policy of keeping persons with criminal antecedents out of the electoral process. A fundamental-right characterisation would force every such condition through the Article 19 reasonableness tests.

**Third, the argumentative load.** When you argue that a roll exercise is unconstitutional, you cannot simply say "the fundamental right to vote has been violated". You must argue through **Article 14** (arbitrariness), **Article 21** (fair procedure), **Article 325** (no identity-based exclusion), **Article 326** (adult suffrage) and the statutory safeguards. That is exactly how the 2026 roll-revision challenge was framed and decided.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** A democracy whose central act is "pure and simple, a statutory right" has misclassified its own foundation. If Parliament can condition the vote, the basic structure's commitment to free and fair elections rests on legislative goodwill.

**Reply.** The commitment does not rest on goodwill. *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975) places free and fair elections within the basic structure, so legislation that destroys the electoral process is void regardless of how the individual entitlement is classified. Article 325 independently forbids identity-based exclusion. And Article 19(1)(a) protects information and secrecy. The statutory characterisation limits the *form of the individual claim*; it does not leave the system unprotected.

**Residual.** The reply is institutionally sound and individually thin. A single voter wrongly deleted from a roll has a statutory appeal and a writ remedy, but no Part III claim of the strongest kind, and the practical value of her remedy depends on whether it can be decided before polling. That gap is real, and an honest answer names it rather than resolving it by assertion.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: fundamental rights; representation of the people; judicial pronouncements.

**Where this lesson is tested.** It is the first limb of the 2026 GS-II question in the index. It also supplies the precision that separates a confident answer from an accurate one.

**Probable framings.** "Is the right to vote a fundamental right?" as the opening limb of a composite question on electoral rolls.

**Traps.**

- Calling the vote a fundamental right because Article 326 guarantees adult suffrage.
- Citing *PUCL* (2003) for the proposition that voting is a fundamental right; it held the **right to know** to be protected under Article 19(1)(a).
- Presenting the *Anoop Baranwal* concurrence as the Court's holding.
- Forgetting section 62 of the RPA 1951 as the operative statutory source.
- Treating "constitutional right" and "fundamental right" as synonyms.

**Answer use.** Answer the limb in three sentences — statutory entitlement, expressive dimensions under Article 19(1)(a), minority constitutional-right view — and then move to the substance of the question. Candidates who spend half their words on classification lose the marks available for the operative limbs.

### Revision notes

1. Article 326 establishes **adult suffrage** and entitlement to registration; it sits in Part XV, not Part III.
2. **Section 62, RPA 1951** confers and conditions the right to vote; section 62(5) bars persons confined in prison or in lawful police custody.
3. *N.P. Ponnuswami* (1952): the electoral right is a creature of statute.
4. *Jyoti Basu v. Debi Ghosal* (1982): the right to elect is "neither a fundamental right nor a Common Law Right... pure and simple, a statutory right".
5. *Kuldip Nayar* (2006) and *Rajbala v. State of Haryana* (2015) reaffirm the statutory characterisation.
6. *ADR* (2002) and *PUCL* (2003): the **voter's right to know** candidate antecedents is protected by **Article 19(1)(a)**.
7. *PUCL* (2013): the secret **negative vote** (NOTA) protected as an exercise of expression.
8. *Anukul Chandra Pradhan* (1997) upheld section 62(5).
9. *Anoop Baranwal* (2023): a **concurring** opinion described the vote as a **constitutional right** flowing from Article 326.
10. Distinguish statutory right, constitutional right and fundamental right; they are three categories, not two.
11. Consequence one: remedy runs through section 24 appeals and election petitions, not ordinarily through Article 32.
12. Consequence two: statutory conditions on the vote are permissible within limits.
13. Consequence three: constitutional challenges must be argued through Articles 14, 21, 325 and 326, not through an assumed Part III right to vote.
14. System-level protection comes from the basic-structure holding in *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975).

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Since the Supreme Court in PUCL held voting to be a fundamental right under Article 19(1)(a), any statutory condition on voting must satisfy Article 19(2)." Identify the errors and give the correct position in two sentences.

**Model answer:** Two errors. *PUCL* (2003) held that the **voter's right to know** a candidate's antecedents is protected by Article 19(1)(a); it did not convert the entitlement to vote into a fundamental right, and the dominant line from *Jyoti Basu* (1982) through *Kuldip Nayar* (2006) and *Rajbala* (2015) treats that entitlement as statutory. Consequently, statutory conditions such as section 62(5) of the RPA 1951 are not routinely tested under Article 19(2); they are tested for legislative competence and for arbitrariness under Article 14 and fairness under Article 21, as *Anukul Chandra Pradhan* (1997) illustrates.

**Misconception to avoid:** Transferring the protection attached to one dimension of the electoral process — information and expression — to the entitlement itself. The Court has protected the former without reclassifying the latter.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** "The right to vote in India is statutory in form and constitutional in significance." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (150 words):**
The formulation is accurate on both halves. **In form**, the entitlement is statutory: Article 326 directs that elections to the House of the People and State Assemblies be on adult suffrage, but the operative right is conferred and conditioned by section 62 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951, with registration governed by the Act of 1950. *Jyoti Basu v. Debi Ghosal* (1982) held the right to elect "neither a fundamental right nor a Common Law Right... pure and simple, a statutory right", reaffirmed in *Kuldip Nayar* (2006) and *Rajbala* (2015). **In significance**, it is constitutional: free and fair elections are part of the basic structure after *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975); Article 325 forbids identity-based exclusion; and *ADR* (2002), *PUCL* (2003) and *PUCL* (2013) protect the voter's information and secrecy under Article 19(1)(a). A concurring opinion in *Anoop Baranwal* (2023) would elevate form to match significance.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** Both halves must be evidenced. A script that proves only the statutory form, or only the democratic significance, is half an answer; the top band additionally labels the *Anoop Baranwal* view as a concurrence rather than a holding.

### Why the next lesson follows

With the electorate settled, the contest can begin. The next lesson follows a single election from the Commission's announcement to the declaration of the result, and identifies at each stage which powers are the Commission's, which are statutory, and which belong to somebody else entirely.

---

## Lesson 13: From Announcement to Declaration

Progress: 13 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Schedule and notification, candidature, the two-constituency cap, expenditure monitoring, repoll, NOTA, and the participation chain

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — RPA 1951 scheme for notification, nominations, expenses and poll conduct, with the Commission's published accessibility instructions and the Conduct of Elections Rules provisions on assisted and home voting
CA search: "Election Commission schedule announcement notification process"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: one election, stage by stage, with the authority named at each step

```text
  STAGE                              AUTHORITY / SOURCE
  ---------------------------------  ------------------------------
  readiness assessment               ECI (Art 324)
         |
  SCHEDULE ANNOUNCEMENT  ----------> MODEL CODE OF CONDUCT BEGINS
         |                           ECI (Art 324; non-statutory code)
         |
  STATUTORY NOTIFICATION             President / Governor on the ECI's
         |                           recommendation, under the RPA 1951
         |
  nominations filed                  Returning Officer (RPA 1951, Pt V)
         |
  SCRUTINY                           Returning Officer
         |
  withdrawal                         candidate
         |
  symbol allotment                   RO under the Symbols Order, 1968
         |
  campaign + expenditure monitoring  ECI machinery; RPA 1951 ss.77, 78
         |
  silence period / exit-poll bar     RPA 1951 ss.126, 126A
         |
  POLL (with NOTA on the machine)    Presiding Officer
         |
  counting                           Returning Officer
         |
  DECLARATION OF RESULT              Returning Officer
         |
  dispute                            HIGH COURT election petition
                                     (Art 329(b); RPA 1951 ss.80, 80A)
```

### 1. Announcement is not notification, and the difference is examinable

The Commission **announces** a schedule after assessing readiness — law and order, availability of forces, rolls, machines, examinations, weather and festivals. That announcement is an exercise of Article 324 supervision and it has one immediate legal-practical consequence: **the Model Code of Conduct comes into force**.

The **statutory election process** begins later, with notifications issued under the Representation of the People Act, 1951 — by the **President** for parliamentary constituencies and by the **Governor** for Assembly constituencies — calling upon the constituency to elect a member. The timetable of nomination, scrutiny, withdrawal and poll then runs from that notification.

```text
  "The ECI alone notifies every election by its own order."   X

  ECI recommends and announces  ->  President/Governor notifies
  ->  Returning Officer carries the statutory timetable
```

This is a genuine trap. The Commission's announcement starts the political clock; the notification starts the legal clock.

### 2. Candidature: qualification, nomination, scrutiny

Three layers stack on top of each other, and an answer should name all three.

1. **Constitutional qualifications** — Article 84 for Parliament and Article 173 for State Legislatures: citizenship, the prescribed oath, minimum age (twenty-five for the House of the People and a Legislative Assembly; thirty for the Council of States and a Legislative Council), and such other qualifications as Parliament prescribes.
2. **Statutory qualifications and disqualifications** — Part II of the Representation of the People Act, 1951, which also requires the candidate to be an **elector** for a relevant constituency.
3. **Procedural validity** — a nomination paper correctly filed with the required proposers and security deposit, within time, before the Returning Officer, who conducts **scrutiny** and accepts or rejects.

The limit that matters: the Commission issues binding administrative instructions within this framework — on affidavits, on the format of disclosure, on scrutiny practice — but it **cannot add a disqualification that Parliament has not enacted**, however undesirable it considers a candidate. We will see in Lesson 16 why that limit is doctrinal and not merely prudential.

### 3. Contesting from more than one constituency

This is a small, precise, frequently tested rule.

- **Section 33(7) of the RPA 1951** caps a person at **two constituencies** in the same general election (or group of by-elections or biennial elections). Before the **1996** amendment there was no such cap, which is why a candidate could contest from three constituencies in the 1991 Lok Sabha election.
- **Section 70** provides that a person may not sit in the House for more than one seat; where elected from more than one, he must vacate all but one within the prescribed time, failing which all seats become vacant.
- The consequence is a **by-election** in the vacated constituency. Proposals that the candidate or his party bear the cost of that by-election have been discussed as reform; they are **not** the present rule.

That last sentence is worth the attention it is getting, because a verified Prelims question in the index tests precisely the difference between a discussed reform and an existing rule.

### 4. Money: ceilings, accounts and the monitoring machine

| Element | Provision | Content |
|---|---|---|
| Account of election expenses | **Section 77, RPA 1951** | Every candidate shall keep a separate and correct account of all expenditure incurred or authorised between the date of nomination and the date of declaration of result; the account is subject to a prescribed **ceiling** |
| Lodging of account | **Section 78, RPA 1951** | The account must be lodged with the prescribed authority within thirty days of the election |
| Consequence of failure | **Section 10A, RPA 1951** | The Commission may declare a person disqualified for **three years** for failure to lodge the account in time and in the prescribed manner, without good reason or justification |
| Excess expenditure | **Section 123(6), RPA 1951** | Incurring or authorising expenditure in excess of the ceiling is a **corrupt practice** |

Around these provisions the Commission has built an administrative machine: expenditure observers, flying squads, static surveillance teams, video surveillance and viewing teams, accounting teams, rate cards for campaign items, shadow observation registers maintained against each candidate, and media certification and monitoring for paid news and advertisements.

The analytical gap to name in every answer on political finance: **the ceiling attaches to the candidate**, while much modern campaigning is conducted by the **party**, by supporters and through digital platforms. Section 77's Explanation historically excluded certain party and leader expenditure from the candidate's account, and the asymmetry between regulated candidate spending and comparatively unregulated ecosystem spending is the real deficit, not the level of the ceiling.

### 5. When a poll goes wrong: adjournment, fresh poll and countermand

Election law provides graduated responses, and the Commission's residual Article 324 power supplements them where the law is silent.

```text
  LOCAL DEFECT
    -> corrective instruction, replacement of personnel,
       or a fresh poll at the affected polling station

  STATUTORY EVENT
    (riot, violence, interference, loss or destruction of
     ballot papers or machines, booth capturing)
    -> adjournment of the poll or a fresh poll under the
       Representation of the People Act, 1951

  CONSTITUENCY-WIDE VITIATION
    -> countermanding of the election, only on a lawful
       basis and with recorded reasons

  POST-RESULT VALIDITY DISPUTE
    -> election petition before the High Court; NOT an
       ECI adjudication
```

Say the limit explicitly: "the Commission may cancel any election whenever it wishes" is false. The trigger, the scale, the recorded reasons, the statutory field and the fairness context all matter, and *Mohinder Singh Gill* — which we reach in Lesson 16 — is the authority both for the breadth of the power and for its legal control.

### 6. NOTA: what it is and what it is not

In ***People's Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India*** (2013) the Supreme Court directed the provision of a **"None of the Above"** option so that a voter who wishes to reject all candidates may do so **without surrendering secrecy** — the earlier procedure for recording a refusal to vote compromised secrecy, which itself deterred the exercise.

```text
  NOTA selected
     -> a secret expression of rejection
     -> counted and reported in the result
     X  NOTA is not a candidate
     X  NOTA votes do not elect anybody
     X  no automatic cancellation of the election
     X  no automatic re-poll, even if NOTA exceeds every
        candidate's vote, under the ordinary present rule
```

The reform frequently proposed — that an election be voided and re-held where NOTA leads — is a **proposal**, not the law. Distinguish the two in any answer; it is the standard close-option trap in objective questions and the standard overclaim in written ones.

### 7. Participation: information, registration, accessibility and ethical voting

Everything so far in this lesson has been about *conducting* an election correctly. There is a second question that the law asks much more quietly: can the people who are entitled to vote actually reach the act of voting at all? A poll can be legally flawless, machine-verified and judicially unimpeachable, and still fail an elector who never knew she was eligible, could not find her name, or could not get a wheelchair up to the door.

That is why the Commission treats participation as an **integrity** question and not as publicity.

```text
  INFORMATION  --->  REGISTRATION  --->  ACCESSIBILITY  --->  ETHICAL VOTING
       |                  |                    |                    |
  voter education    enrolment and       physical and          vote cast without
  and awareness      correction          procedural access     inducement or
  (SVEEP)            services                                  intimidation
       |                  |                    |                    |
  "that I am         "that my name       "that I can          "that my choice
   eligible"          is on the roll"     reach and use        is my own"
                                          the booth"
                              |
          A break at ANY stage produces the same outcome as a
          wrong count: an elector whose entitlement did not
          become a vote.
```

**Information.** The Commission's standing voter-education programme is **SVEEP** — Systematic Voters' Education and Electoral Participation — which has run since 2009 and is designed around a simple premise: awareness, registration and informed participation are one continuous problem, not four separate campaigns. Its interventions are deliberately targeted rather than uniform, because the reason a young urban professional does not vote is not the reason a first-time tribal woman elector does not vote.

**Registration.** Information is useless unless it ends in an entry on the roll, which is why voter-service machinery — enrolment and correction forms, facilitation centres, booth-level officers, continuous updating and the four qualifying dates of Lesson 10 — is the second link rather than an administrative afterthought.

**Accessibility.** This is the link most answers omit, and it is the most concrete. The Commission's standing instructions require polling stations to be located on the ground floor, or with a lift or extended ramp; standardised permanent ramps of proper gradient leading directly to the polling-station door; wheelchairs at identified stations; adequate space inside for a wheelchair to move; standard signage along the approach path; priority entry and, where numbers justify it, a separate queue for electors with disabilities and senior citizens; and verification of these facilities by the Commission's observer. Two further supports are statutory rather than administrative:

| Support | Legal basis | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| Companion for a blind or infirm elector | **Rule 49N**, Conduct of Elections Rules, 1961 | Permits a companion to accompany the elector into the voting compartment and record the vote |
| Postal ballot for an "absentee voter" at home | **Section 60(c)**, RPA 1951, with **Rule 27A**, Conduct of Elections Rules, 1961 | Allows notified categories — senior citizens above a notified age and persons with benchmark disability — to vote from home on application in the prescribed form |

For the 2024 general election the home-voting facility was extended across the country to electors **aged 85 and above** and to **persons with disabilities with at least forty per cent benchmark disability**, on application in the prescribed form made within five days of the notification, with a polling team, a videographer and security personnel visiting the residence and secrecy preserved. Note the structure rather than memorising the threshold: the categories and the age are fixed by **notification and rules**, and the senior-citizen age has already been revised once, so quote the mechanism confidently and the number cautiously.

**Ethical voting.** The last link is the one the Commission cannot engineer. An elector who reaches the booth under inducement has been reached physically but not freely, which is why bribery is a corrupt practice under section 123(1) of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 and why awareness work is addressed to inducement-free voting and not merely to turnout.

Now the limits, which are what make this material examinable rather than promotional:

1. **Voter education must be strictly non-partisan.** The Commission may urge people to vote; it may not urge them how to vote, and it may not allow a governing party's achievements to be carried on the back of an awareness campaign. The Model Code constraints of Lesson 15 bite on exactly this boundary.
2. **Awareness is not a remedy.** A campaign cannot substitute for the legal enrolment and correction machinery — claims, objections, the hearing requirement before deletion and the appeal to the Chief Electoral Officer. If a name is wrongly deleted, the answer is the statutory remedy, not a publicity drive.
3. **Turnout is a weak metric.** Higher participation is desirable, but a roll that is inaccurate in both directions can produce an impressive turnout percentage over a defective base. Participation and accuracy must be argued together, never traded against each other.

The proposition to carry away is the one that joins this lesson to Lesson 19: **a secure machine cannot cure an inaccessible roll or an unreachable polling station**. Both failures end in an uncast vote, and only one of them is ever discussed.

### 8. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** The whole sequence is heavily front-loaded on the Commission — announcement, instructions, observers, surveillance — and almost empty of remedy for the aggrieved candidate until after the result. That is a deliberate asymmetry in favour of the administrator.

**Reply.** The asymmetry is the price of a finishable election. Article 329(b) exists precisely because interlocutory litigation could freeze a national timetable indefinitely, and *N.P. Ponnuswami* explains why interference midstream is barred. Within the window, the aggrieved candidate is not without recourse: observers receive complaints, the Commission can and does issue corrective directions, and all of it is evidence in the later petition.

**Residual.** The reply holds only if the later remedy actually arrives in time. Where an election petition outlives the term of the House, the post-result remedy becomes formal rather than real — which is why time-bound adjudication, not expanded Commission power, is the right reform. We develop that in Lesson 18.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: conduct of elections; electoral reforms; transparency and accountability in political finance.

**Where this lesson is tested.** The two-constituency rule is a verified Prelims item in the index. Expenditure monitoring and NOTA appear in reform questions.

**Probable framings.** "Election expenditure regulation in India controls the candidate and misses the campaign. Discuss."

**Traps.**

- "The ECI issues the election notification." The President or Governor does.
- "A candidate may contest from any number of constituencies." Section 33(7) caps it at two.
- "The party must bear the by-election cost." A proposal, not the rule.
- "NOTA winning forces a re-poll." It does not.
- "The ECI can cancel any poll at will." Only on lawful grounds with recorded reasons.
- Confusing the Model Code's start (announcement) with the statutory process's start (notification).
- Treating voter-education work as optional publicity, or describing home voting as a general facility rather than one confined to notified categories of absentee voters.

**Answer use.** Use the stage-and-authority rail. Questions in this area are usually about **who** may do **what** and **when**, and a candidate who names the authority at each stage answers them almost automatically.

### Revision notes

1. The Commission **announces** the schedule; the **Model Code** begins at announcement.
2. The **President** (parliamentary constituencies) or **Governor** (Assembly constituencies) issues the statutory **notification** under the RPA 1951.
3. Candidature stacks constitutional qualifications (Articles 84 and 173), statutory qualifications and disqualifications (RPA 1951 Part II), and procedural validity before the Returning Officer.
4. The Commission cannot create a disqualification Parliament has not enacted.
5. **Section 33(7), RPA 1951**: maximum **two** constituencies; the cap dates from the **1996** amendment.
6. **Section 70**: a person may hold only one seat; others must be vacated, triggering by-elections.
7. Party-borne by-election cost is a **reform proposal**, not the present rule.
8. **Section 77**: separate and correct account of election expenses, subject to a ceiling; **section 78**: the account is lodged within **thirty days** of the election.
9. **Section 10A**: disqualification up to **three years** for failure to lodge without good reason; **section 123(6)**: expenditure beyond the ceiling is a **corrupt practice**.
10. Monitoring: expenditure observers, flying squads, static surveillance teams, video teams, accounting teams, shadow registers, media certification and monitoring.
11. The structural gap is candidate-focused ceilings against party, supporter and digital spending.
12. Graduated poll responses: station-level fresh poll, statutory adjournment or fresh poll, countermand with recorded reasons, and post-result petition.
13. ***PUCL*** (2013): secret **NOTA** option; NOTA does not win, void the poll or force a re-election.
14. **Sections 126 and 126A**: silence-period restrictions and the bar on publishing exit-poll results during the notified period.
15. Participation chain: **information → registration → accessibility → ethical voting**. **SVEEP**, the Commission's standing voter-education programme since 2009, works on the first and second links and must remain strictly non-partisan.
16. Accessibility: ground-floor stations or lifts and extended ramps, standardised permanent ramps, wheelchairs, signage and priority entry; **Rule 49N** permits a companion for a blind or infirm elector; **section 60(c)** with **Rule 27A** permits notified "absentee voters" — senior citizens above a notified age and persons with benchmark disability — to vote from home. A secure machine cannot cure an inaccessible roll or an unreachable booth.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate contests from three Lok Sabha constituencies, wins two, and is asked to vacate one. He argues that no law caps the number of constituencies and that, in any event, his party should not bear by-election costs. Evaluate both propositions and identify the provisions.

**Model answer:** The first proposition is wrong in law. **Section 33(7) of the Representation of the People Act, 1951**, introduced by the 1996 amendment, caps a candidate at **two** constituencies in the same general election, so a three-constituency candidature is not permissible today, whatever was possible in 1991. The second proposition is right as a statement of present law but is not a defence: **section 70** requires a person elected from more than one seat to vacate all but one, and the resulting by-election is conducted at public cost. Proposals that the candidate or the party bear that cost have been discussed as reform but have not been enacted.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a well-known reform proposal as the existing rule. In this topic, the difference between a recommendation and an enacted provision is itself an examinable fact.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** "India regulates the cost of a candidate and ignores the cost of a campaign." Critically examine the framework for monitoring election expenditure. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (246 words):**
The statement identifies the central design flaw, though the machinery it criticises is genuinely substantial.

**What the law regulates.** Section 77 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 requires every candidate to keep a separate and correct account of all expenditure incurred or authorised between nomination and declaration of the result, subject to a prescribed ceiling. Section 78 requires lodging within thirty days. Section 10A empowers the Commission to disqualify for up to three years for failure to lodge without good reason, and section 123(6) makes expenditure beyond the ceiling a corrupt practice.

**How it is enforced.** The Commission deploys expenditure observers, flying squads, static surveillance teams, video surveillance and viewing teams and accounting teams; maintains shadow observation registers against candidate accounts using published rate cards; and operates media certification and monitoring for advertisements and paid news. Enforcement is therefore real and evidentiary, not merely declaratory.

**Where it fails.** The unit of regulation is the candidate. Party expenditure, expenditure by supporters and sympathetic organisations, and digital advertising and influence operations largely escape the candidate ceiling. Ceilings are also periodically revised upward, while the costliest inputs are the least traceable.

**Qualification.** Raising or abolishing ceilings is not obviously the answer: without a ceiling, disclosure becomes the only discipline — precisely what the February 2024 electoral-bonds judgment restored.

**Way forward.** Bring party and third-party campaign spending within an auditable reporting regime, require platform-level disclosure of political advertising, publish aggregate monitoring outcomes, and tie enforcement to timely adjudication.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The question is about a mismatch between the regulated unit and the real unit of spending. Scripts that list provisions and enforcement teams without naming that mismatch cannot exceed the middle band, even if the legal detail is faultless.

### Why the next lesson follows

One step in the sequence was passed over quickly: symbol allotment. Behind it lies the Commission's quasi-judicial jurisdiction — the power to register parties, to recognise them, to reserve symbols and to decide who owns one when a party splits.

---
## Lesson 14: Parties and Symbols — The Quasi-Judicial Jurisdiction

Progress: 14 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Section 29A registration, recognition under the Symbols Order, split disputes, and the boundary of the jurisdiction

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — RPA 1951 section 29A scheme and the official Election Symbols (Reservation and Allotment) Order, 1968 as amended, read in full for paragraphs 6A, 6B, 6C, 15, 16, 16A and 17
CA search: "Election Commission symbol dispute recognition criteria"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: the party-status ladder, and the two different legal instruments

```text
  an association or body of individual citizens of India
          |
          |  application under RPA 1951, s.29A
          v
  REGISTERED POLITICAL PARTY
     . must bear true faith and allegiance to the Constitution,
       to socialism, secularism and democracy, and uphold the
       sovereignty, unity and integrity of India
     . registration is a STATUTORY status
          |
          |  electoral performance measured against
          |  the SYMBOLS ORDER, 1968
          v
  RECOGNISED STATE PARTY   [para 6A]        RECOGNISED NATIONAL PARTY  [para 6B]
     . reserved symbol in that State           . reserved symbol across India
     . continued recognition rules [para 6C]   . continued recognition rules [para 6C]

  Registration  !=  Recognition.  Recognition  !=  permanent symbol.
```

Two instruments, not one. **Registration** is under a **statute**, the Representation of the People Act, 1951. **Recognition and symbols** are under an **order** made by the Commission, the Election Symbols (Reservation and Allotment) Order, 1968. Keeping the instruments apart keeps the answer clean.

### 1. Registration under section 29A

Section 29A of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 allows an association or body of individual citizens of India calling itself a political party to apply to the Commission for registration. The application must be made within the prescribed period of formation, in the prescribed form, with the prescribed particulars, and the party's memorandum or rules must contain a specific provision that the party **shall bear true faith and allegiance to the Constitution of India as by law established, and to the principles of socialism, secularism and democracy, and would uphold the sovereignty, unity and integrity of India**.

What registration gives: a statutory identity, the ability to have candidates set up by the party, eligibility for the benefits that other laws attach to registered parties, and the gateway to recognition.

What registration does **not** give: recognition, a reserved symbol, or any guarantee that the party's name is protected in perpetuity.

### 2. Recognition under paragraphs 6A and 6B

The Symbols Order divides political parties into **recognised** and **unrecognised**, and recognised parties into **National** and **State** parties. The criteria are performance-based and alternative — satisfying **any one** condition suffices.

**Paragraph 6A — conditions for recognition as a State party** (any one of):

1. at the last general election to the State Legislative Assembly, the party's candidates secured **not less than six per cent** of the total valid votes polled in the State, **and** the party returned **at least two members** to that Assembly; or
2. at the last general election to the House of the People from that State, the party's candidates secured **not less than six per cent** of the total valid votes polled in the State, **and** the party returned **at least one member** to the House of the People from that State; or
3. at the last general election to the Assembly, the party won **at least three per cent** of the total number of Assembly seats (any fraction exceeding half counted as one), **or at least three seats**, whichever is more; or
4. at the last general election to the House of the People from the State, the party returned **at least one member for every twenty-five members or any fraction thereof** allotted to that State; or
5. at the last general election to the House of the People from the State, or to the Assembly, the party's candidates secured **not less than eight per cent** of the total valid votes polled in the State.

**Paragraph 6B — conditions for recognition as a National party** (any one of):

1. in **any four or more States**, at the last general election to the House of the People or to the Assembly of the State concerned, the party's candidates secured **not less than six per cent** of the total valid votes polled in each of those States, **and** the party returned **at least four members** to the House of the People at the last such general election from any State or States; or
2. at the last general election to the House of the People, the party won **at least two per cent** of the total seats in the House of the People (any fraction exceeding half counted as one), and its candidates were elected from **not less than three States**; or
3. the party is recognised as a **State party in at least four States**.

**Paragraph 6C — continued recognition.** A party that fails to satisfy paragraph 6A or 6B at the next general election nonetheless **continues to be treated as recognised**, with the question of further continuation determined thereafter under the terms of that paragraph. This is a cushion against a single bad election, and its existence is why a party's loss of seats does not instantly strip its symbol.

Note the structural logic rather than memorising twelve numbers: recognition rewards either a **vote share with a seat floor** or a **seat share**, and national status is built out of multi-State performance.

### 3. Symbols: reserved, free, and what allotment actually means

The Symbols Order classifies symbols as **reserved** — set apart for a recognised party in a State or across India — and **free**, available for allotment to other candidates. Paragraph 17 requires the Commission to publish, by one or more notifications in the Gazette of India, the lists of National parties and their reserved symbols, State parties and their reserved symbols, and free symbols.

Why symbols matter is worth one sentence of reasoning rather than assertion: in an electorate with many languages and varying levels of literacy, a visual identifier reduces the cost of recognising a party on the ballot or the machine. The symbol is an accessibility device before it is a brand.

### 4. Paragraph 15: the split jurisdiction

This is the Commission's best-known quasi-judicial power.

> When the Commission is satisfied on information in its possession that there are rival sections or groups of a recognised political party each of whom claims to be that party, the Commission may, after taking into account all the available facts and circumstances of the case and hearing such representatives of the sections or groups and other persons as desire to be heard, decide that one such rival section or group or none of such rival sections or groups is that recognised political party, and the decision of the Commission shall be binding on all such rival sections or groups.

Read the text for its four features:

1. The trigger is the Commission's **satisfaction on information in its possession** that there are rival sections or groups **each claiming to be that party**.
2. The procedure requires **hearing** representatives of the groups and other persons who desire to be heard — a quasi-judicial obligation, not an administrative convenience.
3. The outcome may be that **one group is**, or that **none of them is**, the recognised party. The "none" option is textual and is routinely forgotten.
4. The decision is **binding** on all the rival sections or groups.

In ***Sadiq Ali v. Election Commission of India*** (1972) the Supreme Court upheld the exercise of this power and the tests the Commission applies, which look to the support the rival groups command in the party's organisational and legislative wings, read against the party's own constitution and the facts of the case. Where the material does not permit a clear finding before an imminent election, the Commission's standard interim course is to **freeze the reserved symbol** and allot the rival groups new names and interim symbols, so that voters are not misled while the claim is decided.

Paragraph 16 deals with the opposite movement — **amalgamation**, where two or more parties, one or more of them recognised, join to form a new party, and the Commission decides whether the new party is a National or State party and which symbol it gets.

### 5. Paragraph 16A: a real enforcement tool that most notes omit

> Notwithstanding anything in this Order, if the Commission is satisfied on information in its possession that a political party, recognised either as a National party or as a State party, has failed or has refused or is refusing or has shown or is showing defiance by its conduct or otherwise (a) to observe the provisions of the Model Code of Conduct for Guidance of Political Parties and Candidates as issued by the Commission in January 1991 or as amended from time to time, or (b) to follow or carry out the lawful directions and instructions of the Commission given with a view to furthering the conduct of free, fair and peaceful elections or safeguarding the interests of the general public and the electorate, the Commission may, after taking into account all the available facts and circumstances and after giving the party reasonable opportunity of showing cause, either **suspend**, subject to such terms as it may deem appropriate, or **withdraw** the recognition of the party.

Three points follow, and the first is the most important in the whole file.

1. **The Model Code is non-statutory, but it is not consequence-free.** Paragraph 16A attaches a real sanction — suspension or withdrawal of recognition, and with it the reserved symbol — to persistent defiance of the Code or of the Commission's lawful directions.
2. The sanction is subject to **natural justice**: a reasonable opportunity of showing cause is mandatory.
3. The power is directed at **recognised** parties, because what it withdraws is recognition. It gives the Commission nothing against an unrecognised party or an individual candidate.

### 6. The boundary of the jurisdiction

| Within the Commission's quasi-judicial field | Outside it |
|---|---|
| Which rival group **is** the recognised party for Symbols Order purposes | Ownership of party property, funds and offices |
| Freezing a reserved symbol and allotting interim symbols | Membership disputes and expulsions as civil claims |
| Deciding recognition and continued recognition | Enforcing a party's internal constitution generally |
| Deciding amalgamation consequences under paragraph 16 | Determining leadership for all legal purposes |
| Suspending or withdrawing recognition under paragraph 16A | Disqualifying a legislator for defection — that is the Speaker or Chairman under the Tenth Schedule |

The last row deserves emphasis because the two questions often arise from the same political event. A party splits; the Commission decides, for symbol purposes, which faction is the party; the presiding officer decides, under the Tenth Schedule, whether particular legislators incurred disqualification. **These are different questions decided by different authorities under different instruments**, and a finding in one does not automatically settle the other.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** A constitutional authority created to run elections has no business deciding who leads a private political association. Paragraph 15 makes the Commission an arbitrator in internal party politics.

**Reply.** Paragraph 15 does not decide leadership at large; it decides **one question for one purpose** — which claimant may use a reserved symbol on the ballot. Somebody must decide it, because a ballot cannot carry two claimants to the same symbol, and leaving it to civil litigation would leave the election itself unresolved. The Commission's jurisdiction is upheld on exactly that basis, and the ordinary civil rights of the disputants survive the decision.

**Residual.** The reply does not remove the difficulty that, in practice, a symbol decision carries enormous political consequence and is often read as a verdict on legitimacy. The reform response is procedural rather than jurisdictional: reasoned, promptly published orders with disclosed tests, and reduction of the interval during which an interim freeze operates.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: quasi-judicial bodies; political parties and the party system; regulation of political finance and organisation.

**Where this lesson is tested.** Registration-versus-recognition is a standard objective distinction. Paragraph 15 and paragraph 16A are strong Mains content, and paragraph 16A in particular converts a weak answer on Model Code enforcement into a strong one.

**Probable framings.** "How do party registration, recognition and symbol adjudication illustrate the quasi-judicial role of the Election Commission?"

**Traps.**

- Treating registration under section 29A as conferring recognition or a symbol.
- Saying the Commission must award the symbol to one faction; paragraph 15 permits "none".
- Saying the Commission decides party property or leadership generally.
- Saying the Model Code has no sanction; paragraph 16A exists.
- Confusing a symbol decision with a Tenth Schedule decision.
- Treating recognition criteria as a single rule rather than alternatives.

**Answer use.** Structure as instrument, criteria, adjudication, sanction, boundary. Naming the paragraph numbers — 6A, 6B, 6C, 15, 16, 16A, 17 — signals command of the instrument itself.

### Revision notes

1. **Registration**: section 29A, Representation of the People Act, 1951.
2. The party's memorandum must affirm **true faith and allegiance to the Constitution and to socialism, secularism and democracy**, and uphold sovereignty, unity and integrity.
3. **Recognition and symbols**: the **Election Symbols (Reservation and Allotment) Order, 1968**.
4. Registration is not recognition; recognition is not a permanent symbol.
5. **Paragraph 6A**: State party criteria — six per cent plus two Assembly seats; six per cent plus one Lok Sabha seat; three per cent of Assembly seats or three seats, whichever is more; one Lok Sabha member for every twenty-five allotted to the State; or eight per cent of valid votes.
6. **Paragraph 6B**: National party criteria — six per cent in four or more States plus four Lok Sabha members; two per cent of Lok Sabha seats from at least three States; or State-party status in at least four States.
7. **Paragraph 6C**: continued recognition despite failure at the next general election.
8. **Paragraph 17**: Gazette notification of lists of National and State parties, their reserved symbols, and free symbols.
9. **Paragraph 15**: split disputes — hearing, decision that one group **or none** is the party, and a binding outcome.
10. ***Sadiq Ali v. Election Commission of India*** (1972) upheld the paragraph 15 jurisdiction and its tests.
11. Standard interim course where a claim cannot be decided in time: **freeze the symbol**, allot interim names and symbols.
12. **Paragraph 16**: amalgamation — status and symbol of the newly formed party.
13. **Paragraph 16A**: suspension or withdrawal of recognition for defiance of the **Model Code** or of the Commission's lawful directions, after a reasonable opportunity of showing cause.
14. Outside the jurisdiction: party property, membership litigation, general leadership determination, and Tenth Schedule disqualification.

### Concept check

**Question:** A recognised State party splits weeks before an Assembly election. Both factions claim the reserved symbol; the Commission cannot resolve the claim in time. One faction also asks the Commission to declare it the lawful owner of the party office and funds. What can and cannot the Commission do, and under which provisions?

**Model answer:** Under **paragraph 15** of the Symbols Order, 1968, the Commission may, after hearing the rival groups and others who desire to be heard, decide that one group or **none** of them is the recognised party, and that decision binds all of them. Where the claim cannot be decided before the poll, its standard course is to **freeze** the reserved symbol and allot interim names and symbols so that voters are not misled, as the jurisdiction upheld in *Sadiq Ali* (1972) contemplates. It cannot declare ownership of the party office or funds: paragraph 15 settles one question — who may use the reserved symbol as the recognised party — and property and contractual claims remain for the civil courts.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading a symbol order as a comprehensive judgment on which faction "is the real party" for every legal purpose. The order is purpose-bound.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** How do party registration, recognition and symbol adjudication illustrate the quasi-judicial role of the Election Commission of India? Discuss with its limits. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (249 words):**
The three functions show the Commission deciding rights between contesting claimants under published criteria, after hearing — the defining marks of quasi-judicial action.

**Registration.** Section 29A of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 requires a memorandum affirming true faith and allegiance to the Constitution and to socialism, secularism and democracy, and the upholding of sovereignty, unity and integrity. The Commission applies statutory conditions to a claim; it does not confer recognition or a symbol.

**Recognition.** Under paragraphs 6A and 6B of the Election Symbols (Reservation and Allotment) Order, 1968, recognition as a State or National party follows alternative performance thresholds — vote shares with seat floors, or seat proportions, or State-party status in four States — with paragraph 6C cushioning a single adverse election. The criteria are published and objective, limiting discretion.

**Adjudication.** Paragraph 15 empowers the Commission, where rival sections each claim to be a recognised party, to decide after hearing them that one group, **or none**, is that party, the decision binding all; *Sadiq Ali v. Election Commission of India* (1972) upheld the jurisdiction. Paragraph 16 governs amalgamation, and paragraph 16A permits suspension or withdrawal of recognition for defiance of the Model Code or lawful directions, after a show-cause opportunity.

**Limits.** The jurisdiction is purpose-bound: it settles symbol and recognition questions, not party property, membership litigation or leadership generally, and defection disqualification belongs to the Speaker or Chairman under the Tenth Schedule.

**Assessment.** The need is procedural — reasoned, promptly published orders and shorter interim freezes.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The word "quasi-judicial" must be earned, not asserted: look for published criteria, a hearing and a binding decision between rival claimants. Scripts that omit the limits paragraph forfeit the top band, since the question expressly asks for them.

### Why the next lesson follows

Paragraph 16A revealed something surprising: a code with no statutory force can nonetheless cost a party its symbol. That paradox is the subject of the next lesson — how the Model Code of Conduct acquired real authority without ever becoming law.

---

## Lesson 15: The Model Code of Conduct

Progress: 15 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Evolution, legal character, operation window, enforcement tools and the statutory-backing debate

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — the Model Code chronology and the Symbols Order paragraph 16A text
CA search: "Model Code of Conduct enforcement statutory status debate"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: how a voluntary code became an operating system

```text
  1960   Kerala Assembly election: a code of conduct is
         experimented with at the State level
            |
  1960s    circulation and learning across national elections
            |
  1968-69  a broader inter-party minimum-code consensus
           develops through the mid-term election period
            |
  1991     the Commission issues a consolidated Model Code of
           Conduct for the Guidance of Political Parties and
           Candidates --- the version paragraph 16A refers to
            |
  since    successive ECI consolidation and additions:
           general conduct, meetings, processions, polling day,
           polling booth, observers, the PARTY IN POWER,
           and election manifestos
            |
  now      a non-statutory code with statutory consequences
           attached at the edges
```

Do not compress this chronology into a single sentence such as "the MCC was enacted in 1968". Nothing was enacted; a consensus accumulated and the Commission consolidated it.

### 1. The legal character, stated exactly

The Model Code is a **consensus-based, non-statutory code**. It is not an Act, it is not subordinate legislation, and the Code itself creates no offence and prescribes no penalty.

Its authority comes from two sources working together:

1. **Article 324** — the Commission's superintendence, direction and control, which supports directions necessary for a free and fair election where the enacted law does not speak.
2. **Political consensus** — the parties themselves agreed to the minimum code, which is why compliance is ordinarily obtained by direction rather than by prosecution.

```text
  "The MCC is a law."                         X
  "MCC breaches are never punishable."        X

  CORRECT: the Code is not a penal statute, but the same
  conduct may independently attract the Representation of
  the People Act, 1951, the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023
  or other law; and defiance may cost a recognised party
  its recognition under paragraph 16A of the Symbols Order.
```

Both errors are common, and the second is more damaging in a Mains answer because it makes the Commission look powerless, which the law does not.

### 2. The operation window

The Code comes into force **from the date the Commission announces the election schedule** and remains in force **until the completion of the election process**.

Two consequences:

- The trigger is the **announcement**, not the statutory notification. This is why Lesson 13 insisted on the distinction.
- The Code applies in the area to which the election relates — a State going to the polls, or the whole country in a general election — not everywhere at all times.

### 3. The content map: eight fields

| Field | Core expectation |
|---|---|
| General conduct | No activity that aggravates existing differences or creates mutual hatred or tension between communities, castes, religious or linguistic groups; criticism confined to policies, programmes, past record and work |
| Meetings | Prior intimation to local authorities of place and time; compliance with restrictive or prohibitory orders; assistance of police against disturbance |
| Processions | Advance notice of route and timing; avoidance of congested routes; prevention of clashes where two processions coincide |
| Polling day | Cooperation with election officials; proper identity slips without party symbols or names on unofficial slips; no serving or distribution of liquor; no unnecessary crowding near camps |
| Polling booth | Entry restricted to voters and those with a valid pass from the Commission |
| Observers | A channel for parties and candidates to bring specific complaints or problems to the Commission's observers |
| Party in power | No use of official machinery or personnel for campaigning; no use of public funds for advertisement projecting achievements at public expense; no announcement of financial grants, promises of new schemes, or ad hoc appointments that may influence voters; no monopolisation of public spaces and rest houses |
| Election manifestos | Manifestos not to contain anything repugnant to the ideals and principles of the Constitution; promises to be accompanied by a broad indication of the rationale and the ways and means of meeting the financial requirements |

The **party in power** section is where the Code does its most distinctive work, because it addresses the one advantage that no statute conveniently reaches: incumbency expressed through ordinary, lawful administration.

### 4. Enforcement: what the Commission actually does

| Tool | Basis | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| Advisory, censure, warning or reprimand | Article 324 supervision and consensus | Immediate public signalling; part of the record |
| Direction to stop or withdraw a campaign act, advertisement or announcement | Article 324 | Prevents the advantage from hardening |
| Direction restraining a campaigner from campaigning for a stated period | Article 324 | Proportionate and time-bound sanction |
| Transfer, replacement or disciplinary reference of a partisan official | Article 324 with RPA 1950 section 13CC | Removes the instrument of misuse |
| Deferral of new schemes, grants, appointments or discretionary benefits | Article 324 and the Code's party-in-power section | Neutralises incumbency advantage |
| Direction to register a case or refer conduct for prosecution | The underlying statute, not the Code | Converts an overlap into a legal proceeding |
| **Suspension or withdrawal of a recognised party's recognition** | **Symbols Order, paragraph 16A** | The heaviest sanction available, after show cause |

The distinctive value is **speed**. A direction that an advertisement be withdrawn or a scheme deferred operates within days, while the campaign is still running. A prosecution, however sound, concludes long after the result.

### 5. The overlap with enacted law

Some conduct the Code prohibits is independently punishable. The overlap is where a loose answer goes wrong, so hold the three layers separately.

```text
  TIER ONE  MCC norm only
    e.g. misuse of official publicity; announcement of new
    discretionary benefits during the window
    -> ECI direction, censure, restraint, deferral

  TIER TWO  MCC norm + statutory wrong
    e.g. bribery, undue influence, appeals on religion, race,
    caste, community or language, promotion of enmity
    -> ECI response PLUS corrupt-practice or electoral-offence
       consequences under the RPA 1951, and offences under the
       Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 where made out

  TIER THREE  service and administrative control
    e.g. a partisan field officer; a prohibited transfer
    -> transfer, replacement, disciplinary reference
```

### 6. The statutory-backing debate, argued on both sides

**The case for making the Code statutory.** Clear offences and sanctions; enforceability through courts; consistency secured by precedent; protection of the Commission from the accusation that it is improvising. A Parliamentary Standing Committee has recommended statutory status.

**The Commission's resistance, and why it is coherent.** Statutory status would route enforcement through courts, and litigation takes longer than an election. A Code that can be enforced only by prosecution becomes unenforceable **during** the campaign — which is the only time enforcement matters. Speed and flexibility are not conveniences here; they are the entire function.

```text
  NON-STATUTORY CODE              FULL STATUTORY CODE
  + fast                          + clearer offences and sanctions
  + flexible                      + enforceability through courts
  + preventive                    + consistency by precedent
  - weak direct penalties         - litigation slows election-time
  - consistency contested           correction
  - limited speaking orders       - rigidity against novel conduct
```

**The hybrid that a strong answer proposes.** Retain non-statutory rapid directions for campaign fairness; codify **timelines** for deciding complaints; require **speaking orders** with reasons; publish a **complaint-and-outcome database** enabling comparison across parties and elections; identify the categories of conduct that **must** be referred for statutory action; and provide proportionate, reviewable sanctions for repeated non-compliance — paragraph 16A already supplying the apex sanction.

Notice that every element of that hybrid is achievable **without legislation**. That is what makes it a good reform proposal rather than a wish.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** A code whose content, interpretation and enforcement all rest with one authority, with no appeal and often no reasons, is incompatible with the rule of law — however benign.

**Reply.** The Code's directions are not beyond review: they are exercises of Article 324 power and are testable on ordinary grounds of legality, relevance, proportionality and fairness. Paragraph 16A, the heaviest sanction, expressly requires a reasonable opportunity of showing cause. And the alternative — no code at all — would leave incumbency advantage entirely unregulated between the announcement and the poll.

**Residual.** Reviewability in principle is weakened by timing in practice: by the time a direction could be challenged, the election is over and the challenge is academic. This is why the serious reform is about **reasons and publication**, not about new powers. A direction that explains itself can be judged by the public even when it can no longer be judged by a court.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: the Election Commission's role; transparency and accountability; constitutional and non-statutory instruments of governance.

**Where this lesson is tested.** A verified Mains question in the index asks directly about the Commission's role in the light of the Model Code's evolution. The legal-character question is also prime objective material.

**Probable framings.** "Discuss the role of the Election Commission of India in the light of the evolution of the Model Code of Conduct."

**Traps.**

- Calling the Code a statute, or saying it was enacted in 1968.
- Saying the Code has no consequences; paragraph 16A and the statutory overlap both exist.
- Saying the Code begins with the statutory notification; it begins with the **announcement**.
- Attributing the whole present Code to the 1960 Kerala experiment.
- Recommending statutory backing without pricing the loss of speed.

**Answer use.** The marks sit in legal character, enforcement tools and the trade-off — not in the chronology. Give evolution one tight paragraph and spend the rest on how the Code actually works.

### Revision notes

1. The Model Code is **consensus-based and non-statutory**.
2. Its authority rests on **Article 324** supervision plus inter-party consensus.
3. **1960**: a code experimented with at the Kerala Assembly election.
4. **1968–69**: a broader inter-party minimum-code consensus develops.
5. **January 1991**: the Commission's consolidated Model Code for the guidance of political parties and candidates — the version paragraph 16A refers to.
6. It operates from the **announcement of the schedule** until the **completion of the election process**.
7. Eight fields: general conduct, meetings, processions, polling day, polling booth, observers, party in power, election manifestos.
8. The **party in power** section targets incumbency advantage expressed through lawful administration.
9. Manifesto guidance: nothing repugnant to constitutional ideals; a broad indication of rationale and of ways and means of financing promises.
10. Tools: advisory, censure, direction to withdraw, campaigning restraint, transfer or replacement of officials, deferral of schemes, direction to prosecute.
11. **Paragraph 16A, Symbols Order, 1968**: suspension or withdrawal of a recognised party's recognition for defiance of the Code or lawful directions, after show cause.
12. Overlap: bribery, undue influence, identity-based appeals and promotion of enmity attract the RPA 1951 and the **Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023**.
13. The Code's comparative advantage is **preventive speed**.
14. A Parliamentary Standing Committee has recommended statutory status; the Commission has resisted.
15. The defensible reform is hybrid: keep rapid directions, add timelines, speaking orders, a published complaint database and mandatory statutory referrals.

### Concept check
