**Question:** Three days after the schedule is announced, a State government advertises a new cash-transfer scheme using public funds, and a candidate makes a speech appealing for votes on the basis of religion. A commentator says the Commission can do nothing because the Model Code is not law. Evaluate.

**Model answer:** The commentator is wrong on both counts. The advertisement falls within the Code's **party-in-power** section, which bars use of public funds for advertisements projecting achievements and the announcement of new schemes during the window; the Code having come into force on the **announcement**, the Commission may direct withdrawal or deferral under its Article 324 supervision, and persistent defiance may attract **paragraph 16A** of the Symbols Order, under which recognition may be suspended or withdrawn after a reasonable opportunity of showing cause. The speech is a **tier-two** matter: besides a Code breach, an appeal for votes on the ground of religion is a corrupt practice under **section 123(3) of the Representation of the People Act, 1951** and may attract offences under other law, so the Commission may additionally direct that a case be registered.

**Misconception to avoid:** Equating "not a statute" with "not enforceable". The Code is enforced by direction and by consequence, and overlapping conduct is separately punishable under enacted law.

### Original Mains practice — 15 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** Discuss the role of the Election Commission of India in the light of the evolution of the Model Code of Conduct. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (250 words):**
**Thesis.** The Model Code is the instrument through which the Commission converts Article 324's free-and-fair-election mandate into enforceable campaign-time standards without waiting for legislation.

**Evolution.** A code was experimented with at the 1960 Kerala Assembly election, circulated and refined through subsequent national elections, and matured into an inter-party minimum-code consensus during the 1968–69 mid-term election period. The Commission consolidated it in January 1991 and has since added rules on meetings, processions, polling day, the party in power, observers and manifestos.

**Legal character.** The Code is consensus-based and non-statutory. Its force comes from Article 324 superintendence, direction and control, together with the agreement of the parties. It operates from the announcement of the schedule until completion of the election process — not from the statutory notification.

**The Commission's role in operation.** It receives complaints, obtains responses, advises, censures, directs withdrawal of advertisements or deferral of new schemes, restrains campaigners for stated periods, transfers or replaces partisan officials using its control over deputed election staff, and refers independently unlawful conduct for prosecution. Under paragraph 16A of the Election Symbols (Reservation and Allotment) Order, 1968, it may suspend or withdraw a recognised party's recognition for defiance, after a show-cause opportunity.

**Limits.** The Code itself creates no offence; consistency and reason-giving are contested; and criminal adjudication remains with ordinary institutions.

**Verdict.** The Code works because it is fast, and it is fast because it is not a statute. The right reform is published timelines, speaking orders and a complaint-outcome database, not conversion into litigation.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The question joins "role" to "evolution", so chronology must serve the analysis rather than occupy it. Cap at the middle band any script that spends more than a quarter of its words on dates, and reward scripts that name paragraph 16A, which most will omit.

### Why the next lesson follows

Three lessons have now relied on a phrase — "the Commission acted under Article 324 because the law was silent". That is a real doctrine with real limits, and it is time to state it exactly, because stated loosely it becomes the most dangerous sentence in this topic.

---
## Lesson 16: Article 324 as Plenary Power — and Its Limits

Progress: 16 / 20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: *Mohinder Singh Gill*, *A.C. Jose*, *Kanhiya Lal Omar*, natural justice, and the negative-power board

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — the Article 324 judgments read for their exact holdings
CA search: "Article 324 plenary power supplement not supplant"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson; the authorities below are long settled
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: the decision test, applied to any claim of Article 324 power

```text
             Does the Constitution, an Act or a Rule
             SPEAK to the question?
                       |
          +------------+-------------+
          |                          |
         YES                        NO / a genuine
          |                         operational gap
          v                               |
  THE COMMISSION MUST FOLLOW IT.          v
  It may supplement the detail      ARTICLE 324 MAY BE INVOKED
  but may NOT contradict.                 |
          |                               v
          |                   PURPOSE TEST: is the action
          |                   directed at a free and fair
          |                   election?
          |                               |
          |                               v
          |                   LIMITS: legality, relevance,
          |                   fairness, reasons,
          |                   proportionality, judicial review
          |                               |
          +-------------+-----------------+
                        v
               LAWFUL EXERCISE OF POWER
```

The left branch is the one candidates forget. Most wrong answers on this topic are produced by jumping straight to the right branch.

### 1. The problem the doctrine solves

An election is a short event with an unlimited capacity to generate situations nobody legislated for. Ballot papers are snatched; a machine fails; a candidate dies; a natural disaster strikes a district on polling day; a new technology of campaigning appears that no provision anticipated.

If the Commission could act only where a statute expressly authorised it, every novel situation would stop the election. If it could act however it liked, the Representation of the People Acts would be advisory. Indian law chose a middle position, and the two cases that define it are best learnt as a pair.

### 2. *Mohinder Singh Gill v. Chief Election Commissioner* (1978): the reservoir

The dispute arose from a parliamentary constituency where, during counting, ballot papers were destroyed and the process was disrupted; the Commission cancelled the poll and ordered a fresh one. The challenge was that no statutory provision authorised cancellation in those circumstances.

The Court's answer, in substance:

- **Article 324 is a reservoir of power.** Where the enacted law is silent, the Commission is not disabled; the article operates to enable action necessary for the conduct of a free and fair election.
- **The power is purposive.** It exists for the conduct of elections, and must be exercised for that end, not for collateral purposes.
- **It is not lawless.** Article 324 "cannot be exercised arbitrarily"; the rule of law, relevance and the requirements of fairness apply.
- **Natural justice applies flexibly.** Orders with civil consequences ordinarily attract fairness, though the **content and timing** of a hearing vary with the exigency of an election.

The case also supplies the familiar proposition that an order must be judged on the reasons it records, not on reasons supplied afterwards.

### 3. *A.C. Jose v. Sivan Pillai* (1984): the limit

This is the indispensable companion case, and the one that makes the doctrine usable rather than open-ended.

The Commission had directed the use of voting machines at some polling stations in a Kerala Assembly constituency, at a time when the Representation of the People Act, 1951 and the Conduct of Elections Rules provided for voting by ballot paper and contained no provision for machines. The Court held the direction impermissible and set aside the election in those circumstances.

The rule it states is the formula to memorise:

> Where there is an Act and express Rules made thereunder, the Commission cannot override the Act or the Rules; it may **supplement** them, but it cannot **supplant** them.

```text
  LAW SPEAKS      ->  the Commission must obey
  LAW IS SILENT   ->  Article 324 may fill the operational gap
  EVERY CASE      ->  free-and-fair purpose + recorded reasons
                      + fairness + judicial review
```

Two refinements worth adding in a Mains answer. First, the decision is **not** an objection to voting machines as such; the deficiency was the absence of legal authority at that time, and Parliament subsequently amended the law to provide for voting machines. Second, the case shows the correct remedy for a legal gap: **legislate**, do not improvise.

### 4. *Kanhiya Lal Omar v. R.K. Trivedi* (1985): the doctrine applied constructively

Here the challenge was to the Election Symbols (Reservation and Allotment) Order, 1968 — on the argument that the Commission had no power to issue such an Order and, in particular, to decide disputes between rival factions.

The Court upheld the Order. The reasoning is instructive because it shows the doctrine working in the Commission's favour: the subject matter — specification of symbols, recognition of parties, resolution of competing claims to a symbol — is integral to the conduct of elections, and is supported by Article 324 read with the statutory scheme, including the provisions governing political parties and the allotment of symbols.

Put the three cases together and the doctrine is complete:

| Case | Function in the doctrine |
|---|---|
| *Mohinder Singh Gill* (1978) | Establishes the reservoir, the purpose and the controls |
| *A.C. Jose* (1984) | Establishes the limit: supplement, never supplant |
| *Kanhiya Lal Omar* (1985) | Shows the reservoir validly used to build a durable regulatory instrument |

### 5. Natural justice in an election: flexible, not optional

Because election decisions are taken under extreme time pressure, the fairness question is not "was there a hearing?" but "was the procedure fair **given what the situation allowed**?"

| Context | Procedural expectation |
|---|---|
| Immediate violence or booth capturing | An urgent protective order may precede any hearing; reasons recorded contemporaneously |
| Censure or a campaigning restraint where time exists | Notice, opportunity to respond, and a reasoned order |
| Deletion of a name from the roll | Statutory notice, inquiry and opportunity to be heard under section 22 of the RPA 1950, with an appeal |
| Post-result technical verification | The prescribed request, presence of parties, and certification |

The honest qualification: urgency can **shape** natural justice but cannot **erase** legality. If urgency became a formula, every unexplained order could be defended by asserting that an election was in progress.

### 6. The negative-power board: what the Commission cannot lawfully do

This board is the single most useful revision artefact in the topic, because almost every overclaim in an answer appears on it.

| The Commission **cannot** | The correct route |
|---|---|
| Conduct panchayat or municipal elections under Article 324 | State Election Commission, Articles 243K and 243ZA |
| Contradict an enacted Act or express Rule | Amendment by Parliament or the rule-making authority, or judicial review |
| Create a new candidate disqualification | The Constitution and the Representation of the People Act, 1951 |
| Finally declare a returned candidate's election void | High Court election petition under Article 329(b) and the RPA 1951 |
| Decide a Tenth Schedule defection question | Speaker or Chairman, subject to judicial review |
| Ordinarily delimit all constituencies by itself | A Delimitation Commission under the applicable Delimitation Act |
| Conclusively determine a person's citizenship | The competent authority under the Citizenship Act, 1955 |
| Convert the Model Code into a penal statute | Parliament |
| Treat an allegation of machine tampering as established | Evidence and the prescribed verification procedure |
| Decide ownership of a political party's property or funds | Civil courts |

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** "Supplement, not supplant" is easy to recite and hard to apply. Almost any innovation can be presented as filling a gap, because statutes are never exhaustive. The test therefore licenses whatever the Commission wishes to do.

**Reply.** The test is more determinate than it appears, because it asks a prior question: **is there an express provision on this point?** In *A.C. Jose* there was — the law prescribed voting by ballot paper — and the innovation failed immediately. The test only becomes difficult where the law is genuinely silent, and in that zone the remaining controls are real: purpose, relevance, recorded reasons, fairness and reviewability.

**Residual.** The difficulty is not eliminated. The hardest cases are those where a statute speaks in general terms and the Commission adds detail that changes outcomes — as the roll-revision litigation in Lesson 11 showed, where the Court had to decide whether an intensive revision operated **within** section 21(3) or beyond it. The Court's answer there — that the exercise moved within "the precise statutory contours provided by Section 21(3)" and did not supplant the framework — is the doctrine applied, not abandoned.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: powers of constitutional bodies; judicial review of administrative action; separation of powers.

**Where this lesson is tested.** It is the doctrinal core of any question asking what the Commission "can" do, and it rescues answers from both overclaim and underclaim.

**Probable framings.** "Article 324 is a reservoir of electoral power, not a source of power contrary to law. Explain."

**Traps.**

- Quoting *Mohinder Singh Gill* for unlimited power while omitting *A.C. Jose*.
- Saying *A.C. Jose* held voting machines unconstitutional. It held the direction unauthorised **at that time**; Parliament later provided for them.
- Saying natural justice does not apply during an election.
- Using "plenary" as a slogan without the purpose and limit tests.
- Forgetting that Article 324 cannot create a disqualification.

**Answer use.** Never write "plenary power" alone. Write the pair: reservoir **and** supplement-not-supplant. Two cases, one sentence, and the examiner knows you have the doctrine rather than the phrase.

### Revision notes

1. *Mohinder Singh Gill v. Chief Election Commissioner* (1978): Article 324 is a **reservoir of power** where the enacted law is silent.
2. The power is **purposive** — directed at the conduct of free and fair elections.
3. It is **not arbitrary**: legality, relevance, reasons and fairness apply.
4. Natural justice applies with content and timing adapted to election exigency.
5. An order is judged on the reasons it records.
6. *A.C. Jose v. Sivan Pillai* (1984): where an Act and express Rules exist, the Commission may **supplement but not supplant** them.
7. *A.C. Jose* concerned voting machines used without legal authority at that time; the law was later amended to provide for them.
8. The correct response to a legal gap is legislation, not improvisation.
9. *Kanhiya Lal Omar v. R.K. Trivedi* (1985) upheld the **Symbols Order, 1968** and the Commission's authority over recognition and symbols.
10. Decision test: does the law speak? If yes, follow it; if no, Article 324 may fill the gap, subject to purpose and limits.
11. Negative-power board: no local-body elections, no contradiction of statute, no new disqualification, no voiding of an election, no Tenth Schedule decision, no general delimitation, no citizenship determination, no penal code-making, no assumed tampering, no party-property adjudication.
12. The hard cases are general statutory language plus detailed administrative addition; the roll-revision judgment is the modern illustration.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Conduct of Elections Rules prescribe a particular procedure for a step in counting. The Commission issues an instruction adopting a different procedure, explaining that the prescribed one is slow and that Article 324 permits it to act for a free and fair election. Is the instruction valid?

**Model answer:** No. The decision test begins with whether the law speaks to the question, and here express Rules do. *A.C. Jose v. Sivan Pillai* (1984) holds that where an Act and express Rules exist, the Commission may **supplement** them but may not **supplant** them; *Mohinder Singh Gill* (1978) supplies a reservoir only where the enacted law is **silent**, and even then the exercise must be purposive, reasoned and fair. Administrative inconvenience is not a gap. The lawful courses are to work within the Rules, to issue supplementary directions consistent with them, or to seek their amendment by the rule-making authority.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "free and fair elections" as a master key. The purpose test is a limit on an available power, not a substitute for the prior question of whether the power is available at all.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** "Article 324 is a reservoir of electoral power, not a source of power contrary to law." Explain. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (149 words):**
Article 324 vests superintendence, direction and control of rolls and of elections in the Election Commission, and *Mohinder Singh Gill v. Chief Election Commissioner* (1978) read it as a **reservoir**: where enacted law is silent, the Commission may act as the conduct of a free and fair election requires, as when it ordered a fresh poll after ballot papers were destroyed. The reservoir has banks. The power is purposive, must be exercised on recorded and relevant reasons, attracts natural justice with content adapted to election exigency, and remains subject to judicial review. Decisively, *A.C. Jose v. Sivan Pillai* (1984) held that where an Act and express Rules exist the Commission may **supplement** but not **supplant** them, invalidating the use of voting machines before the law provided for them. *Kanhiya Lal Omar v. R.K. Trivedi* (1985) shows the lawful constructive use of the same power in the Symbols Order, 1968.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** Both halves of the quotation must be evidenced by a named case. A script citing only *Mohinder Singh Gill* has explained the reservoir and ignored the quotation's second clause, and cannot exceed half marks.

### Why the next lesson follows

One entry on the negative-power board deserves its own treatment, because a verified Prelims question in the index turns on it: the Commission participates in delimitation but does not own it, and the distinction is easy to state and easy to get wrong.

---

## Lesson 17: Delimitation — Assistance, Not Ownership

Progress: 17 / 20 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The Delimitation Commission, the ECI's role, Article 329(a), the four-commission fact and its limits

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — RPA 1950 Part II provisions and Laxmikanth's note on the Delimitation Commission Acts
CA search: "delimitation commission composition statutory role Election Commission"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson; future readjustment questions belong with the study of Parliament
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: who draws the map

```text
  PARLIAMENT
     enacts a Delimitation Act under Article 327
          |
          v
  CENTRAL GOVERNMENT constitutes the
  DELIMITATION COMMISSION
          |
          +--- Chairperson: a serving or retired Judge of
          |    the Supreme Court
          +--- ex officio: the Chief Election Commissioner,
          |    or an Election Commissioner nominated by him
          +--- ex officio: the State Election Commissioner
               of the State concerned
          |
          v
  draft proposals -> public notice, sittings, objections
          |
          v
  FINAL ORDER published in the Gazette
     . has the FORCE OF LAW
     . its validity cannot be called in question
       in any court  [Art 329(a) and the statutory scheme]
          |
          v
  ELECTION COMMISSION implements the electoral map:
     rolls, constituency-wise arrangements, conduct
```

Read the diagram as an argument about ownership. The Commission is **inside** the Delimitation Commission as an ex officio member and **downstream** of it as the implementing authority. It is not the body that draws the map.

### 1. Why delimitation is separated from the election manager

Drawing constituency boundaries determines, to a significant degree, who can win them. Had the power been given to the body that conducts elections, the same authority would both design the field and umpire the game. Separating the functions — a judge-chaired commission for the map, a constitutional commission for the contest — distributes a power that is too consequential to concentrate.

A second reason is finality. The electoral map must be stable for a decade or more, so the statute makes the final order binding and insulates it from litigation. An authority that must act quickly during elections is not the right body to issue orders designed to be unchallengeable for a decade.

### 2. The composition, read for what it signals

The Delimitation Commission constituted under the applicable Delimitation Act is chaired by a **serving or retired Judge of the Supreme Court**, with the **Chief Election Commissioner or an Election Commissioner nominated by him** and the **State Election Commissioner of the State concerned** as ex officio members. Associate members — sitting members of Parliament and of the State Legislative Assembly — are associated with the exercise for their States, without a right to vote on or sign the final order.

Each element signals something:

- a **judge** chairs because the exercise must be, and appear, apolitical;
- the **ECI** is present because it holds the roll and population data and must implement the result;
- the **State Election Commissioner** is present because local-body delimitation in the State has to align;
- **associate members** are heard because elected representatives know the territory, but they do not control the outcome.

### 3. The Representation of the People Act, 1950's own delimitation provisions

The Delimitation Acts are separate statutes, but the RPA 1950 carries the standing framework within which delimitation orders live:

| Section | Content |
|---|---|
| 3, 4, 7 | Allocation of seats in the House of the People and in State Legislative Assemblies, and the filling of seats and parliamentary constituencies |
| 8 | **Consolidation** of delimitation orders |
| 9 | Power of the **Election Commission** to maintain the Delimitation Order **up to date** |
| 9A, 9B (omitted) | Formerly gave the Election Commission power to determine constituencies reserved for Scheduled Tribes in certain States and in Tripura; the subject now rests on **section 7(1A)-(1C)** and the **Second Schedule** for reserved-seat numbers, with identification of the specific reserved constituencies a function of the statutory **Delimitation Commission** |
| 12, 13 | Power to alter or amend orders, and the procedure as to orders delimiting constituencies |

Section 9 is the nuance to carry. The Commission has a **maintenance** power — keeping the Delimitation Order current, for instance to reflect changes in the boundaries of administrative units — which is categorically different from conducting a delimitation exercise. Maintenance is housekeeping; delimitation is map-making.

### 4. The four-commission fact, and the inference it does not support

A verified Prelims question in the index asks how many Delimitation Commissions the Government of India had constituted till December 2023. The answer rests on the enactment of **Delimitation Commission Acts in 1952, 1962 and 1972 and the Delimitation Act of 2002**.

| Statutory basis | Broad census linkage |
|---|---|
| Act of 1952 | 1951 Census |
| Act of 1962 | 1961 Census |
| Act of 1972 | 1971 Census |
| Act of 2002 | 2001 Census |

Now the limits, which matter more than the number.

1. The count refers to **all-India** Commissions constituted under those Acts. It does not mean that the Election Commission constituted or solely operated them; the Central Government constitutes a Delimitation Commission.
2. It does not mean that no other delimitation work has occurred. A later **State-specific exercise** was conducted for Jammu and Kashmir, constituted in 2020 under the post-reorganisation statutory framework and completing its final order in 2022. A State-specific exercise under a specific statutory framework is not an additional all-India Commission in the sense the question uses.
3. Readjustment of seats has been affected by constitutional amendments freezing the allocation by reference to a specified census. The **current and future readjustment debate belongs with the study of Parliament and federal design**, and should be cross-referred rather than argued out in an Election Commission answer.

### 5. Article 329(a) in operation

Article 329(a) provides that the validity of any **law** relating to the delimitation of constituencies or the allotment of seats to such constituencies, made or purporting to be made under Article 327 or Article 328, **shall not be called in question in any court**. The statutory scheme adds that the published final orders of a Delimitation Commission have the **force of law** and shall not be called in question before any court.

The purpose is practical. If boundaries could be litigated, every general election would begin with map litigation, and the result would be either paralysis or elections held under boundaries that a court might later invalidate — with every seat drawn from them exposed.

The qualification to state: this is a bar on questioning **validity**; it does not convert the Delimitation Commission into an authority free of all legal discipline. The Commission's own statutory procedure — public notice, sittings, objections, association of members — is the accountability mechanism the design relies on, which is why a final order must be distinguished from a mere recommendation.

That distinction is worth a sentence of its own: where a Delimitation Commission makes **recommendations** beyond its final constituency allocation, those recommendations do not become law merely because the Commission proposed them.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Immunising boundary decisions from judicial scrutiny is indefensible. Boundary drawing is exactly where manipulation is cheapest and hardest to detect, and an unreviewable order is an invitation.

**Reply.** The immunity is paired with structural safeguards rather than standing alone: a judge chairs; the Election Commission and the State Election Commissioner sit ex officio; elected representatives are associated; and the process requires publication of proposals, public sittings and consideration of objections. The alternative — judicial redrawing of constituency boundaries — would transfer a profoundly political allocation to courts without improving its legitimacy.

**Residual.** The safeguards are procedural and prospective; once a final order issues, a person who believes the process was captured has no judicial route to say so. The honest position is that the design trades individual remedy for systemic stability, and that the trade is only as good as the composition and transparency of the Commission that makes the order.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: constitutional and statutory bodies; representation and federal design; devolution of seats.

**Where this lesson is tested.** A verified Prelims question in the index asks for the number of Delimitation Commissions. The ownership boundary is also the kind of distinction that decides a close option.

**Probable framings.** Objective questions on composition and count; Mains questions on delimitation usually belong with Parliament and federalism.

**Traps.**

- "The ECI delimits constituencies." It participates and implements.
- "The ECI constitutes the Delimitation Commission." The Central Government does.
- "The Delimitation Commission is a constitutional body." It is **statutory**.
- "Article 329(b) bars challenges to delimitation." It is Article 329(a).
- Treating the Jammu and Kashmir exercise as a fifth all-India Commission.
- Treating a Delimitation Commission's ancillary recommendations as law.

**Answer use.** Use one sentence of ownership — judge-chaired statutory commission, ECI ex officio and implementing — and then cross-refer the readjustment debate instead of importing it.

### Revision notes

1. Delimitation is conducted by a **statutory Delimitation Commission**, not by the Election Commission.
2. The Commission is constituted by the **Central Government** under a Delimitation Act enacted by Parliament under Article 327.
3. Composition: a **serving or retired Supreme Court Judge** as Chairperson; the **CEC or a nominated EC** ex officio; the **State Election Commissioner** of the State concerned ex officio.
4. **Associate members** — MPs and MLAs of the State — are associated but do not vote on or sign the final order.
5. The final order is published in the Gazette and has the **force of law**.
6. **Article 329(a)** bars any court from questioning the validity of a delimitation or seat-allotment law made under Article 327 or 328.
7. **RPA 1950 sections 3, 4 and 7**: allocation of seats and constituencies.
8. **Section 8**: consolidation of delimitation orders; **section 9**: the ECI's power to keep the Delimitation Order up to date.
9. **Sections 9A and 9B are omitted**; reserved-seat numbers for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes now rest on **section 7(1A)-(1C)** and the **Second Schedule**, and identifying the specific reserved constituencies is a function of the statutory **Delimitation Commission**, not a current ECI power.
10. **Sections 12 and 13**: alteration or amendment of orders and the procedure for orders delimiting constituencies.
11. Delimitation Commission Acts of **1952, 1962, 1972** and the **Delimitation Act, 2002**.
12. Census linkage: 1951, 1961, 1971 and 2001 respectively.
13. A **State-specific** exercise for Jammu and Kashmir was constituted in 2020 and completed its final order in 2022; it is not an additional all-India Commission in that count.
14. Maintenance of an existing order is not the same as conducting a delimitation.
15. Ancillary recommendations of a Delimitation Commission do not become law by being proposed.

### Concept check

**Question:** A newspaper reports that "the Election Commission has redrawn the constituencies of a State and the aggrieved residents are moving the High Court". Identify every legal inaccuracy and state the correct position.

**Model answer:** Two inaccuracies. First, constituencies are delimited by a **statutory Delimitation Commission** constituted by the Central Government under a Delimitation Act, chaired by a serving or retired Supreme Court Judge, with the Chief Election Commissioner or a nominated Election Commissioner and the State Election Commissioner as ex officio members; the Election Commission participates and later implements, and under section 9 of the Representation of the People Act, 1950 maintains the order up to date — it does not redraw the map. Second, the remedy described is unavailable: **Article 329(a)** bars any court from questioning the validity of a law relating to delimitation of constituencies or allotment of seats, and the published final order has the force of law and cannot be called in question in any court. The accountability mechanism is the statutory process of published proposals, public sittings and consideration of objections.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the Election Commission's presence on the Delimitation Commission as authorship of its orders. Ex officio membership is participation, not ownership.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** "Delimitation in India is insulated from courts and distributed among institutions." Examine the design and its justification. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (150 words):**
The design separates map-making from election management and then protects the map. Parliament legislates under Article 327; the Central Government constitutes a Delimitation Commission chaired by a serving or retired Supreme Court Judge, with the Chief Election Commissioner or a nominated Election Commissioner and the State Election Commissioner as ex officio members, and MPs and MLAs associated without a vote. Its final order, published in the Gazette, has the force of law, and **Article 329(a)** bars courts from questioning the validity of delimitation legislation. The justification is twofold: distributing the power stops the umpire designing the field, and insulation prevents every general election beginning with boundary litigation that could unsettle every seat. The cost is that a person alleging a captured process has no judicial remedy, so legitimacy rests entirely on composition, published proposals, public sittings and reasoned consideration of objections — a procedural guarantee rather than an adjudicatory one.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** Both nouns in the question must be answered — insulation (Article 329(a) and force of law) and distribution (the composition). A script that handles only one of them stops at half marks regardless of quality.

### Why the next lesson follows

The map and the contest are settled. What remains is the aftermath: when a member's disqualification is questioned, or a result is challenged, the Commission's role changes character entirely — from administrator to adviser, and then to bystander.

---
## Lesson 18: Disqualification Advice and the Election-Dispute Boundary

Progress: 18 / 20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Articles 103 and 192, RPA 1951 sections 8 to 11B and 123, the election-petition route, and the forum map

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — RPA 1951 Part II and the election-petition provisions cross-checked
CA search: "election petition disqualification Representation of the People Act forum"
CA found: none relied on in this lesson; the authorities below are decided
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: four different questions, four different decision-makers

```text
  QUESTION                              DECIDED BY
  ----------------------------------    --------------------------------
  Has a SITTING member incurred a       President (MP) / Governor (MLA),
  disqualification under Art 102 /      who SHALL obtain the opinion of
  Art 191?                              the ELECTION COMMISSION and
                                        SHALL act according to it
                                        [Arts 103, 192]

  Has a member incurred disqualifi-     SPEAKER / CHAIRMAN
  cation by DEFECTION?                  [Tenth Schedule], subject to
                                        judicial review

  Is the ELECTION of a returned         HIGH COURT on an election
  candidate void?                       petition [Art 329(b);
                                        RPA 1951 ss.80, 80A, 100],
                                        appeal to the Supreme Court
                                        [s.116A]

  Is a PRESIDENTIAL or VICE-            SUPREME COURT
  PRESIDENTIAL election disputed?       [Article 71]
```

Four rows, four authorities. The Commission appears decisively in one of them, invisibly in two, and administers the poll in all of them.

### 1. Articles 103 and 192: the binding opinion

Article 103 provides that if a question arises whether a **member of either House of Parliament** has become subject to any of the disqualifications in Article 102(1), the question shall be referred for the decision of the **President**, whose decision shall be final; and before giving a decision, the President **shall obtain the opinion of the Election Commission and shall act according to such opinion**. Article 192 makes identical provision for a **member of a House of a State Legislature**, with the **Governor** as the deciding authority.

Three features repay precise statement:

1. The provision applies to a **sitting member**, and to a question arising **after** election. A pre-election disqualification is tested at nomination and, afterwards, in an election petition.
2. The President's or Governor's decision is **final**, yet is controlled by the Commission's opinion, which is **binding**. Formal authority and substantive determination are deliberately separated.
3. Article 102(1) and Article 191(1) supply the grounds — office of profit, unsound mind as declared by a competent court, undischarged insolvency, non-citizenship or acknowledgement of allegiance to a foreign State, and disqualification under **any law made by Parliament**. That last clause is the doorway through which the Representation of the People Act, 1951 enters.
4. Defection is expressly excluded from this route: Article 102(2) and Article 191(2) place disqualification on the ground of defection under the Tenth Schedule, decided by the presiding officer.

### 2. Statutory disqualifications: RPA 1951, sections 8 to 11B

| Section | Ground | Precision to preserve |
|---|---|---|
| **8** | Conviction for specified offences; a sentence of imprisonment of **two years or more** disqualifies from the date of conviction and for a **further six years** from release | Sub-sections 8(1) to 8(3) operate; **8(4)** is gone after *Lily Thomas* |
| **8A** | Disqualification on the ground of **corrupt practices** | The President decides on the **Election Commission's opinion** |
| **9** | **Dismissal** from government service for corruption or disloyalty to the State | Dismissal, not mere resignation or compulsory retirement |
| **9A** | Subsisting **government contracts** for supply of goods or execution of works | A contract with the appropriate Government |
| **10** | Holding an **office** under a government company | Managing agent, manager or secretary of a company in which the Government has not less than twenty-five per cent share |
| **10A** | Failure to **lodge an account of election expenses** in time and in the prescribed manner without good reason | The **Election Commission** declares the disqualification, for **three years** |
| **11** | **Removal or reduction** of a disqualification by the Election Commission | A genuine remedy, not merely a power |
| **11A** | Disqualification arising out of **conviction and corrupt practices** | Links the sanction to a disqualification period |
| **11B** | Removal of disqualification | Companion remedial provision |

Section 10A is the one place in this list where the **Commission itself disqualifies**. Note the contrast with section 8A, where the Commission's role is to supply the opinion on which the President acts. That difference — decider in one, adviser in the other — is the kind of discrimination that separates a strong answer from a competent one.

### 3. *Lily Thomas v. Union of India* (2013): the sitting-member shield removed

Section 8(4) of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 had provided that a disqualification under section 8 would not take effect against a **sitting member** for three months, and if an appeal was filed within that period, until the appeal was disposed of.

In ***Lily Thomas v. Union of India*** (2013) the Supreme Court struck down section 8(4) as beyond the legislative competence conferred by Articles 102 and 191, holding that Parliament could prescribe grounds of disqualification but could not defer their operation for sitting members alone.

```text
  BEFORE Lily Thomas          AFTER Lily Thomas
  ------------------------    ------------------------------------
  conviction of a sitting     conviction operates IMMEDIATELY
  member -> shield for 3      ------------------------------------
  months and during appeal    an appellate STAY OF THE CONVICTION
                              (not merely of the sentence) suspends
                              the disqualification
```

Two precision points. First, *Lily Thomas* struck down **section 8(4) only**, not section 8. Second, the surviving relief is a stay of the **conviction**; a stay of the **sentence** alone does not remove the disqualification. Candidates routinely blur these, and both are examinable.

### 4. Corrupt practices: section 123

Section 123 of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 contains a **closed statutory list**, not a general morality clause. In outline:

| Clause | Corrupt practice |
|---|---|
| 123(1) | **Bribery** — a gift, offer or promise of gratification to induce a person to stand or not to stand, to withdraw, or to vote or refrain from voting |
| 123(2) | **Undue influence** — any direct or indirect interference, or attempt to interfere, with the free exercise of any electoral right |
| 123(3) and 123(3A) | Appeals to vote or refrain on the ground of **religion, race, caste, community or language**, and promotion of **enmity or hatred** on those grounds for furthering the prospects of an election |
| 123(4) | Publication of **false statements of fact** about the personal character or conduct of a candidate, reasonably calculated to prejudice his prospects |
| 123(5) | Hiring or procuring **vehicles** for the conveyance of electors |
| 123(6) | Incurring or authorising **expenditure in excess of the ceiling** |
| 123(7) | Obtaining or procuring the **assistance of government servants** of specified classes for the furtherance of a candidate's prospects |

Two analytical points that recent questions have targeted.

**Undue influence has a target, and the target is the voter.** Section 123(2) is about interference with the **free exercise of an electoral right**. A candidate's personal enrichment, however unexplained, is a matter for anti-corruption and tax law, and for disclosure obligations. It does not become undue influence without proof of interference with voter freedom — although a specific inducement funded by illicit assets could separately satisfy **bribery** under 123(1) if its elements are proved.

**The remedy for unexplained enrichment is disclosure, not reinterpretation.** ***Union of India v. Association for Democratic Reforms*** (2002) and ***People's Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India*** (2003) established the voter's right to know candidates' criminal, financial and educational antecedents under Article 19(1)(a); the resulting affidavit regime, and its subsequent expansion, is the constitutionally appropriate response.

### 5. The election-petition route, section by section

| Section | Function |
|---|---|
| **80** | No election shall be called in question except by an **election petition** presented in accordance with the Act |
| **80A** | The **High Court** has jurisdiction to try an election petition |
| **81** | Petition presented within **forty-five days** from the date of election of the returned candidate, by any candidate or any elector |
| **83** | The petition must contain a **concise statement of material facts** and set out **full particulars of any corrupt practice** alleged, verified as a pleading |
| **86** | The High Court **shall dismiss** a petition that does not comply with sections 81, 82 or 117 |
| **98** | Final orders: dismiss the petition; declare the election **void**; or declare the election void **and** declare the petitioner or another candidate duly elected |
| **99** | Where a corrupt practice is proved, the order shall **name** the persons proved to have been guilty, after giving them notice and an opportunity to be heard |
| **100** | **Grounds** for declaring an election void |
| **116A** | **Appeal to the Supreme Court** on any question of law or fact, within **thirty days** |

The grounds in section 100 are worth one line of structure. They include that the returned candidate was **not qualified or was disqualified**; that a **corrupt practice** was committed by the returned candidate, his election agent or with his consent; that a **nomination was improperly rejected**; and, where the result **has been materially affected**, improper acceptance of a nomination, a corrupt practice by someone else in the candidate's interest, improper reception, refusal or rejection of votes, or **non-compliance** with the Constitution, the Act, the Rules or orders made under the Act.

Note the architecture: some grounds are **absolute**, while others require proof that the result was **materially affected**. That asymmetry is the heart of most election-petition litigation, and naming it is a mark of genuine command.

### 6. Two case anchors

***N.P. Ponnuswami v. Returning Officer*** (1952) held that Article 329(b) provides an **exclusive** remedy, so an election cannot be called in question midstream by writ; the word "election" covers the entire process from notification to declaration, and interference during it is barred. Later doctrine refines rather than reverses this, permitting intervention that **facilitates** rather than **retards** the electoral process.

***Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain*** (1975) struck down the clause of the Thirty-ninth Amendment that had sought to place the Prime Minister's election beyond the ordinary adjudicatory route, holding **free and fair elections and judicial review** to be part of the basic structure. Article 329A, inserted by that amendment, was later **omitted** by the Forty-fourth Amendment, 1978.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** The architecture is strong on paper and weak in fact. An election petition filed within forty-five days may be decided years later; a corrupt-practice finding that arrives after the House has dissolved voids nothing that matters. The obvious fix is to empower the Commission to disqualify for corrupt practice directly.

**Reply.** The obvious fix is the wrong one, for a reason of principle. A corrupt-practice finding carries a **six-year civic disqualification** and must therefore rest on proof taken in an adversarial proceeding, with the right to be heard that section 99 expressly guarantees before anyone is named. Giving the body that conducts the election the power to adjudicate guilt would also collapse the separation between conducting and judging an election that Article 329(b) was designed to preserve.

**Residual.** The delay is nonetheless a real failure, and the right reform targets **time**, not **threshold**: dedicated election benches, statutory timelines enforced rather than aspirational, digitised service and evidence, and coordination between the court, the Commission and the President or Governor so that the consequential stages do not add further months. Simplify the sequence and the timeline; do not simplify the proof.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: disqualification of members; separation of powers; judicial remedies in electoral matters.

**Where this lesson is tested.** Three verified Mains questions in the index — on grounds and remedies, on simplifying corrupt-practice disqualification, and on election disputes with case law — draw directly on it, and a fourth on corrupt practices and disproportionate assets turns on section 123(2).

**Probable framings.** "On what grounds can a people's representative be disqualified under the RPA 1951, and what remedies are available?" "Discuss the procedures for deciding election disputes and the grounds for declaring an election void."

**Traps.**

- Citing the RPA **1950** for disqualification or election petitions.
- Saying *Lily Thomas* struck down section 8; it struck down **section 8(4)**.
- Saying a stay of **sentence** removes the disqualification; it must be a stay of **conviction**.
- Filing an election petition before the Election Commission; it goes to the **High Court**.
- Saying the Commission can disqualify for a corrupt practice; it cannot, though it disqualifies under **section 10A** for failure to lodge expense accounts.
- Treating disproportionate assets as automatically **undue influence**.
- Routing a Presidential-election dispute to a High Court instead of the Supreme Court under Article 71.

**Answer use.** For "grounds and remedies", build two columns and keep them separate: grounds in sections 8 to 10A, remedies in section 11, appellate stay of conviction, Articles 103 and 192 with the binding opinion, and the petition route with appeal under section 116A.

### Revision notes

1. **Articles 103 and 192**: a sitting member's disqualification question goes to the President or Governor, who **shall obtain the Election Commission's opinion and act according to it**.
2. **Articles 102(1) and 191(1)**: office of profit, unsound mind, undischarged insolvency, non-citizenship or foreign allegiance, and disqualification under any law made by Parliament.
3. Defection is excluded from that route; the **Speaker or Chairman** decides under the Tenth Schedule, subject to judicial review.
4. **Section 8**: conviction-based disqualification; **two years or more** means disqualification from conviction and **six years** from release.
5. **Section 8(4) struck down** in ***Lily Thomas v. Union of India*** (2013); section 8 itself survives.
6. Only a stay of the **conviction**, not of the sentence, suspends the disqualification.
7. **Section 8A**: corrupt-practice disqualification, decided by the President on the Commission's opinion.
8. **Sections 9, 9A, 10**: dismissal for corruption or disloyalty; subsisting government contracts; office under a government company.
9. **Section 10A**: the **Commission** disqualifies for up to **three years** for failure to lodge expense accounts.
10. **Sections 11, 11A, 11B**: removal or reduction of disqualification, and the link between conviction, corrupt practice and disqualification period.
11. **Section 123**: bribery; undue influence; identity-based appeals and promotion of enmity; false statements; hiring vehicles; excess expenditure; obtaining government servants' assistance.
12. **Section 123(2)** targets interference with the voter's **free exercise of an electoral right**.
13. Disproportionate assets are an anti-corruption and disclosure problem, not automatically undue influence; the constitutional answer is disclosure after ***ADR*** (2002) and ***PUCL*** (2003).
14. **Sections 80, 80A, 81, 83, 86, 98, 99, 100, 116A**: the petition route, the High Court forum, forty-five days, pleadings, dismissal for non-compliance, final orders, naming, void grounds, and a thirty-day appeal to the Supreme Court.
15. Some section 100 grounds are absolute; others require that the result was **materially affected**.
16. ***N.P. Ponnuswami*** (1952): Article 329(b) is an **exclusive** remedy barring midstream interference.
17. ***Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain*** (1975): free and fair elections and judicial review are part of the basic structure; Article 329A was later **omitted** in 1978.
18. **Article 71**: Presidential and Vice-Presidential election disputes go to the **Supreme Court**.

### Concept check

**Question:** A sitting MLA is convicted and sentenced to three years' imprisonment. The appellate court stays the sentence pending appeal. He argues that his disqualification is suspended, and that in any event only the Election Commission can disqualify him. Evaluate, naming the provisions.

**Model answer:** Both arguments fail. Under **section 8(3) of the Representation of the People Act, 1951**, a sentence of imprisonment of two years or more disqualifies from the date of conviction and for six years after release, and since ***Lily Thomas v. Union of India*** (2013) struck down **section 8(4)**, that consequence operates immediately against a sitting member. Only a stay of the **conviction** suspends the disqualification; a stay of the **sentence** alone does not. On the second argument, the Commission does not disqualify here: where a question arises about a sitting member, **Article 192** requires the **Governor** to decide after obtaining the Election Commission's opinion and to act according to it, so the Commission's role is a binding opinion within a decision formally made by the Governor. The Commission does disqualify directly in a different situation — **section 10A**, failure to lodge an account of election expenses.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating all disqualification routes as one. The Commission decides under section 10A, advises bindingly under Articles 103 and 192, and has no role at all in a Tenth Schedule question.

### Original Mains practice — 20 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** "Indian election-dispute law is substantively strong and procedurally slow; the correct reform is to simplify the timeline, not the proof." Critically examine. (20 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (250 words):**
**Substantive strength.** Article 329(b) channels every challenge to a parliamentary or State-legislature election into an election petition, and *N.P. Ponnuswami* (1952) explains the design: the electoral process must not be interrupted midstream. The Representation of the People Act, 1951 then supplies a complete scheme — section 80A vests jurisdiction in the High Court; section 81 prescribes forty-five days; section 83 requires material facts and full particulars of any corrupt practice; section 100 lists the void grounds, some absolute and some requiring that the result was materially affected; section 99 requires notice and hearing before anyone is named as guilty; and section 116A gives an appeal to the Supreme Court. *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975) confirms that this remedy cannot be legislated away.

**The procedural failure.** Petitions routinely outlast the term they were meant to void. A corrupt-practice finding under section 8A, which then operates through the President acting on the Election Commission's opinion, can arrive after the House has dissolved, so deterrence collapses even where the law is satisfied.

**Why proof must not be simplified.** A corrupt-practice finding carries a six-year civic disqualification; section 99's hearing requirement is a safeguard, not an obstacle. Empowering the Commission to adjudicate guilt would also merge the conduct of an election with its judging, which Article 329(b) deliberately separates.

**Reform.** Dedicated election benches with enforced timelines, digitised service and evidence, day-to-day hearing, and coordinated court-Commission-President sequencing.

**Verdict.** The statement is correct: compress the sequence and the calendar, preserve the standard of proof.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** At twenty marks, expect both limbs argued, at least six named provisions and two cases, and an explicit reason why the attractive shortcut — Commission-led disqualification — is rejected. Scripts proposing that shortcut without confronting the six-year civic consequence should not reach the top band.

### Why the next lesson follows

One question has hovered over the whole file without being faced: can the machine be trusted? It deserves a lesson of its own, because it is the area where a careless answer does the most damage — in either direction.

---
## Lesson 19: EVM and VVPAT — The Architecture of Trust

Progress: 19 / 20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: How the system records a vote, what the courts directed, what they refused, and how to write about technology responsibly

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — the EVM and VVPAT judgments read for their operative directions
CA search: "EVM VVPAT verification Supreme Court directions safeguards"
CA found: the 2024 and 2025 orders below are decided directions, taught as settled; no pending development is relied on here
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: how one vote is recorded, and where the paper enters

```text
  voter is identified and allowed to vote
            |
            v
  BALLOT UNIT   -- voter presses the button against a
       |           candidate's name and symbol
       | signal
       v
  CONTROL UNIT  -- records and stores the vote; holds the
       |           count; is sealed and kept in the strong room
       |
       v
  VVPAT         -- prints a slip showing the candidate's serial
                   number, name and symbol, displays it to the
                   voter for a few seconds through a window,
                   then the slip falls into a sealed box

  THE SLIP IS NOT HANDED TO THE VOTER.
  It is the VOTER'S verification and the SYSTEM'S audit record.

  SYMBOL LOADING UNIT (SLU) -- used, before the poll, to load
  candidate names and symbols into the VVPAT. Until symbol
  loading, the machines hold no candidate or party data.
```

### 1. The Symbol Loading Unit: the architecture's point of vulnerability

The Symbol Loading Unit deserves attention, because it is where the 2024 directions bite. Until symbols are loaded, the machines are candidate-agnostic; the loading step is therefore the first moment at which a machine could, in principle, be made to behave differently towards one candidate. Secure that step and you have secured the point of maximum theoretical vulnerability.

### 2. Why a paper trail was added at all

An electronic record has one weakness that paper does not: a voter cannot see it. He presses a button and must believe the machine recorded what he pressed. The VVPAT was designed to convert belief into observation — the voter sees a printed slip confirming the choice, and the slip survives as an auditable record.

In ***Subramanian Swamy v. Election Commission of India*** (2013), the Supreme Court held the paper trail to be an important requirement of free and fair elections and directed the introduction of VVPAT in a phased manner. Note the formulation: the Court did **not** hold electronic voting unconstitutional; it required that the electronic record be made independently verifiable.

A further strengthening followed in 2019, when the Court directed that VVPAT slips from **five randomly selected polling stations per Assembly constituency or segment** be counted and matched against the control-unit result, increasing the earlier sample of one.

### 3. *Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India*, 26 April 2024

Petitioners sought a return to paper ballots, delivery of the VVPAT slip to the voter for deposit in a ballot box, and **one hundred per cent** counting of VVPAT slips.

| Relief sought | Court's response |
|---|---|
| Return to the paper-ballot system | **Rejected** |
| VVPAT slip handed to the voter for deposit and counting | **Rejected** |
| One hundred per cent counting of VVPAT slips | **Rejected**; the existing random-sample verification continues |
| Additional safeguards | **Granted**, in two specific directions |

The two directions, stated exactly, are the examinable content:

**Direction one — Symbol Loading Units.** On completion of the symbol-loading process, the Symbol Loading Units are to be **sealed and secured**, with candidates or their representatives signing the seal, and stored in containers kept in the **strong room along with the EVMs** for **at least forty-five days** after the declaration of results, to be dealt with thereafter as the EVMs are.

**Direction two — burnt-memory verification on request.** On a **written request** made within **seven days** of the declaration of the result, by a candidate placed **second or third** in the constituency, the **burnt memory or microcontroller** in **five per cent of the EVM sets** — control unit, ballot unit and VVPAT — per Assembly constituency or segment is to be **checked and verified by a team of engineers from the manufacturers**, after the results are declared. The candidate must identify the machines by polling-station or serial number; candidates or their representatives may be present; the **requesting candidate bears the notified cost**, which is **refunded if the machine is found to have been tampered with**.

Say the qualification explicitly in any answer: a **request-triggered post-result verification safeguard is not a finding that manipulation occurred**. The Court built a procedure for answering a specific doubt with evidence; it did not accept the doubt.

### 4. The follow-up of 7 May 2025

Because the design of the verification procedure was itself contested, the Court returned to the question in a follow-up order dated **7 May 2025**, considering the Commission's standard operating procedure for the checks directed in 2024. The position that emerged:

- verification is to be conducted by engineers of the **two manufacturers**, who must **certify** the integrity of the burnt memory, microcontrollers and programs;
- data in the machines slotted for verification is **not to be erased** before the verification is complete;
- a requesting candidate may seek a **mock poll**, and where one is conducted the recorded data is to be **displayed first** and certified before the machine is cleared for the mock poll;
- the candidate may ask that **Symbol Loading Unit data be retained** for use in that mock poll;
- the **cost** is borne by the requesting candidate in the first instance under the notified schedule.

The design logic is worth naming: the 2024 order created a right to verification, and the 2025 order made sure the right was not defeated by the procedure — specifically by the erasure of the very data that verification would examine.

### 5. How to write about this without amplifying unsupported claims

This is the part that decides marks, because both of the easy positions are wrong.

```text
  POSITION A (unsafe):  "EVMs are being manipulated."
     -> states an allegation as fact; the courts have not found
        manipulation, and no answer may assert it

  POSITION B (also unsafe):  "EVMs are perfectly secure, so
  audit demands are mischievous."
     -> converts institutional assurance into proof, and treats
        verification as an insult rather than a safeguard

  POSITION C (correct):  trust is PRODUCED, not asserted.
```

The framework to use:

```text
  TECHNICAL SECURITY
        +
  PHYSICAL CHAIN OF CUSTODY
     (randomisation of machines, sealing, strong rooms,
      candidate signatures on seals, CCTV, logs)
        +
  CANDIDATE AND AGENT OBSERVATION
     (mock polls before the poll, presence at sealing,
      presence at verification)
        +
  STATISTICAL AND LEGAL AUDIT
     (VVPAT slip counting at randomly selected stations;
      request-based burnt-memory verification)
        +
  TRANSPARENT INCIDENT RESPONSE
     (publishing discrepancies and their resolution)
        =
  EVIDENCE-BASED CONFIDENCE
```

Each line is a different kind of assurance, addressed to a different audience: engineers, administrators, candidates, statisticians and the public. That is why no single measure — not even a hundred per cent slip count — would by itself settle the question.

### 6. The strongest objection, and the strongest reply

**Objection.** A sample is not an audit. If five polling stations per segment are verified and a problem is confined to the unverified remainder, the sample will certify a system that failed. Only full counting answers the question.

**Reply.** The objection overstates what full counting delivers and understates what the layered system does. Full counting would introduce its own risks — a vastly longer, more manual count with greater scope for human error, delay and disputes, which is one reason the Court declined it. More importantly, the sample is not the only safeguard: it is combined with pre-poll randomisation of machines so that nobody knows in advance which machine goes where, mock polls in candidates' presence, sealing with candidate signatures, forty-five-day preservation of Symbol Loading Units, and a request-based forensic check available precisely to the candidates with the strongest incentive to find a problem.

**Residual.** The reply does not eliminate the objection; it reframes it. The genuine open question is not "sample or census" but **disclosure**: whether aggregate verification outcomes, discrepancy data and incident resolutions are published in a form that allows independent evaluation. That is a reform the Commission can adopt without any change in law, and it is the right thing to propose in an answer.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: transparency and accountability; the Election Commission's role; GS-III linkage on technology in governance.

**Where this lesson is tested.** A verified Mains question in the index asks about the challenges before the Commission in ensuring trustworthy elections in the light of the controversy over the use of electronic voting machines. That question is answered badly by candidates who take either of the easy positions.

**Probable framings.** "Electoral trust is an ecosystem, not a machine question. Critically analyse."

**Traps.**

- Saying the 2024 judgment ordered one hundred per cent VVPAT counting. It **refused** it.
- Saying it ordered a return to paper ballots. It refused that too.
- Saying the VVPAT slip is given to the voter. It is not.
- Describing the request-based check as a finding of tampering.
- Forgetting that the request route is open to the candidates placed **second or third**, within **seven days**, for **five per cent** of machines.
- Writing "the Supreme Court found EVMs safe" as a general certificate; it decided the reliefs before it.

**Answer use.** Open with the trust framework, place the judgments inside it as the audit layer, and then widen to the non-machine risks — rolls, money, criminalisation, partisan field administration and misinformation. A secure device cannot cure an unfair campaign.

### Revision notes

1. The system comprises the **Ballot Unit**, the **Control Unit** and the **VVPAT**.
2. The VVPAT prints and briefly displays a slip showing the candidate's serial number, name and symbol; the slip falls into a **sealed box** and is **not given to the voter**.
3. The **Symbol Loading Unit** loads candidate names and symbols; before loading, machines hold no candidate data.
4. ***Subramanian Swamy v. Election Commission of India*** (2013): paper trail held important for free and fair elections; **phased** VVPAT introduction directed.
5. In 2019 the Court directed VVPAT verification at **five randomly selected polling stations per Assembly constituency or segment**, up from one.
6. ***Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India***, **26 April 2024**: return to paper ballots **refused**; slip-to-voter **refused**; one hundred per cent VVPAT counting **refused**.
7. **Direction one**: Symbol Loading Units sealed, signed by candidates or their representatives, and kept with the EVMs in the strong room for **at least forty-five days** after declaration of results.
8. **Direction two**: on written request within **seven days** by the candidate placed **second or third**, burnt memory or microcontroller in **five per cent** of EVM sets per Assembly constituency or segment is checked by manufacturers' engineers; cost borne by the requesting candidate and **refunded if tampering is found**.
9. A verification safeguard is **not** a finding of tampering.
10. **7 May 2025** follow-up: manufacturers' engineers to conduct the check and **certify** integrity of burnt memory, microcontrollers and programs; data **not to be erased** before verification; a **mock poll** may be requested after recorded data is displayed and certified; Symbol Loading Unit data may be retained for it.
11. Trust framework: technical security, physical custody, candidate observation, statistical and legal audit, transparent incident response.
12. Pre-poll safeguards include **randomisation** of machines, mock polls in candidates' presence, and sealing with candidate signatures.
13. The live reform question is **disclosure** of aggregate verification and discrepancy outcomes, achievable without legislation.
14. The machine is one link; rolls, money, criminalisation, field neutrality and misinformation are the others.

### Concept check

**Question:** After a close result, the losing candidate who polled second asks for verification. He demands that every VVPAT slip in the constituency be counted, and claims that the Supreme Court in 2024 ordered exactly that. What is he entitled to, and what has he misunderstood?

**Model answer:** He has misunderstood the holding. In *Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India* (26 April 2024) the Court **refused** a return to paper ballots, refused delivery of the slip to the voter, and **refused** one hundred per cent VVPAT counting; the existing random-sample verification continues. What he is entitled to is the request-based safeguard the Court did grant: by **written request within seven days** of the declaration, a candidate placed **second or third** may have the burnt memory or microcontroller of **five per cent** of the EVM sets per Assembly constituency or segment checked by the manufacturers' engineers, identifying the machines himself, with candidates or representatives present, bearing the notified cost, refundable if tampering is found. Under the **7 May 2025** follow-up, the data must not be erased before verification, the engineers must certify integrity, and he may seek a mock poll after the recorded data is displayed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a judgment that grants a narrow, request-based safeguard as if it had granted the broad relief that was refused. Read what a court ordered, not what was asked for.

### Original Mains practice — 10 marks, 150 words

**Mains question:** "In the light of the controversy over electronic voting machines, what are the challenges before the Election Commission of India in ensuring the trustworthiness of elections?" Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model solution (149 words):**
Trustworthiness is a property of the whole chain, not of one device. On the machine, *Subramanian Swamy v. ECI* (2013) made the paper trail an integrity safeguard, and *Association for Democratic Reforms v. ECI* (26 April 2024) retained the EVM-VVPAT system while refusing a paper-ballot return and full slip counting, adding forty-five-day sealing of Symbol Loading Units and request-based verification of burnt memories in five per cent of machines; the 7 May 2025 follow-up barred erasure of data before verification and required certification by the manufacturers' engineers. The harder challenges lie elsewhere: inclusive and accurate rolls with fair deletion procedure; money power, where candidate ceilings miss party and digital spending; criminalisation, addressed only through disclosure after *ADR* (2002); neutrality of borrowed field administration; and organised misinformation. The Commission's task is therefore to publish verification and complaint outcomes as evidence, since a secure device cannot cure an unfair campaign field.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** The question says "challenges", so the EVM must be one item and not the whole answer. Cap at the middle band any script confined to the machine; reward scripts naming at least three non-machine challenges and stating the judicial position accurately.

### Why the next lesson follows

The last challenge in that list — money and information — is also the one where constitutional doctrine has moved furthest in recent years. The final lesson draws it together with the reform compass and the answer method for the whole topic.
