## Lesson 20: Money, Information, the Committee Agenda and the Reform Compass

Progress: 20 / 20 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: Disclosure jurisprudence, political finance, the committee reform agenda and simultaneous elections, the reform compass, and the answer method for the whole topic

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried — the disclosure judgments read for their exact constitutional ground, and the simultaneous-elections amendment Bill read as introduced
CA search: "political funding disclosure Election Commission transparency; simultaneous elections Bill status"
CA found: none relied on as this lesson's current anchor — the file's single current anchor remains in Lesson 7; the legislative position of the simultaneous-elections Bills is recorded below as a dated status check, with no outcome asserted
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

### Visual: the information ladder, from candidate to system

```text
  RUNG 1  CANDIDATE DISCLOSURE
          criminal, financial and educational antecedents on
          affidavit        [ADR 2002; PUCL 2003; Art 19(1)(a)]
                |
  RUNG 2  INFORMED VOTER CHOICE
          the vote becomes an exercise of expression rather
          than a guess
                |
  RUNG 3  FUNDING DISCLOSURE
          who gave, how much, to whom
          [electoral bonds judgment, 15 February 2024]
                |
  RUNG 4  CAMPAIGN DISCLOSURE
          candidate accounts [ss.77, 78]; party accounts;
          platform advertising --- the weakest rung
                |
  RUNG 5  ENFORCEMENT DISCLOSURE
          complaint-to-outcome records, verification results
          --- within the Commission's own power to supply
                |
         ELECTORAL ACCOUNTABILITY
```

Climb the ladder in an answer and the structure writes itself: each rung is a different information asymmetry, and each has a different remedy.

### 1. Rung one: the voter's right to know

In ***Union of India v. Association for Democratic Reforms*** (2002), the Supreme Court held that a voter has a **fundamental right under Article 19(1)(a)** to know the antecedents of candidates — criminal, financial and educational — reasoning that the freedom of expression involved in voting is hollow if the voter is kept ignorant of whom he is choosing. The Court directed the Commission to call for the necessary information.

When legislative changes sought to limit that disclosure, ***People's Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India*** (2003) struck down the limiting provision, reaffirming the right to know as constitutionally protected.

Two observations for an answer:

- The right vindicated is the voter's **right to information**, not a power of the Commission to disqualify. The Commission's role is as the **disclosure platform**: it prescribes and receives affidavits and publishes them.
- Disclosure is a weaker remedy than disqualification and a stronger one than nothing. It transfers the decision to the electorate, which is the constitutionally appropriate place for a political judgment, while making the judgment informed.

### 2. Rung three: political funding and the disclosure principle

On **15 February 2024**, in ***Association for Democratic Reforms v. Union of India***, the Supreme Court struck down the electoral bond scheme and the connected statutory amendments, holding that **anonymous political funding violated the voter's right to information under Article 19(1)(a)** and that the amendments permitting unlimited corporate contributions without disclosure were unconstitutional. The Court directed the issuing bank to furnish the details to the Election Commission and the Commission to publish them.

Three disciplines about how to use this.

1. **State the exact holding.** The ratio is the **right to information under Article 19(1)(a)**, not a general sentiment about transparency. A precise statement of ground is worth more than three sentences of praise.
2. **Keep the boundary.** Political funding is primarily a political-parties and party-funding subject. Here it is used for one purpose: to show the Commission functioning as the **publication authority** through which the right to information is operationalised.
3. **Name the residue honestly.** Striking down a funding mechanism restored disclosure but left the underlying problem — how parties are lawfully and sufficiently funded with transparency — unresolved. An answer that treats the judgment as having solved political finance is not accurate.

### 3. Rung four: the weakest rung, and why

| What is disclosed well | What is not |
|---|---|
| Candidate antecedents, by affidavit | Spending by parties and third parties attributable to a constituency campaign |
| Candidate election expenses, under sections 77 and 78, within thirty days | Digital and platform advertising targeted at specific electorates |
| Party contribution reports above the prescribed threshold | Aggregated cash flows below reporting thresholds |
| Broadcast and advertisement certification through media monitoring | Organised unpaid amplification and coordinated misinformation |

The structural point established in Lesson 13 returns here in its general form: **the unit of regulation is the candidate, while the unit of campaigning is the ecosystem**. Any serious reform proposal must close that gap, and must do so without pretending that higher ceilings or lower ceilings are themselves the answer.

### 4. One statutory provision that belongs here: the silence period

**Section 126** of the Representation of the People Act, 1951 prohibits, among other things, public meetings and processions in connection with an election, and the display of election matter by cinematograph, television or similar apparatus, during the period of **forty-eight hours** ending with the hour fixed for the conclusion of the poll in a polling area.

**Section 126A** prohibits the conduct of an **exit poll** and the publication or publicising of its result by any means during the period notified by the Commission — a period that has ordinarily run from the beginning of the hours fixed for the poll on the first day of polling to half an hour after the close of poll in all States and Union territories, and that is specified afresh for each election.

The rationale is the same in both: there must be a period in which the voter decides without last-minute influence, and in a multi-phase election, early-phase projections must not shape later-phase voting. These are **statutory** restrictions, not Model Code norms — another instance of the distinction Lesson 15 insisted on.

### 5. The committee-driven reform agenda, and One Nation–One Election

"Electoral reform" is the vaguest phrase in this syllabus, and examiners know it. The phrase has a precise content: a documented lineage of named committees and commissions, each of which identified a specific deficit and proposed a specific cure. An answer that says "many committees have suggested reforms" has said nothing. An answer that names three of them and the deficit each was addressing has already earned its marks.

```text
  1975  Tarkunde Committee        -> WHO APPOINTS the Commission; voting age
         |
  1990  Dinesh Goswami Committee  -> appointment consultation; decriminalisation;
         |                           expenditure control; identity cards
  1998  Indrajit Gupta Committee  -> STATE FUNDING, in kind, for recognised parties
         |
  1999  Law Commission, 170th     -> party finance disclosure; internal party
         |                           democracy; charge-stage disqualification
  2002  Review Commission (NCRWC) -> a law on political parties; criminalisation
         |
  2007  Second ARC, Ethics in     -> decriminalisation with judicial safeguards;
         |    Governance             transparency of party funds
  2015  Law Commission, 255th     -> comprehensive package: funding, criminal
         |                           antecedents, paid news, opinion polls
  2015  Parliamentary Standing    -> FEASIBILITY OF SIMULTANEOUS ELECTIONS
         |    Committee on Law
  2018  Law Commission draft      -> simultaneous elections, with the
         |                           constitutional changes they would need
  2023  High-Level Committee      -> a worked scheme for simultaneous elections
         |    (Ram Nath Kovind)      (report submitted 14 March 2024)
         v
  2024  Constitution (129th Amendment) Bill and
        Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill
```

Read the column on the right and you can see the agenda splitting into four durable demands: **who appoints** the Commission, **how campaigns are funded**, **how criminal antecedents are handled**, and **when elections are held**. The first three run through this entire file. The fourth is what "one nation, one election" means.

Before going further, one discipline that separates a strong script from a weak one: **a recommendation is not a reform**. Of the demands in that column, the lowering of the voting age became law through the Constitution (Sixty-first Amendment) Act, 1988; the paper audit trail and the secret rejection option came from the Supreme Court, not from a committee; candidate disclosure came from Article 19(1)(a) litigation; the four qualifying dates and the Aadhaar framework came from the Election Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021; and the appointment process came from Act 49 of 2023. State funding, a law on internal party democracy and charge-stage disqualification have been recommended repeatedly and enacted never. Say which is which, and the examiner knows you have read rather than revised.

#### 5.1 What the simultaneous-elections proposal is trying to solve

The Statement of Objects and Reasons to the amendment Bill puts the case in three sentences: elections have become expensive and time-consuming; the frequency of elections means the Model Code of Conduct is repeatedly in force; and that repetition disrupts development activity and service delivery. Behind it lies a historical fact worth one line in any answer: elections to the House of the People and to State Assemblies **were in fact held together from 1951 to 1967**, and the cycle broke down when Assemblies and then the fourth Lok Sabha were dissolved prematurely between 1968 and 1970. The proposal is therefore presented as a restoration rather than an invention.

#### 5.2 The High-Level Committee and its scheme

The Union Government constituted a **High-Level Committee on Simultaneous Elections chaired by former President Ram Nath Kovind** in September 2023; it submitted its report on **14 March 2024**. Its recommended design has two steps, and the distinction between them matters because they carry different constitutional costs.

| Step | What it synchronises | Constitutional requirement |
|---|---|---|
| Step one | The House of the People and all State Legislative Assemblies | A constitutional amendment fixing an appointed date and aligning terms |
| Step two | Municipal and panchayat elections, to be held within **one hundred days** of the general elections | A further amendment touching the local-government Parts, which would also require ratification by the States |

Step two is the heavier one, because local-body elections belong to the State Election Commissions under Articles 243K and 243ZA — the boundary established in Lesson 1 — and an amendment reaching into that field attracts the ratification requirement in the proviso to Article 368(2). Many answers merge the two steps and then understate the federal difficulty. Keep them apart.

#### 5.3 The mechanism in the Bill, read carefully

The **Constitution (One Hundred and Twenty-ninth Amendment) Bill, 2024** was introduced in the Lok Sabha on **17 December 2024**, together with the **Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2024**, which would extend the same framework to the Assemblies of Union territories that have them. The constitutional Bill proposes to insert a new **Article 82A** after Article 82.

```text
  APPOINTED DATE
  public notification by the President, issued on the date of the
  FIRST SITTING of the House of the People after a general election
          |
          v
  Every Legislative Assembly constituted AFTER that date, in any
  general election held before the full term of that Lok Sabha ends,
  has its term cut short to END WITH THAT LOK SABHA'S FULL TERM
          |
          v
  From the next general election onwards, the House of the People and
  all Assemblies go to the polls TOGETHER
          |
          +--> IF A HOUSE OR ASSEMBLY IS DISSOLVED EARLY
          |      a fresh election is held for the UNEXPIRED TERM only
          |      -- the remainder of the five-year cycle -- so the
          |      synchronisation restores itself automatically
          |
          +--> IF AN ASSEMBLY ELECTION CANNOT BE HELD WITH THE REST
                 the Election Commission may recommend to the President,
                 who may order that election on a later date; that
                 Assembly's term still ends with the Lok Sabha's
```

Three features of this mechanism deserve to be named precisely.

1. **It operates against the term provisions, not around them.** Articles 83 and 172 fix the five-year term of the House of the People and of every Legislative Assembly, running from the date appointed for its first meeting. The proposed article is drafted to operate **notwithstanding** those provisions, so that an Assembly's term can be ended by the arrival of the Lok Sabha's expiry date rather than by the completion of its own five years. The amendment's whole force lies in that override.
2. **It replaces a full term with an unexpired term.** The familiar rule after a premature dissolution is a fresh five-year House. Under the proposal, the replacement House or Assembly serves only the remainder of the current cycle. That is what makes the system self-correcting — and it is also the feature that produces the sharpest objection.
3. **It gives the Commission a new recommendatory role.** The power to say that a particular Assembly's election cannot be held along with the rest, triggering a presidential order for a later date, would be a genuinely new function, and it sits closer to the advisory column of Lesson 1 than to the administrative one.

#### 5.4 The case for, stated at its strongest

- **Cost.** Repeated national and State elections impose a recurring public cost and a much larger political cost. The Parliamentary Standing Committee of 2015 recorded the Commission's estimate of the government's own expenditure on a combined Lok Sabha and State Assembly round, and the argument is that synchronisation spreads that cost over five years instead of concentrating it almost annually.
- **Governance continuity.** Because the Model Code operates from the announcement of a schedule until the process is complete, an almost permanent election calendar means a recurring suspension of new schemes, transfers and announcements somewhere in the country. Synchronisation would confine that window.
- **Administrative capacity.** Central forces, polling personnel and machines are drawn repeatedly from the same pool. One large exercise every five years is, on this argument, easier to resource properly than a dozen smaller ones.
- **Participation.** A single national event is argued to raise turnout and to simplify the elector's task.

Note the honest scorecard: the cost argument is the weakest of these, because direct government expenditure on elections is a very small share of the Union Budget and of national income, and the much larger campaign spending by parties and candidates would not disappear merely because polls coincide. The Model Code and capacity arguments are the strong ones. Lead with those.

#### 5.5 The case against, sorted by the kind of objection

| Objection | Its core | Why it is hard to answer |
|---|---|---|
| **Federal** | Synchronising State mandates to the Union cycle risks nationalising State elections, letting a national wave decide a State verdict on local issues | Federalism is a basic feature; the objection goes to the character of the Union, not merely to convenience |
| **Accountability** | A parliamentary executive is accountable continuously and at periodic elections; shortening or extending Assembly terms to fit a calendar weakens the periodic check | The framers consciously preferred accountability over stability; an Assembly serving under a year cannot govern meaningfully |
| **Feasibility** | The Bill lets the Commission recommend and the President order the postponement of a State election, with no stated outer limit, and without the parliamentary approval that Article 356 requires | It creates an easier route to postponing an election than the emergency provision itself |
| **Capacity** | A genuinely simultaneous national poll needs machines, paper trails, personnel and central forces on a scale not previously deployed at one moment | The Commission's own logistics, not the Constitution, become the binding constraint |
| **Remedy mismatch** | If the disease is the Model Code's disruptive length, the proportionate cure is a shorter schedule and a tighter Code, not a constitutional amendment | A smaller remedy that treats the same symptom weakens the case for the larger one |

#### 5.6 The strongest reply, and the residual

**Reply.** The federal objection, pressed to its limit, proves too much. Nothing in the proposal transfers a State subject to the Union, alters the Commission's Article 324 field, or lets the Union dismiss a State government; the terms of Houses are already fixed by the Constitution and have already been altered by amendment before. The accountability objection is real but is aimed at the *transition*, where short Assembly terms occur, rather than at the *steady state*, where every House serves a full five years. And the unexpired-term device is not a novelty invented to defeat voters: it is the ordinary consequence of synchronising any fixed cycle, and it exists precisely so that one early dissolution does not destroy the alignment for a generation.

**Residual.** Two things that reply does not settle, and an honest script says so. First, a government that loses its majority in year four would face an election for a one-year House; whether any government can be formed, or any opposition can seriously campaign, on that basis is untested in India. Second, the postponement power is a genuine design gap: a provision that allows an Assembly election to be deferred without a stated outer limit and without parliamentary approval is weaker than the safeguard the Constitution already applies to President's rule, and that asymmetry has not been explained.

#### 5.7 Status discipline, which is where marks are actually lost

This is the single most dangerous paragraph in the topic, because the facts move and candidates repeat what they read a year earlier.

| Proposition | Status |
|---|---|
| Article 82A | **Proposed**. It is a clause in a Bill, not a provision of the Constitution |
| The two Bills | **Introduced** in the Lok Sabha on 17 December 2024 and **referred to a Joint Committee of Parliament** chaired by P.P. Chaudhary |
| The Joint Committee's report | **Not presented** as at the control date of this file; the Committee's term was extended again during 2026, with a reporting deadline in the Winter Session of 2026 |
| Passage, assent, ratification, commencement | **None of these has occurred** |
| Any implementation year | **Not assertable.** Figures in commentary are projections, not commitments |

Permitted formulation: *"a constitutional amendment Bill proposing Article 82A, introduced in December 2024 and still under examination by a Joint Committee of Parliament."* Prohibited: calling it enacted, describing Article 82A as part of the Constitution, saying the Committee has reported, or naming the election at which it will apply.

One last boundary. Simultaneous elections are a question about the **terms of legislatures** and about federal design; they are studied in full with Parliament and federalism. What belongs here is the part that touches this institution: the Commission is the implementing authority, would acquire a new recommendatory power, and is the body whose capacity the whole scheme presupposes.

### 6. The reform compass for the whole topic

Every reform in this topic can be sorted by **what it requires**. Sorting them this way is what turns a list into a graded recommendation, and it is the single most useful thing you can do in a conclusion.

| Reform | What it requires | Assessment |
|---|---|---|
| Publish complaint-to-outcome records and enforcement data | Nothing but Commission practice | Immediately available; the highest-value first step |
| Publish aggregate verification and discrepancy outcomes | Commission practice | Immediately available; addresses machine trust with evidence |
| Reasoned, promptly published orders in symbol and Model Code matters | Commission practice | Immediately available; cures the opacity criticism |
| Published appointment criteria, dossier time and recorded comparative reasons | Selection Committee practice under section 8(1) | Available without amendment; the committee already must act transparently |
| Dedicated election benches with enforced timelines | Statutory and judicial-administrative change | Medium difficulty; the key to making the post-result remedy real |
| Bringing party and third-party campaign spending into an auditable regime | Legislation | Harder; addresses the central political-finance gap |
| Equalising removal protection with an inquiry safeguard | Constitutional amendment | Hardest; must be designed against the accountability cost |
| A permanent independent secretariat and charged expenditure | Legislation, and possibly amendment | *Anoop Baranwal* appealed for it; it is not law |

The compass also tells you what **not** to propose. Do not propose that the Commission be given power to disqualify for corrupt practice: Lesson 18 explained why. Do not propose that the Model Code simply be made a statute: Lesson 15 explained why. A reform that ignores the reason the present design exists is not a reform.

### 7. The answer method for the whole topic

Five moves, in order, for any Election Commission question:

```text
  1. CONSTITUTIONAL CLAIM
     name the Article and the exact field
         e.g. "Article 324(1) vests superintendence, direction
         and control of rolls and of elections to Parliament,
         State Legislatures and the offices of President and
         Vice-President"
         |
  2. NAMED EVIDENCE
     a statute section, an official instrument, or a case
         e.g. section 21(3) RPA 1950; paragraph 16A Symbols
         Order; Mohinder Singh Gill; A.C. Jose
         |
  3. INSTITUTIONAL ANALYSIS
     how the mechanism protects a free and fair election
         |
  4. QUALIFICATION
     the statutory boundary, the ownership limit, or the
     current-status caution
         |
  5. REFORM VERDICT
     precise, proportionate and implementable, sorted by
     what it requires
```

### 8. The thesis selector

| If the demand is | Use this qualified thesis |
|---|---|
| Independence | Constitutional status is necessary but insufficient; appointment, collegial security, finance, staff and transparent enforcement determine practical autonomy |
| Article 324 powers | Article 324 is a reservoir for statutory silence, not a charter to disobey Parliament |
| The Model Code | The Code's speed comes from its non-statutory form; strengthen accountability without losing preventive capacity |
| Machine trust | Trust rests on layered custody, observation and audit, not on assurance or allegation |
| Electoral rolls | Roll integrity means eligible inclusion **plus** lawful correction through notice, evidence and remedy |
| The 2023 appointment Act | Parliament filled the Article 324(2) vacuum; executive predominance and deliberative transparency remain under challenge |
| Disputes and disqualification | The design separates conducting an election from judging it; the defect is delay, not access |
| Parties and symbols | A purpose-bound quasi-judicial jurisdiction, whose legitimacy depends on reasoned and prompt orders |

### 9. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Disclosure is a weak remedy. Voters have repeatedly elected candidates whose affidavits disclosed serious criminal cases, which suggests that information changes nothing and that the real answer is exclusion at the nomination stage.

**Reply.** Exclusion at nomination has a constitutional cost that is easy to overlook. A disqualification based on accusation rather than conviction would invert the presumption of innocence and hand an enormous weapon to whoever controls prosecution — precisely the actor the Commission exists to restrain. Disclosure keeps the political judgment with the electorate while removing the excuse of ignorance; and it is not inert, since disclosure feeds media scrutiny, internal party selection and later prosecutions.

**Residual.** The reply is principled but not fully satisfying, and an honest answer says so. Disclosure has not measurably reduced the representation of candidates with serious pending cases. The reform frontier is therefore **time** — fast-track trials of sitting legislators, so that the question becomes conviction rather than accusation — rather than lowering the threshold for exclusion.

### UPSC application

**Syllabus location.** GS-II: transparency and accountability; electoral reforms; the right to information as a dimension of Article 19(1)(a).

**Where this lesson is tested.** The verified 2024 Mains question on the need for electoral reforms suggested by various committees, with particular reference to "one nation–one election", is **owned by this lesson** — its committee lineage, its status discipline and its reform compass are all taught above. The 2025 question on corrupt practices and disproportionate assets depends on the disclosure line of reasoning developed here, and the compass is what distinguishes a conclusion from a wish list.

**Probable framings.** "Examine the need for electoral reforms with reference to transparency in political funding." "Design an institutional reform package to strengthen the Election Commission without weakening accountability." "Simultaneous elections promise governance continuity at a federal price. Critically examine."

**Traps.**

- Describing the electoral-bonds ruling as a general transparency judgment rather than an **Article 19(1)(a)** holding.
- Treating disclosure jurisprudence as giving the Commission a power to reject candidates.
- Attributing the exit-poll restriction to the Model Code; it is **section 126A**.
- Proposing reforms without stating what each requires — practice, statute or amendment.
- Claiming political finance was solved in 2024.
- Writing that Article 82A is part of the Constitution, that simultaneous elections have been enacted, that the Joint Committee has reported, or naming the election from which the scheme will apply.
- Treating the Kovind Committee's two steps as one, and so missing that the local-body step would also need ratification by the States.

**Answer use.** End every answer with a graded reform paragraph sorted by feasibility. It is the cheapest way to look like a candidate who understands institutions rather than one who has memorised them.

### Revision notes

1. ***Union of India v. Association for Democratic Reforms*** (2002) and ***People's Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India*** (2003): the voter's right to know candidates' criminal, financial and educational antecedents is protected by **Article 19(1)(a)**, and a legislative attempt to limit that disclosure was struck down.
2. The Commission's role is as the **disclosure platform**, not a disqualifying authority.
3. **15 February 2024**: the electoral bond scheme and connected amendments were struck down as violating the right to information under **Article 19(1)(a)**; unlimited undisclosed corporate funding was held unconstitutional; the issuing bank was directed to furnish details to the Commission for publication.
4. Political funding beyond the Commission's publication role belongs with the study of political parties and party funding, and the disclosure residue — how parties are to be funded transparently — remains unresolved.
5. The regulated unit is the **candidate**; the real unit of campaigning is the **ecosystem**.
6. **Section 126, RPA 1951**: restrictions in the **forty-eight hours** ending with the conclusion of the poll in a polling area; **section 126A**: bar on conducting and publicising **exit polls** during the notified period.
7. These silence-period rules are **statutory**, not Model Code norms.
8. Named reform lineage: **Tarkunde (1975)**, **Dinesh Goswami (1990)**, **Indrajit Gupta on State funding (1998)**, **Law Commission 170th (1999)** and **255th (2015)**, **NCRWC (2002)**, **Second ARC, Ethics in Governance (2007)**; on simultaneous elections, the **Parliamentary Standing Committee (2015)**, the **Law Commission draft (2018)** and the **High-Level Committee chaired by Ram Nath Kovind**, constituted in September 2023, which reported on **14 March 2024**.
9. A recommendation is not a reform: State funding, a law on internal party democracy and charge-stage disqualification have been recommended repeatedly and enacted never.
10. **Article 82A is proposed, not enacted.** The **Constitution (One Hundred and Twenty-ninth Amendment) Bill, 2024** and the **Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2024** were introduced in the Lok Sabha on **17 December 2024** and referred to a **Joint Committee of Parliament chaired by P.P. Chaudhary**, which had not presented its report as at this file's control date.
11. The proposed mechanism: an **appointed date** notified on the date of the first sitting of a new House of the People; Assemblies constituted after it end with that House's full term, the article operating **notwithstanding Articles 83 and 172**; a premature dissolution produces an election for the **unexpired term** only; and the Commission may recommend deferring a particular Assembly election, which the President may then order for a later date.
12. Reform compass: sort every proposal by whether it needs Commission practice, legislation, or constitutional amendment.
13. Practice-level reforms available now: complaint-to-outcome records, verification and discrepancy data, reasoned published orders, and published appointment criteria with recorded reasons; and do not propose Commission-led disqualification for corrupt practice or a straightforward statutory Model Code, since both ignore the reason for the present design.
14. Answer method: constitutional claim, named evidence, institutional analysis, qualification, reform verdict.
15. The criminalisation frontier is **speed of trial**, not a lower threshold for exclusion.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate's affidavit discloses three pending cases for serious offences; a rival asks the Election Commission to reject his nomination "in view of the right to know". Is the request legally sound, and what does the disclosure jurisprudence actually require?

**Model answer:** The request is not legally sound. *Union of India v. Association for Democratic Reforms* (2002) and *People's Union for Civil Liberties v. Union of India* (2003) established the **voter's right to know** a candidate's criminal, financial and educational antecedents under **Article 19(1)(a)**, and the Commission's resulting role is to prescribe, obtain and publish the affidavit. It does **not** create a power to reject a nomination: disqualification is governed by Articles 102 and 191 and by Part II of the Representation of the People Act, 1951, which attach consequences to **conviction**, not to pending accusations, and Lesson 16's doctrine forbids the Commission from creating a disqualification Parliament has not enacted. The remedy the jurisprudence contemplates is publicity and an informed electoral judgment, supplemented by faster trials of legislators.

**Misconception to avoid:** Converting a right to information into a power of exclusion. Disclosure relocates the decision to the voter; it does not transfer it to the regulator.

### Original Mains practice — 20 marks, 250 words

**Mains question:** Design an institutional reform package to strengthen the independence and credibility of the Election Commission of India without weakening its accountability. (20 marks, 250 words)

**Model solution (246 words):**
**Principle.** Reform should close the gaps the Constitution left open — appointment, finance, secretariat and enforcement transparency — without creating an unaccountable authority or transferring judicial functions to the administrator.

**Appointment.** Section 7 of Act 49 of 2023 constitutes a Selection Committee of the Prime Minister, the Leader of Opposition and a Prime Minister-nominated Union Cabinet Minister. Without amendment, section 8(1)'s transparency duty can be operationalised: publish eligibility criteria and the shortlist principle, give members adequate dossier time, and record comparative reasons. A durable settlement would rebalance the committee; that choice is for Parliament and is currently before the Court, which the answer must not pre-empt.

**Tenure.** Retain the CEC's Supreme Court judge removal; attach to the Article 324(5) recommendation route for other Commissioners a mandatory independent inquiry, notice and recorded reasons — securing dissent without producing irremovable members.

**Finance and staff.** Article 324 has no analogue of Article 322, and establishment expenditure is a votable demand. *Anoop Baranwal* appealed for a permanent secretariat and charged expenditure; implement it by statute, with full audit and parliamentary reporting so autonomy does not become opacity.

**Enforcement credibility.** Publish a complaint-to-outcome record for Model Code matters with timelines and speaking orders; publish aggregate verification and discrepancy data after each election; issue reasoned symbol orders promptly.

**Dispute speed.** Create dedicated election benches with enforced statutory timelines; do not empower the Commission to adjudicate corrupt practice, which carries a six-year civic consequence.

**Sequencing.** Practice reforms now; statutory reforms next; amendment last.

**Scoring note (unique to this question):** Twenty marks require sequencing, not enumeration. Reserve the top band for scripts that sort reforms by what each requires, state at least one reform they reject with a reason, and avoid asserting an outcome in the pending challenge.

### Where this leads

You can now do five things that a reader of summaries cannot. You can state the exact field of Article 324 and the two articles that sit outside it. You can read Act 49 of 2023 by section and say what changed and what only appears to have changed. You can take a roll question to the right provision and the right forum. You can state the plenary-power doctrine with both of its cases. And you can write about appointment, machines and money in language that will still be true next month.

What follows is the practice architecture: the verified previous-year questions displayed exactly as printed and deliberately unanswered, cumulative checks across the whole file, fresh Mains practice with models, a remediation set built from the errors this topic actually produces, master maps, consolidated register notes, the coverage matrix and the source ledger.

# VERIFIED PYQ LINKAGE AND ANSWER APPROACHES

This index gives each verified question its exact printed demand and a way of working it. No answer, answer letter, truth marking, elimination cue or corrective mapping appears anywhere in this section. Objective questions retain their printed response choices because those choices are part of the question.

**On wording.** Every question below was read from an official paper held locally, except where expressly stated otherwise. Where the text layer of a scanned paper introduced a spacing or bracket artefact, the artefact is identified and only that artefact has been normalised. Where the printed English itself contains a typographical error, the error is reproduced and marked `[sic]`; it has not been silently corrected.

## Direct objective questions on this topic

### 2021 Prelims GS-I, Question 80

> Consider the following statements :
>
> 1. In India, there is no law restricting the candidates from contesting in one Lok Sabha election from three constituencies.
>
> 2. In 1991 Lok Sabha Election, Shri Devi Lal contested from three Lok Sabha constituencies.
>
> 3. As per the existing rules, if a candidate contests in one Lok Sabha election from many constituencies, his/her party should bear the cost of bye-elections to the constituencies vacated by him/her in the event of him/her winning in all the constituencies.
>
> Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
>
> (a) 1 only
>
> (b) 2 only
>
> (c) 1 and 3
>
> (d) 2 and 3

**Paper.** Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2021, General Studies Paper I, Question 80, read from the official paper held locally (booklet series marking `BXC-U-FTHI/68A`), with the English page verified against the page image. No answer is recorded here, and no statement is marked true or false.

**Mapped lesson.** Lesson 13, section on contesting from more than one constituency.

**How to work it.** Take each statement to its own provision. For the first, ask whether the Representation of the People Act, 1951 presently caps the number of constituencies a candidate may contest in the same general election, and from which amendment year that cap dates. For the second, treat it as a historical proposition about a candidature and locate it in time relative to that amendment. For the third, identify the exact rule it asserts and test that assertion against the enacted provisions and the rules made under them, rather than against anything proposed or debated elsewhere. Decide each statement independently before looking at the combinations.

### 2024 Prelims GS-I, Question 71

> How many Delimitation Commissions have been constituted by the Government of India till December 2023 ?
>
> (a) One
>
> (b) Two
>
> (c) Three
>
> (d) Four

**Paper.** Civil Services (Preliminary) Examination 2024, General Studies Paper I, Set A, Question 71, read from the official paper held locally. An official Set A answer key is also held locally; no answer is recorded or inferred here.

**Mapped lesson.** Lesson 17, section on the four-commission fact and the inference it does not support.

**How to work it.** Count the statutory exercises by their enabling Acts rather than by recollection of events, and keep the count to all-India Commissions constituted by the Central Government. Then, separately from the count, confirm that the exercise is owned by a statutory Delimitation Commission and not by the Election Commission, so that a correct number is not accompanied by an incorrect ownership claim.

## Direct descriptive questions on this topic

### 2018 GS-II, Question 1 — 10 marks, 150 words

> In the light of recent controversy regarding the use of Electronic Voting Machines (EVM), what are the challenges before the Election Commission of India to ensure the trustworthiness of elections in India? (Answer in 150 words)

**Paper.** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2018, General Studies Paper II, Question 1, read from the official paper held locally. The scan's text layer rendered the word-limit bracket as `{Answer in 150 words]`; only that mismatched bracket pair has been normalised.

**Mapped lessons.** Lesson 19 for the machine and the judicial safeguards; Lessons 10, 11, 13, 18 and 20 for the non-machine challenges.

**How to work it.** The directive is "what are the challenges", so the answer enumerates and evaluates rather than defends or attacks the machine. Read "trustworthiness of elections" as wider than "trustworthiness of EVMs": the controversy is the occasion, not the scope. Build the chain — roll, candidature, campaign field, money, machine, custody, count, remedy — and identify the challenge at each link, then close with what the Commission can publish or do without new powers.

### 2019 GS-II, Question 11 — 15 marks

> On what grounds a people's representative can be disqualified under the Representation of Peoples Act, 1951? Also mention the remedies available to such person against his disqualification.

**Paper.** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2019, General Studies Paper II, Question 11, read from the official paper held locally. The scan's text layer carried the line-break hyphenation `Representa- tion`, which has been rejoined; nothing else was altered. **Word limit provenance:** the question as printed carries marks but no individual word limit; the paper's instruction block states that answers to Questions 11 to 20 should be in **250 words**, so the limit is instruction-derived rather than printed with the question.

**Mapped lesson.** Lesson 18, with Lesson 2 for the constitutional layer.

**How to work it.** Separate the two limbs physically on the page, because the second limb carries half the marks and is the one most often compressed. For grounds, work down the statutory list and distinguish those that operate on conviction from those that operate on conduct, contract or office, and note which authority declares each. For remedies, think in four directions: relief from the Commission; appellate relief and what exactly must be stayed; the constitutional reference route for a sitting member and whose opinion binds there; and the petition route with its appeal. Keep the Tenth Schedule out of both limbs, since the question names a statute.

### 2020 GS-II, Question 1 — 10 marks, 150 words

> "There is a need for simplification of procedure for disqualification of persons found guilty of corrupt practices under the Representation of Peoples Act". Comment. (Answer in 150 words)

**Paper.** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2020, General Studies Paper II, Question 1, read from the official paper held locally.

**Mapped lesson.** Lesson 18, with Lesson 16 for why one attractive shortcut is unavailable.

**How to work it.** "Comment" requires a stated position with its qualification, not a neutral survey. First set out the present chain honestly — where a corrupt practice is proved, who names the guilty, which authority then declares the disqualification and on whose opinion — because the argument for simplification only becomes visible once the number of forums and stages is on the page. Then separate two things that the word "simplification" fuses: the **sequence and calendar**, and the **standard of proof**. Decide which of them your position would change, and state the civic consequence that makes the other one untouchable.

### 2022 GS-II, Question 11 — 15 marks, 250 words

> Discuss the procedures to decide the disputes arising out of the election of a Member of the Parliament or State Legislature under The Representation of the People Act, 1951. What are the grounds on which the election of any returned candidate may be declared void ? What remedy is available to the aggrieved party against the decision ? Refer to the case laws. (Answer in 250 words)

**Paper.** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2022, General Studies Paper II, Question 11, read from the official paper held locally. The scan's text layer rendered "procedures to" as `proceduresto`; only that missing space has been restored.

**Mapped lessons.** Lesson 18 for the statutory scheme and the cases; Lesson 2 for the constitutional channel.

**How to work it.** The question has four limbs — procedure, void grounds, remedy, case law — and the examiner can see at a glance whether all four are present, so label them. Begin above the statute with the constitutional provision that makes the petition the only route, and the case that explains why courts stay out during the poll. For void grounds, do not list indiscriminately: sort them into those that operate by themselves and those that require the result to have been materially affected. For remedy, state the appellate forum and its time limit. For case law, choose authorities that do different work rather than three that say the same thing.

### 2022 GS-II, Question 15 — 15 marks, 250 words

> Discuss the role of the Election Commission of India in the light of the evolution of the Model Code of Conduct. (Answer in 250 words)

**Paper.** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2022, General Studies Paper II, Question 15, read from the official paper held locally.

**Mapped lesson.** Lesson 15, with Lesson 14 for the sanction attached to defiance and Lesson 16 for the source of the enforcement power.

**How to work it.** The question joins a role to an evolution, so the chronology must earn its place by explaining how the Code acquired authority without acquiring legal force. Give the evolution one disciplined paragraph. Spend the body on legal character, the moment the Code begins to operate, the enforcement tools actually available and their basis, and the one instrument that attaches a heavy consequence to persistent defiance, which most answers omit. Close on the trade-off between speed and enforceability and state which way you would resolve it.

### 2024 GS-II, Question 1 — 10 marks, 150 words

> Examine the need for electoreal [sic] reforms as suggested by various commities [sic] with particular reference "one nation–one election" principle. (Answer in 150 words)

**Paper.** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2024, General Studies Paper II, Question 1. The locally held copy of the paper carries a third-party watermark overlay, and the question text was read from its text layer and verified against the page image. The printed English contains the typographical errors `electoreal` and `commities`, which are reproduced and marked `[sic]`; the printed phrase is "with particular reference" followed directly by the quoted principle, and no missing word has been supplied. Only the opening and closing double quotation marks, which the text layer could not render, have been reconstructed as quotation marks around the quoted phrase.

**Mapped lesson.** Lesson 20 §5, which owns this question: the named committee lineage from Tarkunde to the High-Level Committee, the two-step scheme that Committee recommended, the proposed Article 82A and the way it would operate against Articles 83 and 172, the unexpired-term device for a premature dissolution, the claimed benefits, the federal, accountability, feasibility and capacity objections with the strongest reply and residual, and the exact legislative status. Lesson 20 §6 supplies the reform compass and the sequencing method. The wider federal consequences of synchronising legislative terms are developed further with Parliament and federal design.

**How to work it.** "Examine" asks you to weigh, so the answer must contain a case and a counter-case rather than a preference. Begin by establishing the wider reform agenda the question refers to, and only then narrow to the named principle, which is a special reference and not the whole scope. For the named principle, separate three questions that get fused: what it would require constitutionally, what it would deliver administratively, and what it would cost federally. Be strict about status: describe its legislative position as it stands at the date you write and do not assert a stage it has not reached or an implementation year.

### 2025 GS-II, Question 1 — 10 marks, 150 words

> Discuss the 'corrupt practices' for the purpose of the Representation of the People Act, 1951. Analyze whether the increase in the assets of the legislators and/or their associates, disproportionate to their known sources of income, would constitute 'undue influence' and consequently a corrupt practice. (Answer in 150 words)

**Paper.** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2025, General Studies Paper II, Question 1, read from the official paper held locally.

**Mapped lesson.** Lesson 18, sections on corrupt practices and on the disclosure route; Lesson 20 for the remedy the jurisprudence actually supplies.

**How to work it.** Two directives, two tasks. "Discuss" the statutory category first: establish that it is a closed list and name its heads compactly. Then "analyse" the specific proposition, and the analysis turns on identifying whose freedom the relevant head protects. Ask what the provision's target is, and whether the fact described operates on that target at all or on something else entirely. Having answered that, state what the constitutionally correct response to the described fact is, and keep open the narrow situation in which a different head of the same list could be attracted on proof of its own elements.

## Necessary cross-topic question

### 2026 GS-II, Question 4 — 10 marks, 150 words

> Is the right to vote a fundamental right? Discuss the position of the Election Commission of India while undertaking the revision of electoral rolls. Can it also examine the question of citizenship of voters?

**Paper.** Civil Services (Main) Examination 2026, General Studies Paper II, Question 4. **Wording provenance:** the locally held official scan of this paper carries **no extractable text layer**, so the wording above is reproduced from the repository's audited routing ledger for the 2026 General Studies Paper II, which records the English text as read from that official scan. It is therefore a ledger-recorded transcription of the official paper rather than a direct text-layer extraction, and that limitation is stated rather than glossed over. Marks and word limit are recorded in the same ledger as 10 marks and 150 words.

**Mapped lessons.** Lesson 12 for the first limb; Lessons 10 and 11 for the second; Lesson 11 for the third. The citizenship-law dimension beyond electoral registration belongs with the study of citizenship and is only cross-referred here.

**How to work it.** Three limbs and only 150 words, so budget them. The first limb is a classification question — settle it in two or three sentences by distinguishing the categories of right involved and attaching the right authority to each, without letting the classification consume the answer. The second limb is about the Commission's **position**: name the constitutional source of the roll power, the statutory machinery that carries it, and the procedural duties that constrain it. The third limb is a boundary question: ask what the Commission must necessarily be satisfied about in order to register an elector, and then ask whether being satisfied for that purpose is the same act as determining a person's status under the law that governs status. The answer to the third limb lies in the distance between those two acts, and in what must happen when the Commission is not satisfied.

## How to use this index

Work each question cold before looking at its mapped lesson, then reread only the mapped lesson and attempt it again. The index is deliberately unanswered: an election-law question whose answer you have already read teaches you recognition, not retrieval, and recognition is not what is tested.

---

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These checks cut across lessons. Each is designed around a distinction the file has built and that a single lesson could not test alone.

## Cumulative check 1 — the ownership switch

**Question:** For each of the following, name the deciding authority and its constitutional or statutory source: (i) a municipal election; (ii) whether a sitting MP has become subject to an Article 102(1) disqualification; (iii) whether a returned candidate's election is void; (iv) whether an MLA has defected; (v) a dispute about the election of the Vice-President.

**Model answer:** (i) The **State Election Commission** under **Article 243ZA**. (ii) The **President**, who under **Article 103** shall obtain the **Election Commission's opinion** and act according to it. (iii) The **High Court** on an election petition under **Article 329(b)** and sections 80 and 80A of the Representation of the People Act, 1951, with appeal to the Supreme Court under section 116A. (iv) The **Chairman or Speaker** of the House under the **Tenth Schedule**, subject to judicial review. (v) The **Supreme Court** under **Article 71**.

## Cumulative check 2 — the four numbers that look alike

**Question:** Distinguish the following without confusing them: the six-year rule for a Commissioner; the six-year disqualification after release; the forty-five-day petition period; the forty-five-day Symbol Loading Unit preservation.

**Model answer:** The **six-year term or age sixty-five, whichever is earlier**, is the Commissioner's tenure under **section 9 of Act 49 of 2023** — statutory, not constitutional. The **six years from release** is the additional period of disqualification following a conviction attracting **section 8 of the RPA 1951**. The **forty-five days** in **section 81 of the RPA 1951** is the period within which an election petition must be presented from the date of election of the returned candidate. The **forty-five days** in the 26 April 2024 judgment is the minimum period for which sealed Symbol Loading Units are to be kept with the EVMs in the strong room after declaration of results. Two statutes, one judgment, four different functions.

## Cumulative check 3 — statute or not

**Question:** Classify each of the following as a constitutional provision, a statute, subordinate legislation or a non-statutory instrument, and state the consequence of the classification: the Model Code of Conduct; the Election Symbols (Reservation and Allotment) Order, 1968; section 126A; Article 324(6).

**Model answer:** The **Model Code** is **non-statutory**: it creates no offence, so its force comes from Article 324 directions, inter-party consensus and the consequences attached elsewhere. The **Symbols Order, 1968** is an **order issued by the Commission**, upheld in *Kanhiya Lal Omar v. R.K. Trivedi* (1985) as supported by Article 324 read with the statutory scheme; it binds, and paragraph 16A under it permits suspension or withdrawal of recognition. **Section 126A** is a **statutory** provision of the RPA 1951 barring conduct and publication of exit polls during the notified period — enforceable as law, not as a code. **Article 324(6)** is **constitutional**, imposing a duty on the President or a Governor to make staff available when the Commission so requests.

## Cumulative check 4 — supplement or supplant

**Question:** Apply the Article 324 decision test to three situations: (i) the Commission issues instructions on the format of candidate affidavits where the Rules are silent on format; (ii) the Commission adopts a counting procedure different from the one the Rules prescribe; (iii) the Commission directs a fresh poll after ballot papers are destroyed, in a situation the statute does not expressly cover.

**Model answer:** (i) **Lawful**: the law is silent on format, so Article 324 supplies a gap-filling power exercised for a free and fair election. (ii) **Unlawful**: express Rules speak, and under *A.C. Jose v. Sivan Pillai* (1984) the Commission may supplement but not supplant them; administrative inconvenience is not a gap. (iii) **Lawful**: this is *Mohinder Singh Gill* (1978) itself — Article 324 is a reservoir where the enacted law is silent, subject to purpose, recorded reasons, fairness and judicial review.

## Cumulative check 5 — the roll, end to end

**Question:** Trace an eighteen-year-old's path onto the roll and the safeguards that protect her if her name is later proposed for deletion, naming provisions at each step.

**Model answer:** Entitlement arises under **section 19 of the RPA 1950** once she is not less than eighteen on a **qualifying date** — since Act 49 of 2021, one of **1 January, 1 April, 1 July or 1 October** — and is **ordinarily resident** in the constituency, with ordinary residence governed by **section 20**. She applies for inclusion under **section 23**, which also permits the Electoral Registration Officer to require an Aadhaar number for identity under **section 23(4)**, subject to the **section 23(6)** protection against denial for inability with prescribed sufficient cause. If deletion is later proposed on the ground that she has ceased to be ordinarily resident or is otherwise not entitled, the proviso to **section 22** requires a **reasonable opportunity of being heard**; an appeal lies to the **Chief Electoral Officer** under **section 24**; **section 23(3)** freezes changes between the last date for nominations and the completion of that election; and **section 30** bars a civil suit while leaving constitutional review intact.

## Cumulative check 6 — what the 2023 Act changed, and what it did not

**Question:** State three things Act 49 of 2023 changed and three things it left exactly as they were.

**Model answer:** **Changed:** a statutory eligibility standard was created (section 5); a formal Search Committee headed by the Minister of Law and Justice producing a panel of five was introduced (section 6); and tenure was placed in statute with an express bar on re-appointment and an aggregate six-year cap for an Election Commissioner who becomes Chief Election Commissioner (section 9). It also repealed the Act of 1991 (section 21). **Unchanged:** the President remains the appointing authority; the President continues to fix the number of Election Commissioners (section 3, mirroring Article 324(2)); and the removal architecture is unchanged, the Chief Election Commissioner being removable only like a Supreme Court judge and others only on his recommendation (section 11, mirroring Article 324(5)).

## Cumulative check 7 — three different "disqualifications"

**Question:** The Commission "disqualifies" in one situation, "advises" in another and has "no role" in a third. Identify each with its provision.

**Model answer:** It **disqualifies** under **section 10A of the RPA 1951**, declaring a person disqualified for up to three years for failure to lodge an account of election expenses in time and in the prescribed manner without good reason. It **advises bindingly** under **Articles 103 and 192**, where the President or Governor shall obtain its opinion on a sitting member's disqualification and act according to it, including for a corrupt-practice disqualification under **section 8A**. It has **no role** in a **Tenth Schedule** defection question, which the Speaker or Chairman decides subject to judicial review.

## Cumulative check 8 — status discipline

**Question:** Write the single safest sentence you can about each of the following as at the control date: the 2023 appointment Act; the simultaneous-elections proposal; the Commission's citizenship inquiry.

**Model answer:** On the **2023 Act**: "The Act operates; the challenge to section 7 is pending, interim interference was declined on 22 March 2024, and on 23 September 2026 the matter was directed to be placed before the Chief Justice of India for the constitution of a larger Bench." On **simultaneous elections**: "Article 82A is proposed, not enacted; the Constitution (129th Amendment) Bill, 2024 and the Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2024 were introduced on 17 December 2024 and referred to a Joint Committee that had not presented its report, so no passage and no implementation year may be asserted." On **citizenship**: "The Commission may make a limited inquiry into citizenship for electoral purposes but cannot finally determine status, and must refer unsatisfied cases to the competent authority under the Citizenship Act, 1955."

## Cumulative check 9 — registration, recognition, symbol

**Question:** A newly formed party is registered, wins seats in one State, splits two years later, and one faction is found to have defied the Model Code. Name the instrument and provision engaged at each stage.

**Model answer:** **Registration** — section 29A of the Representation of the People Act, 1951, requiring a memorandum affirming true faith and allegiance to the Constitution and to socialism, secularism and democracy. **Recognition** — paragraph 6A of the Election Symbols (Reservation and Allotment) Order, 1968, on any one of its alternative performance conditions, with paragraph 6C cushioning a later adverse election and paragraph 17 governing Gazette notification of symbols. **Split** — paragraph 15, under which the Commission may decide, after hearing, that one group or none is the recognised party, the decision binding all; *Sadiq Ali* (1972) upholds the jurisdiction. **Defiance of the Model Code** — paragraph 16A, permitting suspension or withdrawal of recognition after a reasonable opportunity of showing cause.

## Cumulative check 10 — what the courts refused

**Question:** List four reliefs that courts have refused in this area, and state why the refusals matter for answer writing.

**Model answer:** A return to **paper ballots**; delivery of the **VVPAT slip to the voter** for counting; **one hundred per cent VVPAT counting** — all refused on 26 April 2024; and interim **replacement of the statutory selection panel** with the earlier judicially created panel, refused on 22 March 2024. They matter because candidates routinely write as though these reliefs were granted. An answer that reports a refusal as an order is factually wrong, and the error is easy for an examiner to detect.

## Cumulative check 11 — the reform you should not propose

**Question:** Name two popular reforms this file rejects, and give the reason for each.

**Model answer:** First, **empowering the Commission to disqualify for corrupt practice**. A corrupt-practice finding carries a six-year civic disqualification and must rest on proof taken adversarially, with the naming safeguard that requires notice and hearing; and it would collapse the separation, built into Article 329(b), between conducting an election and judging it. Second, **converting the Model Code into a statute without more**. The Code's value is preventive speed inside a short campaign window; statutory enforcement routes it through courts, which are slower than elections. The defensible versions are time-bound election benches in the first case and a hybrid of rapid directions with published timelines, speaking orders and complaint data in the second.

## Cumulative check 12 — the participation chain

**Question:** An election is held with an accurate roll, audited machines and an uncontested count, yet a large number of entitled electors do not vote: some never knew they were eligible, some could not climb to a first-floor polling room, and some were offered money to stay away. Identify, for each failure, the link of the participation chain that broke and the legal or institutional instrument that addresses it.

**Model answer:** The first failure is at the **information** link, addressed by the Commission's standing voter-education programme, SVEEP, which must remain non-partisan and may urge people to vote but never how to vote. The second is at the **accessibility** link: standing instructions require ground-floor polling stations or a lift or extended ramp, standardised permanent ramps, wheelchairs, signage and priority entry; **Rule 49N** of the Conduct of Elections Rules, 1961 permits a companion for a blind or infirm elector; and **section 60(c)** of the RPA 1951 with **Rule 27A** permits notified categories of absentee voters — senior citizens above a notified age and persons with benchmark disability — to vote from home. The third is at the **ethical voting** link, where inducement is a corrupt practice under **section 123(1)** of the RPA 1951. The unifying proposition is that an uncast vote and a miscounted vote produce the same democratic loss, so a secure machine cannot cure an inaccessible roll or an unreachable booth.

## Cumulative check 13 — proposal or law?

**Question:** Sort the following into enacted law and unenacted proposal, and state the source of each: proxy voting for overseas electors; the remote voting machine for domestic migrants; registration of an overseas elector at the passport address; a fresh poll where NOTA polls highest; the party bearing the cost of a by-election caused by a dual victory; synchronised elections to the House of the People and all State Assemblies.

**Model answer:** **Enacted law:** registration of an overseas elector in the constituency of the place of residence in India entered in his passport, under **section 20A of the RPA 1950**, inserted in 2010 — a registration entitlement, with the vote cast in person. **Unenacted proposal, in each case:** proxy voting for overseas electors, proposed in 2017 and never enacted; the remote voting machine, developed as a prototype and demonstrated to political parties in January 2023, requiring legislative, administrative and technological change and never used in an election; a fresh poll where NOTA leads, which no provision supports after *People's Union for Civil Liberties* (2013); party-borne by-election costs, discussed as reform against **sections 33(7) and 70**; and simultaneous elections, which exist as **Article 82A in the Constitution (One Hundred and Twenty-ninth Amendment) Bill, 2024**, introduced on 17 December 2024 and referred to a Joint Committee of Parliament. The discipline the question tests is that in this topic the boundary between a recommendation and an enacted provision is itself an examinable fact.

## Cumulative check 14 — the closing proposition

**Question:** In one sentence each, state what the Election Commission is, what it is not, and what remains unresolved about it.

**Model answer:** **It is** a permanent, independent, all-India constitutional body vested by Article 324(1) with superintendence, direction and control of electoral rolls and of elections to Parliament, the State Legislatures and the offices of President and Vice-President. **It is not** an election court, a delimiting authority, a citizenship tribunal, a maker of disqualifications, or the authority for local-body elections. **What remains unresolved** is the design of its appointment, the asymmetry of removal protection among its members, and its financial and secretarial dependence — none of which concerns its capacity to run an election, and all of which concerns who is chosen to run it.

---

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

These three questions are distinct from every lesson-local question in the file and are intended to be attempted after the whole topic is complete.

## Final 10-mark question — 150 words

**Question:** "The Election Commission of India is strongest where the Constitution is specific and weakest where it is silent." Examine. (10 marks, 150 words)

**Model answer (150 words):**
The proposition maps the institution accurately. Where the Constitution is **specific**, the Commission is secure: Article 324(1) vests superintendence, direction and control over rolls and over elections to Parliament, the State Legislatures and the offices of President and Vice-President; Article 324(5) protects the Chief Election Commissioner with Supreme Court judge removal and bars adverse variation of his service conditions; and Article 324(6) obliges the President and Governors to supply staff on request. Where it is **silent**, vulnerability appears. Article 324(2) left appointment to a law Parliament did not make for over seven decades; the Constitution prescribes no qualifications, no term and no bar on later office; and unlike Article 322 for Public Service Commissions, Article 324 contains no charging clause, so establishment expenditure is a votable demand. The remedy is therefore legislative and practice-level — criteria, reasons, inquiry before removal, a secure secretariat — not an expansion of electoral power.

**Scoring note:** The question is comparative, so both halves need at least two constitutional references each. A script that praises the institution generally, or criticises it generally, has not examined the proposition.

## Final 15-mark question — 250 words

**Question:** "Every significant controversy about the Election Commission is a controversy about a boundary." Discuss with examples drawn from its powers, its advice and its adjudication. (15 marks, 250 words)

**Model answer (250 words):**
The claim holds across the topic, because almost every dispute asks where one authority's field ends.

**Boundary of field.** Article 324(1) names Parliament, the State Legislatures and the offices of President and Vice-President. Panchayat and municipal elections belong to State Election Commissions under Articles 243K and 243ZA. The recurring error of treating the Commission as the universal election authority is a boundary error.

**Boundary of power.** *Mohinder Singh Gill v. Chief Election Commissioner* (1978) makes Article 324 a reservoir where the law is silent; *A.C. Jose v. Sivan Pillai* (1984) holds that where an Act and express Rules exist the Commission may supplement but not supplant them. The controversy is never whether the Commission has power, but whether the statute had already spoken.

**Boundary of advice.** Under Articles 103 and 192 the Commission's opinion on a sitting member's disqualification binds the President or Governor, yet the decision is formally theirs; defection under the Tenth Schedule is outside the route altogether.

**Boundary of adjudication.** Paragraph 15 of the Symbols Order, 1968 lets the Commission decide which rival group is the recognised party, binding all; it does not decide party property, membership or leadership generally. Article 329(b) reserves the validity of an election to the High Court.

**Boundary of determination.** The Commission may inquire into citizenship for electoral purposes but cannot determine status, which belongs to the competent authority under the Citizenship Act, 1955.

**Verdict.** The institution's legitimacy depends on policing these boundaries as carefully in practice as the law states them.

**Scoring note:** The script must supply at least four distinct boundaries with a named source for each, and must not convert the question into a general account of powers. Reserve the top band for answers that state at least one boundary in the Commission's favour — that is, a power wrongly denied to it — as well as limits.

## Final 20-mark question — 250 words

**Question:** "Free and fair elections in India are secured less by any single guarantee than by the way several imperfect mechanisms correct one another." Critically analyse with reference to the Election Commission of India. (20 marks, 250 words)

**Model answer (250 words):**
The statement describes the system correctly, and its honesty is its strength.

**Each mechanism is imperfect.** Constitutional status does not secure appointment: Article 324(2) left that to a law absent for decades, and section 7 of Act 49 of 2023 now gives the executive two of three seats, with the challenge pending. Removal protection is asymmetric: only the Chief Election Commissioner has Supreme Court judge security, the others depending on his recommendation. Finance is dependent, since Article 324 has no analogue of Article 322. The field machinery is borrowed, with careers rooted in the parent government. The Model Code creates no offence. The machine audit is a sample, not a census. The election petition arrives after the House may have dissolved.

**Yet the mechanisms correct one another.** Article 324(6) and the deemed deputation of election officers convert borrowed staff into a commanded machinery. *Mohinder Singh Gill* supplies power where law is silent, while *A.C. Jose* prevents that power from displacing statute. The Model Code's weakness is offset by paragraph 16A of the Symbols Order, which can cost a recognised party its recognition. The sample audit is paired with randomisation, sealing, candidate presence and request-based burnt-memory verification. Article 329(b)'s bar is offset by a guaranteed post-result remedy that *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* held cannot be legislated away.

**Residue.** Correction is not cure: delay blunts the dispute remedy, appointment remains contested, and enforcement transparency is discretionary.

**Verdict.** Strengthen the weakest correctors — timelines, published reasons, appointment criteria — not powers.

**Scoring note:** At twenty marks the script must do both movements: name the imperfection **and** the specific corrector, with sources. A list of weaknesses without correctors, or of strengths without weaknesses, caps at the middle band. The top band additionally identifies which corrector is weakest and says why.

---

# REMEDIATION

Each entry below is an error this topic actually produces, the correction, and the lesson to reread.

## Error family 1: the universal-authority error

**The error.** Treating the Election Commission as the authority for every public election in India.

**Why it happens.** The institution's name and visibility suggest universality, and local-body polls use the same district officers.

**The correction.** Article 324(1) names Parliament, the State Legislatures and the offices of President and Vice-President. Panchayat elections belong to the State Election Commission under **Article 243K** and municipal elections under **Article 243ZA**. Shared staff and shared dates do not merge the authorities. **Reread Lesson 1.**

## Error family 2: the composition error

**The error.** Writing that the Constitution provides for a three-member Commission, or that the 2023 Act fixes the number at three.

**The correction.** **Article 324(2)** and **section 3 of Act 49 of 2023** both leave the number of other Election Commissioners to the **President** to fix from time to time. Three members is a settled practice since October 1993, not a legal requirement. **Reread Lesson 3 and Lesson 5.**

## Error family 3: the removal-symmetry error

**The error.** Writing that all members of the Commission are removed like Supreme Court judges, or that the Chief Election Commissioner removes an Election Commissioner.

**The correction.** Only the **Chief Election Commissioner** is removable in like manner and on like grounds as a Supreme Court judge (Article 324(5) first proviso; section 11(2)). Other Election Commissioners and Regional Commissioners are removable **only on the CEC's recommendation** (second proviso; section 11(3)) — and removal is effected by the **President**, not by the CEC. **Reread Lesson 3 and Lesson 6.**

## Error family 4: the chairmanship error

**The error.** Inferring from the CEC's chairmanship or stronger tenure that he has a superior or casting vote.

**The correction.** *T.N. Seshan v. Union of India* (1995) and **section 18(2)** of Act 49 of 2023 settle it: business is transacted unanimously as far as possible, and differences are decided by **majority**. Note also that **section 18(1)** requires **unanimity** for regulating procedure and allocating business. **Reread Lesson 3 and Lesson 6.**

## Error family 5: the Bill-stage error

**The error.** Stating that the 2023 Act fixes salary equal to the **Cabinet Secretary**, or that the Search Committee is headed by the Cabinet Secretary.

**The correction.** The enacted **section 10(1)** provides a salary **equal to the salary of a Judge of the Supreme Court**, and **section 6** places the **Minister of Law and Justice** at the head of the Search Committee. Both Cabinet Secretary formulations come from Bill-stage summaries. **Reread Lesson 5 and Lesson 6.**

## Error family 6: the *Anoop Baranwal* error

**The error.** Writing that the judgment permanently added the Chief Justice of India to the selection panel, struck down earlier appointments, or barred Parliament from legislating.

**The correction.** The committee of the Prime Minister, the Leader of Opposition and the Chief Justice of India was directed to operate **"till a law is made by the Parliament"**. Earlier appointments were not invalidated wholesale, and the judgment expressly preserved Parliament's space. The secretariat and charged-expenditure observation was a **fervent appeal**, not a direction. **Reread Lesson 4.**

## Error family 7: the status error

**The error.** Describing the 2023 Act as upheld, struck down or stayed; or describing a split verdict on **reference** as a verdict on **validity**.

**The correction.** The safe formulation is that the Act operates while the challenge to section 7 is pending; interim interference was declined on 22 March 2024; and on 23 September 2026 the matter was directed to be placed before the Chief Justice of India for the constitution of a larger Bench. Verify afresh before writing. **Reread Lesson 7.**

## Error family 8: the charged-expenditure error

**The error.** Inferring from salary parity with a Supreme Court judge that the Commission's expenditure is charged on the Consolidated Fund.

**The correction.** Article 324 has **no analogue of Article 322**. Establishment expenditure is presented as a demand for grants within the Ministry of Law and Justice demands and is votable. *Anoop Baranwal* **appealed** for charged status; the appeal is not self-executing. **Reread Lesson 7.**

## Error family 9: the statute-mix-up error

**The error.** Citing the **RPA 1950** for disqualification, corrupt practices or election petitions; or the **RPA 1951** for the preparation of electoral rolls.

**The correction.** **1950 builds the electorate** — allocation, delimitation framework, officers, rolls, appeals, bar on civil courts. **1951 runs the election** — notification, qualifications and disqualifications, party registration, conduct, expenses, corrupt practices, petitions. **Reread Lesson 9.**

## Error family 10: the qualifying-date and Aadhaar errors

**The error.** Saying the qualifying date is 1 January, or that Aadhaar is mandatory for enrolment or voting.

**The correction.** Act 49 of 2021 substituted **four qualifying dates** — 1 January, 1 April, 1 July and 1 October. Section 23(4) of the RPA 1950 permits the Electoral Registration Officer to **require** Aadhaar for identity and authentication; **section 23(6)** forbids denial of inclusion or deletion for inability to furnish it for a prescribed sufficient cause, with alternate documents contemplated. **Reread Lesson 10.**

## Error family 11: the roll-process errors

**The error.** Saying the Returning Officer handles roll deletions; saying civil courts may decide roll entitlement; or forgetting the hearing before deletion.

**The correction.** The **Electoral Registration Officer** acts under **sections 22 and 23**, with a mandatory **reasonable opportunity of being heard** before most deletions; appeal lies to the **Chief Electoral Officer** under **section 24**; **section 30** bars civil courts while leaving constitutional review intact. The Returning Officer belongs to the poll chain. **Reread Lesson 8 and Lesson 11.**

## Error family 12: the citizenship overreach, in both directions

**The error.** Either saying the Commission can decide who is a citizen, or saying it cannot look at citizenship at all.

**The correction.** The 2026 judgment permits a **limited inquiry into citizenship for electoral purposes** — necessarily so, since section 16(1)(a) of the RPA 1950 disqualifies a non-citizen from registration — while denying any power of final determination and requiring a **reference to the competent authority under the Citizenship Act, 1955** where the Commission is not satisfied. **Reread Lesson 11.**

## Error family 13: the right-to-vote error
