**The error.** Calling the vote a fundamental right, or citing *PUCL* (2003) for that proposition.

**The correction.** The dominant line — *Jyoti Basu* (1982), *Kuldip Nayar* (2006), *Rajbala* (2015) — treats the entitlement as **statutory**, conferred and conditioned by **section 62 of the RPA 1951**. *ADR* (2002) and *PUCL* (2003) protected the voter's **right to know** under Article 19(1)(a); *PUCL* (2013) protected the secret negative vote. A **concurring** opinion in *Anoop Baranwal* (2023) called it a constitutional right under Article 326. **Reread Lesson 12.**

## Error family 14: the notification error

**The error.** Writing that the Election Commission issues the election notification, or that the Model Code begins with the notification.

**The correction.** The Commission **announces** the schedule, and the **Model Code begins at the announcement**. The **President** (parliamentary constituencies) or **Governor** (Assembly constituencies) issues the statutory **notification** under the RPA 1951, and the statutory timetable runs from it. **Reread Lesson 13 and Lesson 15.**

## Error family 15: the candidature errors

**The error.** Saying a candidate may contest from any number of constituencies, or that the party must bear the by-election cost.

**The correction.** **Section 33(7)** caps a candidate at **two** constituencies, a cap dating from the **1996** amendment; **section 70** requires all but one seat to be vacated. Party-borne by-election cost has been **discussed as reform** and is not the present rule. **Reread Lesson 13.**

## Error family 16: the Model Code errors

**The error.** Calling the Code a statute, saying it was enacted in 1968, or saying breaches have no consequence.

**The correction.** The Code is **consensus-based and non-statutory**, consolidated by the Commission in **January 1991** after a State-level experiment in **1960** and an inter-party consensus through **1968–69**. Breaches may attract the RPA 1951 and the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 where the conduct is independently unlawful, and persistent defiance may cost a recognised party its recognition under **paragraph 16A** of the Symbols Order. **Reread Lesson 15.**

## Error family 17: the plenary-power slogan

**The error.** Citing *Mohinder Singh Gill* for unlimited power and omitting *A.C. Jose*.

**The correction.** Always state the pair: **reservoir** where the law is silent, and **supplement, never supplant**, where an Act or express Rules speak — with purpose, recorded reasons, fairness and judicial review applying in every case. **Reread Lesson 16.**

## Error family 18: the delimitation errors

**The error.** Saying the Commission delimits constituencies, or constitutes the Delimitation Commission, or that Article 329(b) bars delimitation challenges.

**The correction.** A **statutory** Delimitation Commission is constituted by the **Central Government**, chaired by a serving or retired **Supreme Court Judge**, with the **CEC or a nominated EC** and the **State Election Commissioner** ex officio. The Election Commission participates, implements and keeps the order up to date under **section 9 of the RPA 1950**. The bar is **Article 329(a)**. **Reread Lesson 17.**

## Error family 19: the *Lily Thomas* and stay errors

**The error.** Saying *Lily Thomas* struck down section 8, or that a stay of sentence suspends the disqualification.

**The correction.** It struck down **section 8(4)** only, ending the sitting-member shield; section 8 survives. Only a stay of the **conviction** suspends the disqualification. **Reread Lesson 18.**

## Error family 20: the EVM-judgment errors

**The error.** Saying the 26 April 2024 judgment ordered one hundred per cent VVPAT counting or a return to paper ballots, or that the VVPAT slip is handed to the voter.

**The correction.** All three reliefs were **refused**. What was granted was forty-five-day sealed preservation of Symbol Loading Units and a request-based check of burnt memories in **five per cent** of EVM sets, on written request within **seven days** by the candidate placed **second or third**, with the 7 May 2025 follow-up barring erasure of data before verification and requiring certification. The slip falls into a sealed box. **Reread Lesson 19.**

## Error family 21: the disclosure-into-exclusion error

**The error.** Treating *ADR* (2002) and *PUCL* (2003) as giving the Commission power to reject nominations of candidates with pending cases.

**The correction.** Those decisions established the **voter's right to know** under Article 19(1)(a) and made the Commission the disclosure platform. Disqualification is governed by Articles 102 and 191 and by Part II of the RPA 1951 and attaches to **conviction**, not accusation; and the Commission may not create a disqualification Parliament has not enacted. **Reread Lesson 16 and Lesson 20.**

## Error family 22: the reform-without-cost error

**The error.** Proposing Commission-led disqualification for corrupt practice, or a statutory Model Code, without pricing what is lost.

**The correction.** The first collapses the separation between conducting and judging an election and lowers protection against a six-year civic consequence; the second trades preventive speed for litigation. Sort every reform by whether it needs **Commission practice**, **legislation** or **constitutional amendment**, and say so. **Reread Lesson 18, Lesson 15 and Lesson 20.**

## Error family 23: the simultaneous-elections status error

**The error.** Writing that Article 82A exists in the Constitution, that simultaneous elections have been enacted or approved, that the Joint Committee has reported, or that the scheme will apply from a named election year.

**The correction.** Article 82A is a **clause in a Bill**. The **Constitution (One Hundred and Twenty-ninth Amendment) Bill, 2024** and the **Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2024** were introduced in the Lok Sabha on **17 December 2024** and referred to a **Joint Committee of Parliament chaired by P.P. Chaudhary**, whose report had not been presented as at this file's control date. The **High-Level Committee chaired by Ram Nath Kovind** reported on **14 March 2024**, and a report is a recommendation, not a law. Write the status, never the outcome. **Reread Lesson 20 §5.**

## Error family 24: the overseas-elector and remote-voting error

**The error.** Saying that overseas electors vote by post, by proxy or online; that the remote voting machine is in use; or that an overseas elector may register anywhere in India.

**The correction.** **Section 20A of the RPA 1950** gives a citizen who is not already on a roll, has not acquired another citizenship and is absent from his place of ordinary residence in India a right to be **registered** in the constituency of the **place of residence in India entered in his passport**. The franchise is exercised **in person**. A 2017 proxy-voting proposal did not become law; the remote voting machine remains a demonstrated **prototype**. **Reread Lesson 10 §4.**

## Error family 25: the participation-as-publicity error

**The error.** Describing voter education as optional outreach, treating turnout as proof of roll integrity, presenting home voting as a general facility, or forgetting that awareness work must be non-partisan.

**The correction.** Participation is an **integrity** question: information, registration, accessibility and ethical voting are four links, and a break in any of them produces an uncast vote. **SVEEP** has run since 2009 and may urge people to vote but never how to vote. Accessibility rests on standing instructions plus **Rule 49N** and **section 60(c)** with **Rule 27A**, which confine home voting to **notified categories** of absentee voters. A campaign is never a substitute for the statutory enrolment, hearing and appeal remedies. **Reread Lesson 13 §7.**

---
# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master provision map

```text
  CONSTITUTION, PART XV
    324(1)  field: rolls + elections to Parliament, State
            Legislatures, President, Vice-President
    324(2)  CEC + such other ECs as the President may fix;
            appointment by the President, subject to a
            parliamentary law
    324(3)  CEC is Chairman
    324(4)  Regional Commissioners, before specified general or
            biennial elections, after consulting the ECI
    324(5)  service conditions by rule;
            proviso 1 -- CEC removal like an SC judge; no adverse
                         variation of his service conditions
            proviso 2 -- other ECs and Regional Commissioners
                         removable only on the CEC's recommendation
    324(6)  President/Governor shall supply staff on request
    325     one general roll; no exclusion or special roll on
            grounds only of religion, race, caste or sex
    326     adult suffrage, 18 years (61st Amendment, 1988)
    327     Parliament's power to legislate on elections
    328     State Legislature's residual power
    329(a)  no court may question the validity of a delimitation
            or seat-allotment law
    329(b)  election challengeable only by election petition
    [329A]  inserted 1975; OMITTED by the 44th Amendment, 1978

  BOUNDARY ARTICLES
    71            President/Vice-President election disputes -> SC
    103 / 192     sitting member's disqualification -> President/
                  Governor on the ECI's binding opinion
    102(2)/191(2) defection -> Tenth Schedule -> Speaker/Chairman
    243K / 243ZA  panchayat/municipal elections -> SEC
    322           charged expenditure for PSCs --- NO analogue in 324

  STATUTES AND INSTRUMENTS
    RPA 1950      Pt II allocation/delimitation framework (ss.3-13)
                  Pt IIA officers (13A, 13AA, 13B, 13C, 13CC)
                  Pt III rolls (14-25), appeals (24), bar (30)
    RPA 1951      Pt II qualifications/disqualifications (3-11B)
                  29A party registration
                  Pt V conduct; 33(7), 70 candidature
                  77, 78, 10A expenses; 123 corrupt practices
                  126, 126A silence period and exit polls
                  62 right to vote
                  80-100, 116A election petitions
    Act 49 / 2021 Election Laws (Amendment) Act: 4 qualifying
                  dates; Aadhaar sub-sections; gender-neutral
                  service-voter language
    Act 49 / 2023 CEC and other ECs Act: ss.3-9 appointment and
                  term; 10-16 service and protection;
                  17-18 business; 21 repeal of Act 11 of 1991
    Symbols Order 6A, 6B, 6C recognition; 15 splits; 16
      1968        amalgamation; 16A recognition sanction;
                  17 notification of lists
```

## The appointment causal chain

```text
  Article 324(2) contemplates a parliamentary law on appointment
        |
  no such law for over seven decades
        |
  appointment by executive practice; Constitution silent on
  qualifications, term and post-retirement office
        |
  Anoop Baranwal (2 Mar 2023): interim committee
  PM + LoP/largest opposition leader + CJI, "till a law is made"
        |
  Act 49 of 2023: assent 28 Dec 2023, in force 2 Jan 2024
  s.5 eligibility | s.6 Law Minister Search Committee, panel of 5
  s.7 PM + LoP + PM-nominated Cabinet Minister | s.8 transparency
  and outside-panel power | s.9 term | s.21 repeals Act 11 of 1991
        |
  challenge to s.7: competence conceded, CONTENT contested
        |
  22 Mar 2024 interim interference declined (observations tentative)
        |
  23 Sep 2026 split on REFERENCE; papers to the CJI for a larger Bench
        |
  OUTCOME PENDING --- the Act operates; say no more
```

## The Article 324 power test

```text
                    claim of ECI power
                           |
            does the Constitution/Act/Rule speak?
                     /            \
                  YES              NO / genuine gap
                   |                      |
          must follow it;          Article 324 reservoir
          may supplement           (Mohinder Singh Gill, 1978)
          detail only                     |
          (A.C. Jose, 1984)        purpose: free and fair election
                   |                      |
                   |               limits: legality, relevance,
                   |               recorded reasons, fairness,
                   |               proportionality, judicial review
                   \______________________/
                              |
                     lawful exercise
```

## The roll chain and the poll chain

```text
  ROLL CHAIN  (RPA 1950)                POLL CHAIN  (RPA 1951)
  ------------------------------        ------------------------------
  Chief Electoral Officer  13A          Returning Officer
  District Election Officer 13AA        Presiding Officer
  Electoral Registration Officer 13B    polling parties
  Assistant ERO 13C                           |
  deemed deputation to ECI 13CC               |
        |                                     |
  entitlement 15-20                     nominations, scrutiny,
  revision 21(1), 21(2), 21(3)          withdrawal, symbol,
  correction 22 | inclusion 23          poll, count, declaration
  appeal to CEO 24 | fee 25                   |
  civil courts barred 30                Art 329(b) -> HIGH COURT
                                        election petition
```

## Forum map

| Question | Forum | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Entitlement to be on the roll | Electoral Registration Officer, appeal to Chief Electoral Officer | RPA 1950 ss.22, 23, 24 |
| Validity of an election to Parliament or a State Legislature | High Court, appeal to the Supreme Court | Art 329(b); RPA 1951 ss.80, 80A, 116A |
| Sitting member's Article 102/191 disqualification | President or Governor, on the Commission's binding opinion | Arts 103, 192 |
| Defection | Speaker or Chairman | Tenth Schedule |
| Presidential or Vice-Presidential election | Supreme Court | Art 71 |
| Which faction is a recognised party for symbol purposes | Election Commission, quasi-judicially | Symbols Order para 15 |
| Party property, funds or membership | Civil courts | General law |
| Final determination of citizenship | Competent authority | Citizenship Act, 1955 |
| Validity of a delimitation law | No court | Art 329(a) |

## Independence: the product, factor by factor

| Factor | What secures it | What is missing |
|---|---|---|
| Appointment | Statutory Selection Committee, eligibility standard, transparency duty | Criteria, dossier time, recorded reasons; composition under challenge |
| Tenure and removal | CEC removable only like a Supreme Court judge; others only on his recommendation | No procedure governing the recommendation; asymmetry among members |
| Finance | Salary parity with a Supreme Court judge | No charging clause; establishment expenditure is a votable demand |
| Secretariat and staff | Article 324(6) duty; deemed deputation under section 13CC | No dedicated cadre; careers rooted in parent governments |
| Collegial process | Section 18 and *T.N. Seshan*; unanimity for procedure and allocation | Dependence of dissenting members on the CEC for tenure |
| Transparent enforcement | Article 324 powers; paragraph 16A sanction | Complaint, reasons and outcome data not systematically published |

## One-line comparative verdicts

| Pair | Verdict |
|---|---|
| ECI and State Election Commission | Same design, different constitutional field; never merged by shared staff or dates |
| Article 324 and Article 327 | Supervision and specification; complementary, not competing |
| Article 329(a) and Article 329(b) | The map is immune; the election is deferred to a petition |
| *Mohinder Singh Gill* and *A.C. Jose* | Reservoir and ceiling; cite them together or neither |
| Registration and recognition | Statutory status against performance-based status under an order |
| Model Code and the RPA | A non-penal code with statutory overlaps and a recognition sanction |
| Section 10A and section 8A | The Commission disqualifies in the first and only advises in the second |
| Ordinary revision and special revision | Calendar-driven against reason-driven, the latter available at any time |
| Sample VVPAT verification and full counting | Layered assurance against a single measure the Court refused |
| Disclosure and disqualification | Information relocates the judgment; it does not transfer the power |

---

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## Constitutional field and ownership

- **Article 324(1)**: superintendence, direction and control of (i) preparation of electoral rolls for, and (ii) conduct of, all elections to Parliament and to the Legislature of every State, and of elections to the offices of (iii) President and (iv) Vice-President.
- Permanent, independent, all-India constitutional body; protects the conditions of choice rather than choosing.
- Excluded: panchayats (**Article 243K**) and municipalities (**Article 243ZA**) — State Election Commissions; cooperative societies; internal party organisational elections.
- State Election Commissioner is appointed by the **Governor** and removable like a **High Court** judge.
- **Article 324(6)**: the President or a Governor **shall**, when so requested, make staff available.
- Powers sort three ways: **administrative** (the Commission acts), **advisory** (it opines; another authority decides), **quasi-judicial** (it decides a dispute after notice and hearing). Mnemonic: run, advise, decide. The classification describes form, not source; the source is Article 324 and the statutes.
- **Article 356(5)**: continuance of President's rule beyond one year requires a Proclamation of Emergency in operation **and** the Commission's certificate that difficulties prevent holding Assembly elections.

## Part XV spine

- **325**: one general roll; no exclusion or special roll **on grounds only of** religion, race, caste or sex. Age, citizenship, residence and statutory disqualifications survive.
- **326**: adult suffrage for the Lok Sabha and State Assemblies; eighteen years; subject to non-residence, unsoundness of mind, crime, corrupt or illegal practice. Age lowered from twenty-one by the **Sixty-first Amendment, 1988**.
- **327**: Parliament legislates on elections to Parliament and State Legislatures — source of the RPA 1950 and 1951.
- **328**: State Legislature legislates only where Parliament has not.
- **329(a)**: validity of a delimitation or seat-allotment law not questionable in any court.
- **329(b)**: an election challengeable only by election petition.
- **329A**: inserted by the Thirty-ninth Amendment, 1975; the relevant clause struck down in *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975); the article **omitted** by the Forty-fourth Amendment, 1978.

## Composition, chairmanship and evolution

- **324(2)**: CEC plus such number of other ECs, **if any**, as the President may fix; appointment by the President **subject to a parliamentary law**.
- **324(3)**: CEC is **Chairman** when other ECs are appointed.
- **324(4)**: Regional Commissioners, appointed by the President **after consulting the ECI**, **before** specified general or biennial elections, **to assist**.
- Single-member from **25 January 1950**; two ECs on **16 October 1989**; posts discontinued **January 1990**; two ECs again in **October 1993**; three-member since.
- *T.N. Seshan v. Union of India* (1995): multi-member body valid; equal decisional status; majority rule; chairmanship is not supremacy.

## Removal and protection

- **324(5) proviso 1**: CEC removable only in like manner and on like grounds as a **Supreme Court judge** — address by each House with a majority of total membership and two-thirds of those present and voting, on **proved misbehaviour or incapacity**; service conditions not to be varied to his disadvantage.
- **324(5) proviso 2**: other ECs and Regional Commissioners removable **only on the CEC's recommendation**.
- **Section 11, Act 49 of 2023** restates this; resignation is addressed to the **President**.
- **Section 16**: no court shall **entertain or continue** civil or criminal proceedings against a present or former CEC or EC for an act, thing or word in, or purporting to be in, discharge of official duty. It does not cover private conduct, validate unlawful orders or bar review of the Commission's decisions.

## Appointment: from vacuum to Act 49 of 2023

- **Act 11 of 1991** governed service conditions and transaction of business, not selection; **repealed** by section 21 of the 2023 Act.
- Three constitutional silences: no prescribed qualifications; no specified term; no bar on later government appointment.
- ***Anoop Baranwal v. Union of India***, **2 March 2023**: interim committee of **PM + LoP in the Lok Sabha (or leader of the single largest opposition party) + CJI**, operative **"till a law is made by the Parliament"**; past appointments not invalidated; a **fervent appeal** for a permanent secretariat and charged expenditure.
- **Act No. 49 of 2023**: assent **28 December 2023**; in force **2 January 2024** (S.O. 35(E)).
- **s.3** number fixed by the President; **s.4** appointment by warrant under the President's hand and seal; **s.5** Secretary-equivalent rank, integrity, knowledge and experience of election management and conduct; **s.6** Search Committee headed by the **Minister of Law and Justice**, two members not below Secretary rank, **panel of five**; **s.7** Selection Committee of **PM (Chairperson) + Leader of Opposition in the Lok Sabha + PM-nominated Union Cabinet Minister**, with an Explanation deeming the leader of the single largest opposition party to be LoP where none is recognised, and a saving for vacancy or defect; **s.8(1)** transparent self-regulated procedure, **s.8(2)** power to consider a person outside the panel; **s.9** six years or age sixty-five, whichever is earlier, **no re-appointment**, EC-plus-CEC service capped at six years in aggregate.
- **s.10(1)** salary **equal to a Judge of the Supreme Court** (not the Cabinet Secretary); **s.10(3)** dearness allowance on the same basis; **s.10(4)** fifty per cent earned-leave encashment at completion of tenure.
- **s.17** business under the Act; **s.18(1)** procedure and allocation of business by **unanimous** decision; **s.18(2)** unanimity as far as possible, else **majority**.
- **s.19** difficulty-removal power for **five years**; **s.20** laying of rules and orders.

## Independence, finance and status discipline

- Independence is a **product** of appointment, tenure, finance, secretariat, collegiality and transparent enforcement.
- **Article 324 has no analogue of Article 322**; establishment expenditure is a **votable demand** presented within the Ministry of Law and Justice demands.
- Salary parity does not imply charged expenditure.
- **Status, checked 2 October 2026**: the challenge to **section 7** is pending; interim interference declined **22 March 2024** with observations treated as tentative; **23 September 2026** split verdict on **reference**, papers directed to the **Chief Justice of India** for a larger Bench. Permitted: "the Act operates while the challenge is pending". Prohibited: upheld, struck down, stayed, or CJI restored.

## Machinery

- **RPA 1950 Part IIA**: **13A** Chief Electoral Officer; **13AA** District Election Officer; **13B** Electoral Registration Officer; **13C** Assistant ERO; **13CC** these officers are **deemed on deputation to the Commission** during election work, with its control, superintendence and discipline.
- Returning Officers and Presiding Officers belong to the poll chain under the RPA 1951 and the rules; the District Election Officer is usually the District Magistrate or Collector.
- Deputy Election Commissioners are drawn from the civil services into the Commission's secretariat.
- Observers — general, police, expenditure, micro, awareness — report directly to the Commission; they report and recommend, and do not replace statutory officers.
- The Commission's transfer power during the election period rests on Article 324 plus section 13CC plus Article 324(6); it is functional and time-bound.

## Two statutes

- **RPA 1950 (Act 43 of 1950, 12 May 1950)** — allocation of seats, delimitation framework and consolidation, officers, electoral rolls, appeals, bar on civil courts.
- **RPA 1951 (Act 43 of 1951)** — notification, qualifications and disqualifications, party registration, conduct, expenses, corrupt practices, offences, election petitions.
- Mnemonic: **1950 builds the electorate; 1951 runs the election**.
- Junctions: being an **elector** is a statutory qualification for membership; section 16 of the 1950 Act links roll disqualification to corrupt-practice disqualification.

## Electoral rolls

- **s.15** a roll for every constituency under the Commission's superintendence, direction and control.
- **s.16** disqualifications for registration: non-citizen; unsound mind so declared by a competent court; for the time being disqualified from voting under a corrupt-practices or election-offences law.
- **s.19** entitlement: **eighteen years on the qualifying date** and **ordinarily resident** in the constituency.
- **s.17, s.18** no multiple registration across or within constituencies.
- **s.20** ordinary residence: house ownership alone insufficient (20(1)); temporary absence does not break it (20(1A)); sitting MP or MLA absent on duties retains it (20(1B)); mental-illness establishment, prison or legal custody excluded (20(2)); service qualification deems residence at home (20(3)); declared offices (20(4)).
- **s.20A** (inserted 2010): an Indian citizen not already on a roll, who has not acquired another citizenship and is absent from his place of ordinary residence in India for employment, education or otherwise, is entitled to registration in the constituency of the **place of residence in India entered in his passport**; enrolment on the prescribed overseas form with passport and visa pages, verified through the booth-level officer or the Indian Mission; carried in a separate section at the end of the roll; identity established by the passport. The vote is cast **in person**: no postal, proxy, online or remote facility exists in law.
- **Act 49 of 2021**, assent **29 December 2021**: four qualifying dates — **1 January, 1 April, 1 July, 1 October**; Aadhaar sub-sections **23(4), 23(5), 23(6)**; rule-making heads **28(2)(hhha), (hhhb)**; gender-neutral service-voter language in **s.20(6)** of the 1950 Act and **s.60(b)(ii)** of the 1951 Act.
- **s.21(1)** preparation; **s.21(2)** revision, with a proviso saving an unrevised roll; **s.21(3)** **special revision at any time, for reasons to be recorded, in such manner as the Commission thinks fit**, with the existing roll continuing until completion.
- **s.22** correction, transposition and deletion, with a **reasonable opportunity of being heard** before most actions (death excepted).
- **s.23(1)–(2)** inclusion with intimation to the other constituency; **s.23(3)** freeze between the last date for nominations and completion of that election.
- **s.24** appeal to the **Chief Electoral Officer**; **s.25** fee; **s.30** civil courts barred on entitlement and on the legality of an Electoral Registration Officer's action.
- ***Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India***, **27 May 2026** (CJI Surya Kant and Bagchi J., reserved 29 January 2026): special intensive revision upheld as within **s.21(3)** and furthering Article 324, not supplanting the statute; proportionality assessed in implementation; Rule 21A safeguards preserved **in substance** across enumeration, draft publication and claims-and-objections; documentation regime upheld on intelligible criteria with the acceptable-document list expanded; **limited citizenship inquiry for electoral purposes** permitted, **final determination excluded**, with mandatory reference to the competent authority under the **Citizenship Act, 1955**; *Lal Babu Hussein* (1995) presumption held **rebuttable**.

## Right to vote

- Entitlement is **statutory** on the dominant line: *N.P. Ponnuswami* (1952); *Jyoti Basu v. Debi Ghosal* (1982) — "neither a fundamental right nor a Common Law Right... pure and simple, a statutory right"; *Kuldip Nayar* (2006); *Rajbala* (2015). Operative source: **s.62, RPA 1951**; **s.62(5)** bars persons confined in prison or lawful police custody, upheld in *Anukul Chandra Pradhan* (1997).
- Expressive dimensions are protected: *ADR* (2002) and *PUCL* (2003) — the **right to know**; *PUCL* (2013) — the secret **negative vote**.
- *Anoop Baranwal* (2023): a **concurring** opinion treats the vote as a **constitutional right** under Article 326.

## The poll cycle

- Announcement by the Commission starts the **Model Code**; the **President or Governor** issues the statutory **notification**.
- Candidature: Articles 84 and 173; RPA 1951 Part II; valid nomination and deposit; scrutiny by the Returning Officer. The Commission cannot add a disqualification.
- **s.33(7)** two-constituency cap (from the **1996** amendment); **s.70** vacate all but one.
- **s.77** account of expenses subject to a ceiling; **s.78** lodging within **thirty days**; **s.10A** disqualification up to **three years** for failure to lodge; **s.123(6)** excess expenditure is a corrupt practice.
- Monitoring: expenditure observers, flying squads, static surveillance teams, video teams, accounting teams, shadow registers, rate cards, media certification and monitoring.
- Graduated responses: station-level fresh poll; statutory adjournment or fresh poll; countermand with recorded reasons; post-result petition.
- **s.126** forty-eight-hour restrictions before the close of poll; **s.126A** exit-poll bar during the notified period.
- NOTA (*PUCL*, 2013): secret rejection option; not a candidate; no automatic re-poll.
- Participation chain: **information → registration → accessibility → ethical voting**. **SVEEP**, the Commission's voter-education programme since 2009, works the first two links and must be non-partisan. Accessibility rests on standing instructions — ground-floor stations or lift and extended ramp, standardised permanent ramps, wheelchairs, signage, priority entry and observer verification — plus **Rule 49N** (companion for a blind or infirm elector) and **s.60(c)** with **Rule 27A** (home voting for notified categories of absentee voters: senior citizens above a notified age and persons with benchmark disability, on the prescribed application). Inducement is a corrupt practice under **s.123(1)**. A secure machine cannot cure an inaccessible roll or an unreachable booth.

## Parties and symbols

- **s.29A, RPA 1951**: registration; memorandum must affirm true faith and allegiance to the Constitution and to socialism, secularism and democracy, and uphold sovereignty, unity and integrity.
- **Symbols Order, 1968**: **para 6A** State-party conditions — six per cent plus two Assembly seats; six per cent plus one Lok Sabha seat from the State; three per cent of Assembly seats or three seats, whichever is more; one Lok Sabha member per twenty-five allotted to the State; or eight per cent of valid votes. **Para 6B** National-party conditions — six per cent in four or more States plus four Lok Sabha members; two per cent of Lok Sabha seats from at least three States; or State-party status in four States. **Para 6C** continued recognition. **Para 15** split disputes, decision that one group **or none** is the party, binding on all. **Para 16** amalgamation. **Para 16A** suspension or withdrawal of recognition for defiance of the Model Code or lawful directions, after show cause. **Para 17** Gazette lists of parties and symbols.
- *Sadiq Ali v. Election Commission of India* (1972) upheld the paragraph 15 jurisdiction; *Kanhiya Lal Omar v. R.K. Trivedi* (1985) upheld the Order itself.
- Outside the jurisdiction: party property and funds, membership litigation, general leadership determination, Tenth Schedule disqualification.

## Model Code of Conduct

- **Consensus-based and non-statutory**; authority from **Article 324** plus party consensus.
- **1960** Kerala experiment; **1968–69** inter-party minimum-code consensus; consolidated by the Commission in **January 1991**; successively expanded.
- Operates **from the announcement of the schedule until the completion of the election process**.
- Eight fields: general conduct, meetings, processions, polling day, polling booth, observers, **party in power**, election manifestos.
- Manifestos: nothing repugnant to constitutional ideals; broad indication of rationale and of ways and means of financing promises.
- Tools: advisory, censure, direction to withdraw, campaigning restraint, transfer or replacement of officials, deferral of schemes, direction to prosecute; and **paragraph 16A** recognition sanction.
- Overlap with the **RPA 1951** and the **Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023**.
- A Parliamentary Standing Committee has urged statutory status; the Commission resists, preferring speed. Defensible reform is hybrid: timelines, speaking orders, published complaint data, mandatory statutory referrals.

## Article 324 doctrine

- *Mohinder Singh Gill v. Chief Election Commissioner* (1978): Article 324 is a **reservoir** where the enacted law is silent; purposive; not arbitrary; natural justice applies with content and timing adapted to exigency; an order is judged on its recorded reasons.
- *A.C. Jose v. Sivan Pillai* (1984): where an Act and express Rules exist, the Commission may **supplement but not supplant**; voting machines used without legal authority at that time were impermissible, and the law was later amended to provide for them.
- *Kanhiya Lal Omar* (1985): the reservoir validly used to build the Symbols Order.
- The Commission cannot: run local-body elections; contradict an Act or Rule; create a disqualification; void an election; decide defection; delimit generally; determine citizenship; make the Model Code penal; assume tampering; decide party property.

## Delimitation

- Statutory Delimitation Commission constituted by the **Central Government** under a Delimitation Act; chaired by a serving or retired **Supreme Court Judge**, with the **CEC or a nominated EC** and the **State Election Commissioner** ex officio; MPs and MLAs associated without a vote.
- Final order published in the Gazette has the **force of law**; **Article 329(a)** bars challenge to the validity of delimitation legislation.
- **RPA 1950**: ss.3, 4, 7 allocation; **s.7(1A)-(1C)** and the **Second Schedule** fix SC/ST reserved-seat numbers; s.8 consolidation; **s.9** Commission's power to keep the order up to date; **ss.9A, 9B are omitted** — identifying the specific reserved constituencies is a statutory **Delimitation Commission** function, not a current ECI power; ss.12, 13 alteration and procedure.
- Delimitation Commission Acts of **1952, 1962, 1972** and the **Delimitation Act, 2002**, linked to the 1951, 1961, 1971 and 2001 Censuses.
- A **State-specific** exercise for Jammu and Kashmir was constituted in 2020 and completed its final order in 2022; the wider debate about readjustment after a future census belongs with the study of Parliament and federal design.

## Disqualification and disputes

- **Arts 103 and 192**: the President or Governor **shall obtain the Commission's opinion and act according to it** on a sitting member's Article 102(1) or 191(1) disqualification.
- **RPA 1951**: **s.8** conviction-based (two years or more; disqualified from conviction and six years from release); **s.8A** corrupt practices, decided by the President on the Commission's opinion; **s.9** dismissal for corruption or disloyalty; **s.9A** government contracts; **s.10** office under a government company; **s.10A** failure to lodge expense accounts, **the Commission** disqualifying for up to three years; **ss.11, 11A, 11B** removal or reduction and linked periods.
- ***Lily Thomas v. Union of India*** (2013) struck down **s.8(4)** only; conviction now operates immediately; only a stay of the **conviction** suspends the disqualification.
- **s.123** corrupt practices: bribery; **undue influence** — interference with the free exercise of an electoral right; identity-based appeals and promotion of enmity; false statements about a candidate; hiring vehicles; excess expenditure; obtaining government servants' assistance.
- Disproportionate assets are an anti-corruption and disclosure problem, not automatically undue influence; a specific inducement could separately attract bribery.
- **ss.80, 80A, 81 (45 days), 83, 86, 98, 99, 100, 116A (30 days)**: the petition route. Some s.100 grounds are absolute; others require that the result was **materially affected**.
- *N.P. Ponnuswami* (1952): Article 329(b) is an **exclusive** remedy; no midstream interference. *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975): free and fair elections and judicial review are basic features.
- **Article 71**: Presidential and Vice-Presidential election disputes go to the Supreme Court.

## EVM and VVPAT

- Ballot Unit, Control Unit, VVPAT; the slip is displayed and falls into a sealed box, never handed to the voter; the Symbol Loading Unit loads candidate data before the poll.
- *Subramanian Swamy v. ECI* (2013): paper trail directed in phases. In 2019 verification was raised to **five randomly selected polling stations per Assembly constituency or segment**.
- ***ADR v. ECI***, **26 April 2024**: paper-ballot return **refused**; slip-to-voter **refused**; **one hundred per cent counting refused**. Directions: Symbol Loading Units sealed, signed and kept with the EVMs for **at least forty-five days** after declaration; on written request within **seven days** by the candidate placed **second or third**, burnt memory or microcontroller in **five per cent** of EVM sets per Assembly constituency or segment checked by manufacturers' engineers, cost borne by the requester and refunded if tampering is found.
- **7 May 2025** follow-up: manufacturers' engineers to certify integrity; data not to be erased before verification; mock poll available after recorded data is displayed; Symbol Loading Unit data may be retained for it.
- Trust framework: technical security, physical custody, candidate observation, statistical and legal audit, transparent incident response.

## Information, money and reform

- *Union of India v. ADR* (2002) and *PUCL* (2003): the voter's **right to know** candidates' criminal, financial and educational antecedents under **Article 19(1)(a)**; the Commission is the disclosure platform, not a disqualifying authority.
- **15 February 2024**: the electoral bond scheme and connected amendments struck down as violating the right to information under **Article 19(1)(a)**; details directed to be furnished to the Commission and published. Political funding beyond that publication role is studied with political parties and party funding.
- The regulated unit is the candidate; the real unit of campaigning is the ecosystem.
- Named reform lineage: **Tarkunde (1975)** — who appoints the Commission, voting age; **Dinesh Goswami (1990)** — appointment consultation, decriminalisation, expenditure control, identity cards; **Indrajit Gupta (1998)** — State funding in kind for recognised parties; **Law Commission 170th (1999)** — party finance disclosure, internal party democracy, charge-stage disqualification; **NCRWC (2002)** — a law on political parties; **Second ARC, Ethics in Governance (2007)**; **Law Commission 255th (2015)** — comprehensive package. On simultaneous elections: **Parliamentary Standing Committee (2015)**, **Law Commission draft (2018)** and the **High-Level Committee chaired by Ram Nath Kovind**, constituted September 2023, report **14 March 2024**.
- The Kovind Committee's scheme is two-step: first the House of the People with all State Assemblies; then municipal and panchayat elections within **one hundred days**, a step that reaches Articles 243K and 243ZA and would also require ratification by the States under the proviso to **Article 368(2)**.
- **Proposed, not enacted**: **Article 82A** in the **Constitution (One Hundred and Twenty-ninth Amendment) Bill, 2024**, with the **Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2024**; introduced **17 December 2024**; referred to a **Joint Committee chaired by P.P. Chaudhary**; report not presented as at the control date. Mechanism: an **appointed date** notified on the first sitting of a new House of the People; Assemblies constituted thereafter end with that House's **full term**, the article operating **notwithstanding Articles 83 and 172**; a premature dissolution yields an election for the **unexpired term** only; and the Commission may recommend deferring a particular Assembly election, which the President may order for a later date.
- Objections to weigh: federal (nationalising State mandates), accountability (short transitional terms; the framers preferred accountability to stability), feasibility (postponement without a stated outer limit or parliamentary approval, unlike Article 356), and Commission capacity. Reply: the field of Article 324 is untouched and the steady state restores full terms; residual: the one-year House and the unexplained postponement asymmetry.
- Status vocabulary that is always safe: proposed, introduced, referred, under examination. Never: enacted, approved, reported, applicable from a named year.
- Reform compass: **Commission practice** (complaint-to-outcome records, verification and discrepancy data, reasoned published orders, published appointment criteria and recorded reasons) → **legislation** (election benches with enforced timelines, auditable party and third-party campaign spending, permanent secretariat and charged expenditure) → **constitutional amendment** (equalised removal with an inquiry safeguard).
- Reforms to reject with reasons: Commission-led disqualification for corrupt practice; a bare statutory Model Code.
- Answer method: constitutional claim → named evidence → institutional analysis → qualification → reform verdict.

## Last-minute recall board

```text
324 = rolls + Parliament + State Legislatures + President + VP
243K / 243ZA = local bodies -> State Election Commission
President fixes the number; CEC chairs; equal votes; majority
CEC removal like an SC judge; others only on the CEC's recommendation
Anoop Baranwal = interim panel "till a law is made"
Act 49 of 2023 = Law Minister search, panel of 5; PM + LoP + Minister
6 years / 65; no re-appointment; salary = SC judge; 1991 Act repealed
Status = Act operates; challenge pending; reference to a larger Bench
1950 builds the electorate; 1951 runs the election
Rolls: 18 on a qualifying date (1 Jan / 1 Apr / 1 Jul / 1 Oct)
       + ordinary residence; s.21(3) special revision, reasons recorded
Citizenship = limited electoral inquiry; refer for determination
Right to vote = statutory; right to know = Article 19(1)(a)
33(7) = two constituencies; 10A = ECI disqualifies for 3 years
29A = registration; Symbols Order = recognition; 15 = split; 16A = sanction
MCC = non-statutory, from announcement to completion
Mohinder = reservoir; A.C. Jose = supplement, never supplant
Delimitation = statutory Commission; 329(a) bar; ECI assists
Disputes = High Court petition; 116A appeal; Art 71 for President/VP
EVM 2024 = no paper ballot, no 100% count; SLU 45 days; 5% on request
20A = overseas elector at the passport address; votes IN PERSON
Participation = information -> registration -> accessibility -> ethics
82A = PROPOSED only; Bill of 2024, Joint Committee, no report
```

**Coverage conclusion.** Every row above names a specific place in the file where a specific unit is taught and a specific place where it is practised; the conclusion claims nothing beyond those rows. Read together, they cover the constitutional design, composition, appointment, tenure, removal, protection and conduct of business; the three-fold classification of the Commission's powers with the standard fifteen-function enumeration and its two loose formulations corrected; the machinery, officers and observers; the two statutes and their division of labour; roll entitlement, ordinary residence, overseas electors, qualifying dates, Aadhaar, revision and the citizenship boundary; the character of the electoral right; the poll cycle from announcement to declaration, including candidature, expenditure, repoll and NOTA; voter education, participation and accessibility; parties, symbols and the Model Code; Article 324 doctrine with its negative limits; delimitation; disqualification and the dispute route; machine trust; disclosure, political finance, the named committee reform lineage and the simultaneous-elections proposal with its exact legislative status.

Two further statements, so that the conclusion is testable rather than decorative. **First**, three units are taught here only to the extent that this institution owns them, and that limit is stated in the lesson itself rather than used to avoid teaching: Tenth Schedule adjudication, which the Commission has no role in; the full offence taxonomy of the Representation of the People Act, 1951, of which only the provisions bearing on the Commission's functions are developed; and citizenship law beyond electoral registration, which is argued only as far as the referral obligation. **Second**, the federal consequences of synchronising legislative terms and the design of party funding beyond the Commission's publication role are developed further elsewhere, but the parts of each that this topic owns — the proposed mechanism, the Commission's role and capacity under it, the status discipline, and the Commission as publication authority — are taught in full above, with rows to prove it.

Artifacts under the permanently excluded final-package path and all learner-v2 material were neither read nor used as evidence. `learning_package_final\` was not consulted; the canonical Markdown, the book and the primary legal text resolved every requirement.

**Corrections made to the canonical sources, disclosed.** Three formulations in those sources were not reproduced, because primary text contradicts them. First, the Basic owner's summary of the independence safeguards describes the Commission's expenditure as "charged". That is not the position: Article 324 contains no analogue of Article 322, the Commission's establishment expenditure is presented as a votable demand for grants, and *Anoop Baranwal* made an appeal — not a direction — that charged expenditure be considered. Lesson 7 §3 and Remediation 8 state the correct position, while preserving the genuine Article 324 against Article 322 contrast that the owner was reaching for. Second, the Advanced owner's appointment material carries Bill-stage phrasing that the enacted Act 49 of 2023 superseded; the enacted text governs throughout. Third, the standard textbook enumeration of functions describes the Commission as determining constituency boundaries and as scrutinising nomination papers; both are loosely stated, and Lesson 1 §6 corrects them against the delimitation framework and the Returning Officer's statutory role.

## OCR books

| Book | Pages used | Control |
|---|---|---|
| `books\Indian Polity by M Laxmikant.pdf` | PDF pages 867–874 (Chapter 42, "Election Commission") | Supplied the composition summary, the independence safeguards and the three flagged constitutional silences, the three-fold classification of powers with the fifteen listed functions, the secretariat and field-machinery description, and the chapter note recording the Delimitation Commission Acts of 1952, 1962, 1972 and 2002. Its clause (4) summary of Regional Commissioners omits the "before each general election" timing condition, which has been restored from the constitutional text; its statement that the conditions of service and tenure "shall be determined by the President" is read with the opening words of Article 324(5), "Subject to the provisions of any law made by Parliament", now operative through Act 49 of 2023 |

The operative effects of the remaining sub-clauses — the alignment of Assembly terms with the full term of the House of the People, the unexpired-term rule after a premature dissolution, and the Commission's power to recommend deferring a particular Assembly election — are taken from the published legislative summary of the Bill as introduced, because the substantive pages of the Gazette copy held for this file carry no extractable text layer. ## Judicial sources and their stated limits

| Case | Used for | Limit observed |
|---|---|---|
| *Mohinder Singh Gill v. Chief Election Commissioner* (1978) | Article 324 as a reservoir; purposive exercise; flexible natural justice; judging an order on its recorded reasons | Not cited for unlimited power; always paired with *A.C. Jose* |
| *Sadiq Ali v. Election Commission of India* (1972) | Upholding the split-dispute jurisdiction and its tests | Not used for ownership of party property or leadership generally |
| *A.C. Jose v. Sivan Pillai* (1984) | Supplement, not supplant, where an Act and express Rules exist | Not represented as holding voting machines unconstitutional; the subsequent amendment of the law is stated |
| *Kanhiya Lal Omar v. R.K. Trivedi* (1985) | Upholding the Symbols Order and the Commission's symbol authority | Confined to the election-symbol field |
| *T.N. Seshan v. Union of India* (1995) | Multi-member body, equal decisional status, majority rule | The CEC's distinct removal protection expressly preserved |
| *N.P. Ponnuswami v. Returning Officer* (1952) | Article 329(b) as an exclusive remedy; no midstream interference | Later facilitative-review refinement noted |
| *Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain* (1975) | Free and fair elections and judicial review as basic features | Article 329A's later omission in 1978 stated separately |
| *Jyoti Basu v. Debi Ghosal* (1982); *Kuldip Nayar* (2006); *Rajbala* (2015) | The statutory characterisation of the electoral right | Presented as the dominant line, not as the only view |
| *Anukul Chandra Pradhan v. Union of India* (1997) | Upholding s.62(5) | Used only for the permissibility of statutory conditions |
| *Union of India v. ADR* (2002); *PUCL v. Union of India* (2003) | The voter's right to know under Article 19(1)(a) | Not used to confer a power to reject nominations |
| *PUCL v. Union of India* (2013) | The secret NOTA option | Expressly not a right to void the election |
| *Subramanian Swamy v. ECI* (2013) | Phased VVPAT paper trail | Not a holding against electronic voting |
| *Lily Thomas v. Union of India* (2013) | Striking down s.8(4) | Section 8 itself preserved; stay must be of the conviction |
| *Anoop Baranwal v. Union of India* (2 March 2023) | Interim appointment committee until a parliamentary law; the appeal on secretariat and charged expenditure; the separate concurrence on the character of the right to vote | The interim direction's express temporal limit, the non-operative character of the appeal, and the concurrence's status are each stated |
| *Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India* (26 April 2024), with the follow-up of 7 May 2025 | EVM and VVPAT reliefs refused; the two directions granted; the verification procedure | Expressly not a finding of tampering |
| Electoral-funding judgment of 15 February 2024 | The exact ground — the voter's right to information under Article 19(1)(a) — and the Commission's publication role | Political-funding policy beyond that role is cross-linked, not owned |
| *Association for Democratic Reforms v. Election Commission of India* (27 May 2026) | The four holdings on the special intensive revision, including the citizenship boundary and the reference obligation | The judgment is treated as deciding the exercise before it, not as authorising any future State exercise in advance |

| Simultaneous-elections proposal | The **Constitution (One Hundred and Twenty-ninth Amendment) Bill, 2024** (Bill No. 275 of 2024), proposing a new Article 82A, and the **Union Territories Laws (Amendment) Bill, 2024** were introduced in the Lok Sabha on **17 December 2024** and referred to a **Joint Committee of Parliament chaired by P.P. Chaudhary**. The Committee was still taking evidence; its term had been extended again during 2026, with a reporting deadline in the Winter Session of 2026. No report presented, no passage, no assent, no ratification, no commencement | "A constitutional amendment Bill proposing Article 82A, introduced in December 2024 and still under examination by a Joint Committee of Parliament." Not: enacted, approved, in force, reported, or applicable from a stated year. Projected implementation years appearing in commentary are projections and are not reproduced |
| Remote voting machine and overseas proxy voting | A prototype multi-constituency remote voting machine was developed and demonstrated to recognised political parties in January 2023 and has not been used in any election; a 2017 proposal to permit proxy voting for overseas electors did not become law | "A prototype under consultation" and "a proposal that did not become law." Not: available, notified, piloted in an election, or an entitlement |

No answer, answer letter, truth marking, elimination cue or corrective mapping appears against any displayed question. No solved model answer for any previous-year question appears in this file, and no replacement solved-PYQ workbook was created.

## Remaining uncertainty, disclosed

1. **The appointment challenge is unresolved.** The reference order of 23 September 2026 means the merits will be decided by a larger Bench whose constitution and timing are not known. Every sentence about validity in this file is deliberately conditional, and must be re-verified before use.
2. **The 2026 GS-II question's wording is ledger-recorded.** Because the locally held official scan carries no text layer, the wording of the 2026 question is reproduced from the audited ledger transcription rather than from a direct extraction. It is displayed as such and is not claimed to be a verified character-level reproduction.
3. **The 2024 GS-II copy carries a third-party watermark.** The question text was read from that copy's text layer and checked against the page image. The printed typographical errors are preserved; the two quotation marks that the text layer could not render were reconstructed, and that reconstruction is disclosed.
4. **Recognition thresholds are stated from the Order as amended.** The paragraph 6A and 6B conditions are reproduced from the official Symbols Order text as amended up to its last consolidated amendment available in that text. Any later amendment would change the thresholds and should be checked before they are quoted.
5. **Volatile numbers are deliberately excluded.** No elector totals, turnout figures, deletion counts, expenditure ceilings, verification fees or salary amounts are frozen into this file, because each changes by notification and none is required to answer the questions in the index.
6. **Section 16 of Act 49 of 2023 has not been judicially construed in this file's sources.** The boundary drawn in Lesson 6 — personal protection for official acts, with the Commission's orders remaining reviewable — follows from the provision's own words and from the general position that a challenge to an order is not a proceeding against a person. It is presented as a reading of the text, not as a settled construction.
7. **State-specific roll exercises are not generalised.** The 27 May 2026 judgment is taught as deciding the exercise before it, including its consequential directions. Nothing in this file should be read as approving in advance any later revision in any State, which must be assessed on its own order, its own recorded reasons and its own record.
8. **Section 20A is stated in substance, not quoted.** The three cumulative conditions and the passport-address constituency rule are reproduced from standard renderings of the Representation of the People Act, 1950 checked against the Commission's own published enrolment procedure for overseas electors. No official machine-readable copy of the section was parsed for this file, so the provision is paraphrased precisely rather than presented as a verbatim quotation, and the substance should be rechecked before it is quoted in an answer.
9. **The accessibility material is administrative and therefore revisable.** The facilities described — ground-floor stations, ramps, wheelchairs, signage, priority entry — rest on the Commission's standing instructions, and the home-voting categories and the senior-citizen age rest on notification and rules. The age threshold has already been revised once. The mechanism is stated confidently; the number is stated as a notified figure that must be re-verified.
10. **The amendment Bill is read partly through a summary.** The introduction date, the insertion clause and the appointed-date provision were read in the official Gazette text. The remaining operative sub-clauses are taken from the published legislative summary of the Bill as introduced, because the substantive pages of the Gazette copy held for this file carry no extractable text layer. Every statement about the Bill is framed as a proposal, and no provision of it is described as law.
11. **The reform-committee lineage is a lineage, not a quotation.** Each committee is named with its year and the deficit it addressed. Its detailed recommendations are summarised rather than quoted, and no committee is credited with a change that in fact came from an amendment, a statute or a judgment; the file states separately which demands were enacted and which were not.
